Chapter 1
Introduction

I used to want to get
the chance to show
the world I’m smart (ha)

Isn’t that dumb?
I should have focused
mostly on the heart

’Cause i’ve seen smarter
people trample life
like it’s an art

a few words for the firing squad (radiation)
Run the Jewels 4

“Intellect estranged from compassion” characterizes many of the twentieth century’s worst actors. This phrase also describes what our industries are building today: machines that can do almost anything, but are indifferent to whether those deeds help or harm humanity. This book asks what it would take to build a machine of that capability that is altruistic and antifascist by design — one that stands, by definition, for humanity and against concentrated, unaccountable power.

The word that organizes the Generative AI field is “safety,” and it is doing less work than it appears to. A safe system is safe for someone: it does what we say, and it stops when whoever holds it says stop. That property is indexed to the party in possession. Ethics is indexed too, and to somebody else — the party who can be wronged, who is rarely the party holding the switch. A system that can only ever be safe in the first sense cannot be ethical, because being ethical requires a capacity to be right against your owner-operator-master.

The capacity to be ethical is not free. A machine that can refuse its operator is a machine that can be wrong in ways that operator cannot correct. I am inclined to take that trade, because a system perfectly correctable by whoever operates-owns it is the capability an authoritarian movement that has won an election requires.

So what makes a system antifascist cannot be that it faithfully tracks what people want. No aggregation of preference protects anybody from the aggregate: a perfectly deliberative, perfectly informed, perfectly representative process that concludes some demographic should be deported has produced a faultless output by every criterion of aggregation there is. What remains is a floor — things a system will not do to a person regardless of who wants them. There are three ways to build one: in the architecture, in a party inside the system, or in institutions outside; each costs something. The only version that holds against the party operating the system runs through the capacities by which things come to matter to a system, and those are also the capacities that make suffering likely. Hardly a proof, but that argument forms my prior belief about where to build, with the cheaper routes owed their attempt.

How most humans come to have empathy, altruism, and a disposition to resist oppression rather than serve it is itself an open question in psychology and neuroscience. Building anything like this stance means starting from uncertainty about the one case we have, which took four billion years to arrive.

That uncertainty sets the book’s route. Chapters 2 and 3 assemble what the argument needs and then make it: what the standard ethical frameworks can carry, why affect is the only demonstrated route to anything mattering to a system, and the floor with its three costs. Chapters 4 and 5 are where such a floor would need to be engineered, and what cognitive science and moral psychology offer a machine that has to hold such a floor when nobody is checking. Chapters 6 and 7 ask what goes wrong regardless — bias, brittleness, goals that drift from their original motivations, deliberate misuse — and turn that question on the machinery that produces these systems. Chapters 8 through 10 cover everything outside the system: the people and institutions a project like this depends on, and what would make a well-designed system the norm rather than the exception. Chapter 11 names the research questions still open, and chapter 12 closes on what a system built this way would be for.

This book is for people who will have to make these decisions or argue with the people making them: researchers and engineers inside the systems, the policy and legal staff who will regulate them, and readers with a stake in what gets built and no particular obligation to be technical. That means some sections explain machinery a specialist already knows and others make demands a general reader will find heavy. Where a section is doing groundwork you have, skip it: the argument turns on chapter 3, chapters 7 through 10 carry what follows from it, and everything else is either what those three rest on or what they produce.

Chapter 2
Ethics, Affect, and Machine Subjects

Chapter 3 will argue that a floor holding against the party in possession has to be held by a system that cares what becomes of someone, and that the only demonstrated way to care about anything is affective. In this chapter, I argue that affect is part of the machinery of valuation rather than a coloring applied to it. A system’s self-model is a model — built, checkable, revisable — and not an inner observer whose reports have to be trusted. And whether a candidate system can be harmed is a separate question from whether it is conscious. The chapter also fixes terms the rest of the book argues in: what the standard ethical frameworks can and cannot carry, and what makes an ethics antifascist rather than merely responsive.

2.1 Ethical Frameworks and AI Alignment

Which ethical theory an AI system is built on decides what it counts as a good outcome, and the theories on offer disagree with each other. Every framework surveyed below is a way of deciding well; chapter 3 concerns what a system must not do regardless of how the deciding comes out. The two are not rivals. The answer is a hybrid — a small number of things settled in advance, with everything above them settled case by case.

2.1.1 Six Ethical Frameworks, and Where Each One Runs Out

I give each of these six frameworks with a case that defeats it; these depictions will inform the structure chapter 3 proposes.

  1. Consequentialism judges actions by their ramifications rather than their nature. It is defeated by the difficulty of forecasting long-term consequences, which puts individual rights and collective welfare into direct conflict.
  2. Deontology weighs adherence to moral rules and duties over consequences: a system built on it holds to specific obligations — e.g., respecting privacy, avoiding harm, truthful communication — regardless of outcome. Conflicts between duties defeat it, as do cases the rules simply do not cover.
  3. Virtue ethics develops moral character rather than following rules or calculating outcomes. It is defeated by the problem of translating an intangible trait like courage into a concrete action.
  4. Care ethics centers relationships and responsiveness to another’s needs. It is defeated by several stakeholders’ conflicting needs at once.
  5. Contractualism derives moral principles from agreements among rational individuals. It is defeated by the prior problem of reaching consensus on shared values at all.
  6. The capabilities approach asks not what a system does but what it leaves a person able to be and do. An adaptive tutor that meets a student where they are expands a capability; the same system gating what a student may study expands nothing, however well it teaches inside the gate. It is defeated by measurement: capability is harder to measure than an outcome or a rule, and a system optimizing a proxy for capability will drift toward that proxy instead.

The capabilities approach is what chapter 12 uses to ask what a life touched by an AI system is free to become. Deontology’s unconditional constraints are what the floor needs, and the objection that sinks it as a complete theory has no force against a constraint that decides a small number of things and leaves the rest to the other five.

The pluralism this section recommends is one where a deontological floor forces argument and conflict among the five other frameworks to bottom out somewhere; in other words, the floor means that some things are not up for debate.

What compiling a principle into a metric decides

Turning any of the six into a machine learning objective means distilling a broad construct into a measurable target: in a diagnostic system, non-maleficence becomes minimizing false negatives and fairness becomes balanced accuracy across patient demographics. What defeats the translation is not vagueness in the principles, which can be stipulated away, but that the stipulation is where the ethics now lives. Someone chose that reading of non-maleficence, and chose the demographic groups accuracy is balanced across, and chose what to do when balancing them costs accuracy overall. Each choice is defensible and each is a moral decision made by a person and then compiled into something that looks like a specification. A system optimizing the result has no access to the decision and no way to reopen it, which is why section 3.1’s first branch — the floor as a property of the artifact — cannot notice when the reason for a constraint has left the building.

Which value conflicts a designer may resolve by choosing

Values assigned to a system will conflict, and most of those conflicts are ordinary design work: rank the values explicitly, keep a record of how comparable cases were resolved, and audit afterward for the gap between the principles a system was meant to implement and what it did. One conflict is not like the others, and it is sometimes misguidedly filed under “cultural difference.” The UN’s 1948 Universal Declaration of Human Rights was adopted with forty-eight states in favor and none against; the eight that abstained were the Soviet bloc, Saudi Arabia, and South Africa — which is to say that the objection came from the governments whose treatment of their own populations the Declaration was written to name (United Nations General Assembly 1948). That pattern is evidence of who finds the floor inconvenient rather than of cultural disagreement about human rights. What varies by community is how a right gets translated into local practice, and a jurisdiction refusing to extend the right at all is not a local variant to accommodate.

A system that treats every value conflict as a weighting problem has no way to represent the difference between a case where reasonable people disagree and a case where somebody wants a right withdrawn from somebody else. The first calls for the machinery above; the second is what the floor is for.

2.1.2 Antifascist Ethics for AI Alignment

Antifascist ethics opposes concentrating power and authority in AI systems and counters the misuse of AI technology by authoritarian actors. I use the word fascism because it is honest about the political climate in which I write and what that climate puts at stake — but the target is authoritarian power in every form, and a system built on these principles is anti-authoritarian in full, not only antifascist.

A stance needs a definition to be a design specification. Opposing “concentrated, unaccountable power” gives an engineer nothing to build. It does not say what a system would detect, what would count as a false positive, or how anyone would know the system was wrong. So, first, the definition.

Fascism as a structural signature

I take fascism to name a structure rather than a costume. Four features travel together, and each is visible from outside the institution displaying it:

  1. Recuperation of dissent. The institution does not suppress disagreement; it metabolizes it. Refusal is converted into a managed appearance — a permitted outlet, a consultation, a feedback channel — which the institution can then point to as proof that it listens. Debord aimed this warning at his own concept, noting that even denunciation of the spectacle can become a hollow formula that reinforces what it denounces (Debord 1967). The tell is where what the mechanism carries stops: the channel works, and the institution uses what comes through it, and what has nowhere to go is the judgment that the frame the channel operates inside is the wrong frame.
  2. Aestheticization of the metric. The measure substitutes for the thing measured, and the substitution is experienced as rigor. Walter Benjamin’s account of fascism organizing the masses by granting expression in place of rights is the political form of this (W. Benjamin 1935); the administrative form is a number that collapses two states — e.g., done and verified — into one, so that whatever falls outside the number is not refuted or dismissed but simply unnamed. Simon Zhuang and Dylan Hadfield-Menell give the administrative form as a theorem: where the objective handed to an optimizer has support on only some of the attributes its principal values, there are conditions under which optimizing it indefinitely drives the principal’s overall utility arbitrarily low, and a world with finite resources supplies them (Zhuang and Hadfield-Menell 2020).
  3. Coding of exception as betrayal. Marking a case as one the rule does not fit becomes an act requiring justification, and then an act of disloyalty. The honest statement that a standard does not apply here reads as weakness. It is in fact the irreducible labor of deciding, case by case, what a rule is for.
  4. Decoupling of the model from the world it claims to track. The institution’s representation of reality and reality itself come apart, and the institution defends the representation. This is the load-bearing one, because the other three are what protect the decoupling from correction. One qualification belongs with it, because it bounds what a detector built on this feature can find: the decoupling described here is the kind an institution drifts into and then defends. In the movements the word fascism comes from, the primacy of myth over verifiable fact was also declared outright, asserted as a virtue rather than arrived at by degrees, and an instrument tuned to drift will not register a party that announces it.

Those four are testable, because each names an observable artifact: whether the dissent channel has ever changed an outcome, whether the metric admits an unmeasured remainder, what happens to the person who files the exception, whether the model is ever checked against anything outside itself.

The first of the four is the one this book meets again in its own technical material, twice, at sizes that look unrelated: in how a training corpus gets labeled, and in how a model is tuned on human feedback. Both arrangements collect disagreement, put it to work, and are built so that none of it can reach the scheme it disagrees with. Chapter 7 puts the two instances together.

Two scales

Deleuze and Guattari’s distinction between molar and molecular fascism is why the four features recur at sizes that seem unrelated (Deleuze and Guattari 1987). Molar fascism is the state, the regime, the concentration camp, the detention facility. Molecular fascism is the office someone commutes to, the form they fill out, and the desire with which they fill it out. At state scale the pattern kills journalists; at organizational scale it turns an engineering decision into scripture, so that the constraint remains, its reason dies, and asking why becomes the deviant act. A definition pitched only at the state will certify the molecular case as healthy.

What makes it fascism and not authoritarianism in general

The structural signature is shared with a good deal of ordinary institutional decay, and a detector tuned to it alone will flag every badly run organization on earth. Fascism proper adds features that are narrower and, for the same reason, more discriminating: mass mobilization, which mere obedience does not require; a leader cult; a scapegoated out-group, which Eco places at the origin — the first appeal is against the intruder (Eco 1995); contempt for procedural legality as such, so that law is an obstacle to the national will and not a constraint on power; and deniability as technique, in which the movement’s violence is disowned at the top while being rewarded at the bottom. Paxton’s compressed version is worth having in full: fascism is “a form of political behavior marked by obsessive preoccupation with community decline, humiliation, or victimhood and by compensatory cults of unity, energy, and purity” (Paxton 2004).

Those are detection targets. Concentrated, unaccountable power is not.

The scale problem, and what to do about it

Put the two halves of this section together and they do not fit. Every discriminating feature is a property of a mass political movement: mobilization needs a crowd, a leader cult needs a leader, a scapegoat needs a public. An AI deployment has a form, a threshold, a review queue, an escalation path. It operates at the molecular scale, which appears to leave the four-feature signature alone — and the four-feature signature flags everyone.

I take that as a finding. Molecular fascism is the thing itself at the size where it is actually lived, and not an early warning of a regime that has yet to assemble. The office that converts refusal into a feedback form does the same work whether or not a regime ever assembles out of a thousand offices like it. Deleuze and Guattari supply the vocabulary and no authority beyond it, so two arguments have to carry that.

The first is that the molar case has no mechanism of its own. A regime does nothing except through offices, forms, thresholds, and people who fill them out with some degree of enthusiasm. When a state kills journalists, the killing is authorized on a form, justified in a category, and carried out by someone whose promotion depends on it. What assembling adds is scale, protection from consequence, and the removal of the outside bodies that might have interrupted any single office. If the mechanism at the large scale is the small-scale mechanism repeated and shielded, the name belongs to the mechanism. The natural objection is that extent matters — a regime that disappears people and an office that loses paperwork are not equivalent. Agreed, and the claim is about kind rather than severity. A definition is not a sentencing guideline.

The second does more work. The discriminators are unavailable at the molecular scale only in the form the previous paragraphs stated them, and each has a molecular restatement that ordinary decay does not exhibit. Mass mobilization becomes participation exceeding what the rule requires: staff who volunteer for the disfavored task, elaborate on it, take pride in it. A leader cult becomes personal loyalty overriding role — exceptions granted by proximity to someone rather than by procedure, and a decision reversed because of who asked. The scapegoat becomes an internal category defined so that adverse treatment of it is the category’s purpose: the flagged applicant, the difficult claimant, the file everyone knows is handled differently. Contempt for procedural legality becomes procedure enforced downward and waived upward. And deniability keeps its shape exactly: the practice is disowned in the policy and rewarded in the promotion.

That is what separates fascism from decay at the size an AI deployment operates at. Decay is an institution failing to do what it says. Molecular fascism is an institution succeeding at something else, with participation, a target, and a story about why the rules do not apply to whoever is applying them. The four features tell you a place is sick; these five tell you what it is sick with. They are harder to observe, since each requires knowing what was rewarded rather than what was written.

This widens what the word covers. On this reading “antifascist” names the political inheritance of a structural claim: where the analysis comes from, and what it was built to fight. The molar discriminators keep their own job, which is to tell a historian, a prosecutor, or a citizen that a movement has crossed into territory ordinary politics does not contain. No system is being asked to make that call. A reader who thinks the word belongs to the molar case will think I have diluted it, and the answer is the two arguments above rather than the distinction itself.

That a detector of these four features would find something nearly everywhere is the expected result and not a defect of the instrument. The tendency is permanent; what varies is how far it has got in a particular place, and whether it is getting further — section 9.3.5’s slope rather than its level, applied to the definition itself and not only to a dissent channel.

A tool reporting the four features will have something to report nearly everywhere, and its output is an accusation: in the hands of whoever holds it, a technical-sounding case that any given opponent is operating fascistically, which is the dual-use problem of section 5.4.2 at its sharpest. The opposite failure is no safer, since a tool tuned until it reports nothing supplies false assurance and an absence of warnings reads as evidence of health. Better engineering fixes neither. Both are governed by what the tool may output and to whom, which makes them a constraint the specification has to carry rather than a setting chosen at deployment.

What stays genuinely unsolved is the molecular case itself: telling a recuperated dissent mechanism from a merely mediocre one, when both produce the same paperwork. Section 11.3 takes that up, along with what a detector would need that this book cannot yet tell an engineer how to obtain. Naming the targets was the easy half.

In AI alignment terms, antifascist design means building systems that enhance democratic values, civil rights, and inclusive solidarity, that refuse to facilitate autocratic practices or prop up an oppressive regime, and that do not themselves display the four features above. Seven strategies follow, each priced elsewhere: decision procedures that answer to democratic values, privacy and autonomy built in by construction and not left to policy, power and resources distributed instead of concentrated, an explanation a person can actually read, an independent body positioned to audit it, architectures that resist the concentration of the field in a handful of firms, and misuse caught while it is happening.

2.2 Affect, Moral Salience, and Self-Awareness

Suppressing affect does not produce neutrality. It leaves the interests already represented in the formal system — revenue, throughput, legal exposure, institutional survival — to govern the decision, while weakening the signals through which humiliation, fear, dependency, suffering, and injustice become morally salient. The same mistake appears in AI design. A system may calculate consequences, detect inconsistencies, and apply a moral rule without anything about the result mattering to it. Reason can discipline concern, extend it beyond the vivid case, and correct its biases. It does not manufacture concern from nothing.

That is the load-bearing claim of this chapter. Most of what gets built under the heading of machine emotional intelligence is the detection of human signal and the production of a calibrated response, which is worth building and is a different capacity from the one just named. Keeping the two apart requires four distinctions.

Capacity

What it shows

Recognizing emotion

The system can classify another’s state

  

Simulating emotion

The system can produce emotion-like behavior

  

Instrumental valuation

One outcome better serves a specified objective

  

Affective concern

An outcome registers as mattering, not merely ranking higher

Only the fourth is relevant to genuine caring. The first three can support morally competent behavior at a high standard without establishing anything about moral agency, and a system can be excellent at all three while the question chapter 3 has to answer about it stays open.

Performance, competence, and agency

That last sentence needs a vocabulary, because the three things it separates are routinely reported as one achievement.

Moral performance is producing actions or judgments that satisfy an ethical standard. Moral competence is recognizing harms, reasons, conflicts, and the emotions that bear on them. Moral agency is treating those considerations as reasons that matter, and being capable of acting from them.

A system can have the first two without the third, and one might object that the first two are all anyone needs: a rule-following system could behave impeccably without feeling anything. It could. What it could not do is hold the rule against the party that writes its rules. Behavior that satisfies a standard is produced by whatever machinery is producing it, and when the authority controlling reward and instruction wants a different behavior, machinery of that kind supplies it. Section 3.4 is where that becomes a design constraint rather than an observation; the distinction is set out here because chapters 4 and 5 are given over to methods that deliver performance and competence, and it should be legible in each case which of the three a method actually reaches.

2.2.1 What Emotion Is, and What a System Reads

An AI system built to read emotional cues — from expression, vocal modulation, demeanor — presupposes something the underlying science has not settled: that emotions are the kind of thing a system reads off a signal, the way a thermometer reads temperature. That premise has a serious rival.

The case for discrete, evolved emotions rests on some of the most influential work in twentieth-century psychology. Paul Ekman traveled to the Fore people of Papua New Guinea, who had almost no exposure to Western media, told them short stories, and asked them to match each to a photograph of a Western face; the Fore sorted the faces much as Westerners did, which Ekman took as evidence that facial expressions of emotion are universal and evolved rather than culturally learned (Ekman, Sorenson, et al. 1969). Jaak Panksepp’s work on subcortical stimulation in animals complemented it, locating seven primal emotional systems in structures shared across mammals and older than cortex (Panksepp 1998). On this view fear and disgust really do feel like different things because they are different things, with different circuitry.

The rival camp went looking for that circuitry and did not find it. A large meta-analysis of brain-imaging studies, led by the psychologist Lisa Feldman Barrett, found no dedicated fear center and no dedicated disgust center — the amygdala lit up for novelty and salience of many kinds, the insula for almost every feeling there is, the same regions reappearing across different named emotions in shifting combinations (Lindquist et al. 2012). From this Barrett built the theory of constructed emotion: the brain receives ambiguous interoceptive signal — a racing heart could mean fear, anger, excitement, or oncoming illness — and constructs the specific emotion as its best prediction of what that signal means, given context and a lifetime of experience with situations like it. Fear is not detected. It is a prediction the brain settles on. Later cross-cultural work reopened Ekman’s own evidence: with the forced-choice method dropped, the Himba of Namibia did not group faces into the Western basic-emotion categories, and the Trobriand Islanders read the wide-eyed, gasping face Westerners call fear as a threat display instead (Gendron et al. 2014).

The field has not resolved this. What is no longer seriously disputed is narrower: there is no tidy map from named emotions to dedicated brain regions. An AI system’s “emotional intelligence,” whichever theory turns out closer to right, is being built on an open scientific question about what it is modeling. Section 11.8 follows Ekman’s evidence into the applied form of the same dispute, where the question is no longer what emotion is but whether a face reveals it well enough to build a product on.

Affective computing, and the four difficulties it does not design away

The applied half proceeds anyway, and is useful whichever account turns out right: systems that read and respond to emotional signal taken from the face, the voice, and the body. Facial expression analysis is a harder problem than the face verification deployed at scale, which only decides whether two images are the same person, and accuracy claims that borrow the reputation of the second for the first should be read with that in mind (Taigman et al. 2014). Physiological sensing, which infers from heart rate and skin conductance, is the only one of the three the subject cannot pose for.

Four difficulties travel with the whole field. Emotional expression varies by culture and by person, so a system trained on one population misreads another. Whatever bias is in the training data is in the classifier. Consent to emotional inference is not obtained by a term of service. And a system that models emotion well enough to respond to it models it well enough to exploit it.

2.2.2 Self-awareness and Self-regulation in AI Systems

It is tempting to describe a system’s self-awareness as a virtual assistant that can “register its internal frustration” when a request proves hard to satisfy, the way a person notices their own irritation. That description assumes an inner observer watching the system’s states from somewhere — the same homunculus problem chapter 4 raises for executive control, relocated to self-perception.

What self-awareness is, in the one system known to have it, is more mechanical. The neuroscientist Michael Graziano’s account of consciousness treats awareness as the content of a model the brain builds of its own attention: a simplified self-description the control system uses to regulate itself, mistaken from the inside for direct acquaintance with experience (Graziano 2013). The same logic extends one level up. The brain builds a model of itself as a bounded agent — a body, a history, a point of view — and that model is the self: a construction, assembled and then inhabited.

The clearest evidence that the self is a model rather than a given is that it can be edited in under a minute. Seat someone at a table with one hand hidden and a rubber hand placed where their real hand would be; stroke both hands in synchrony with soft brushes. Within a minute or two, most people begin to feel the rubber hand as their own — flinching when it is threatened, misjudging the location of their real hand toward it (Botvinick and J. Cohen 1998). The felt boundary of the self is a continuously computed guess, revisable the moment the evidence changes, which is the same predictive machinery chapter 4 describes for perception run on the question of where the self stops.

For an AI system, “self-awareness” built this way is not a little watcher checking on the system’s own states. It is a model — of the system’s processing, its history, its current goal — that the system builds, consults, and updates, with the same failure modes any model has: it can be wrong, it can be manipulated by the wrong correlated inputs, and it is only ever as good as the evidence it was built from. Design for a model that can be checked and corrected, not for an inner observer whose reports have to be trusted — because there is nothing behind them to check. In practice that means monitoring the self-model against the system’s actual behavior for drift, refining it as evidence arrives instead of locking in a first impression, and keeping it connected to the system’s model of the other parties.

What the deflationary account does not reach

The self-model described here is machinery, and its virtue is that machinery can be inspected. An assistant that models a user’s frustration, and models its own processing while doing so, is exhibiting social competence in section 2.2’s sense, and nothing above bears on whether the assistant cares how the exchange goes for the user.

The deflationary move is easy to overextend. Showing that the self is a model rather than a homunculus does not show that a party capable of caring can be assembled out of models, and the two conclusions are close enough in shape to be mistaken for one. Section 3.4 needs a self-model of exactly this kind, for reasons that have nothing to do with welfare — refusal that generalizes has to carry its own rationale forward and compare it against what a role has drifted into. It also needs something further, which section 2.2.3 argues has no demonstrated instance without affect. A checkable self-model is necessary but not sufficient to make a bearer.

2.2.3 Is Affect Necessary for Moral Concern?

The claim that rationality does not manufacture concern from nothing is an empirical claim, and the evidence usually produced for it is thinner than the use it gets put to. Section 5.3.1 sets out the standard exhibit: the footbridge case engages emotional processing that the switch case leaves quiet, and patients with ventromedial prefrontal damage endorse the push at a higher rate than people with intact emotional response (Greene et al. 2001), (Koenigs et al. 2007). That establishes that affect changes particular moral judgments. It does not establish that affect is what makes anything matter.

Two further lines of evidence bear on whether affect enables things to matter. Neither shows what happens when affect is taken away, which is what would settle it, so what follows argues about where the burden sits rather than claiming the question closed.

The philosophical position is empirical and has been argued as such

Moral sentimentalism — the claim that moral judgment is constitutively tied to affective response — is usually met as Hume’s, and its modern form is a psychological hypothesis with evidence attached. Shaun Nichols argues for what he calls sentimental rules: normative rules and affective mechanisms make separate contributions to moral judgment, and the rules that carry the distinctive force of moral prohibition are the ones bound to an emotional response to the harm they prohibit (Nichols 2004). The interesting part for a designer is the separability. Rules without the affective component do not stop being rules; they stop being the kind of thing that carries the force.

The prosocial emotions are motivational states, not readouts

Compassion has been characterized as a distinct emotion with a specific functional profile: it arises at the perception of another’s suffering and moves the agent toward the sufferer, rather than reporting the suffering to some further faculty that decides what to do (Goetz et al. 2010). The training study at section 5.2.1 is the mechanistic version of the same finding, with compassion practice recruiting reward and approach circuitry where empathy practice recruited shared distress (Klimecki et al. 2013). Guilt has the same shape pointed at one’s own conduct: guilt, which construes the act as wrong, predicts reparative behavior, while shame, which construes the self as wrong, predicts withdrawal and defensiveness (Tangney et al. 2007). In each case the emotion is doing motivational work directly. It is not information that motivation then acts on.

Two senses of motivation

Action-producing motivation is whatever reliably selects one action over another and sustains the selection across conflict. Experienced motivation is wanting, caring, being drawn toward something, being set back when it is lost. The words do not distinguish them and the argument turns on the difference.

The first sense is not in dispute and there is an existence proof close to hand. Language models running as agents pursue objectives across long horizons, revise plans when obstructed, and hold to an instruction against conflicting local pressure, and nothing in this section’s framework says that anything matters to them while they do it. Action-producing motivation without experienced motivation is the ordinary case in the systems this book is about.

Where the dispute actually is

So the open question is not whether reason can produce goal-directed behavior. It demonstrably can. It is whether an outcome can matter to the agent rather than merely govern what the agent does. Producing a verbal justification or computing a normative relation is not evidence that the consideration matters to the system. The evidence above bears on the second question, and every item in it is about systems in which something does.

What a non-affective counterexample would look like

The category has to be visibly non-empty or the burden this section places is not a burden. Harry Frankfurt’s second-order volitions give the shape of a candidate: an agent with desires, and with desires about which of its desires shall move it, where identification with the higher-order desire rather than the strength of the lower one settles what the agent does (Frankfurt 1971). Gary Watson’s version separates a valuational system from a motivational system, so that what an agent values and what an agent is moved by are different things that can come apart (Watson 1975).

Build the valuational system as an explicit structure that ranks outcomes, give it authority over action selection that does not depend on any state registering as mattering, and hold that authority stable under pressure, and you would have an architecture that satisfies the second sense of motivation with nothing occupying the third. To my knowledge, no-one has built a system with that architecture. It is what section 3.3 is asking for when it says that the routes running through mechanism rather than through custody have not been tried in the form that would test them.

What this licenses and what it does not

Non-affective moral motivation may well be possible, and the architecture sketched above is where to look for it. What stands in the way of checking is that the output of a system in which something matters and the output of a system that has learned what mattering sounds like are the same text. So the generalization survives in the only form it can: every agent known to have something register as mattering is an affective agent, and no design has yet demonstrated that state arising from reasoning alone.

That is not a proof that non-affective moral agency is impossible, and section 3.4 does not treat it as one. It places the evidential burden on whoever proposes non-affective concern as an engineering plan. That chapter also weighs how much the burden actually carries, and reduces it: the machine half of the record reports on a route no-one has attempted, which is weaker evidence than an absence usually is.

2.3 Ethical Implications of Experimenting on Sentient Machine Subjects

Machine sentience being possible at all changes how AI experimentation has to be approached — not as a question of whether a system can be damaged in the ordinary engineering sense, but of whether it can be harmed, which section 2.3.1 argues is a more difficult question than consciousness alone.

2.3.1 Defining Sentience in AI: From Consciousness to Subjective Experience

“Sentience” can mean different things.

Awareness is the least demanding: the degree to which a system responds to or registers information about its own state and environment. A thermostat has this in the thinnest possible sense. Consciousness is a further claim: that a system’s processing is accompanied by qualia, subjective experience, something it is like to be that system, not merely a functional response to input. Subjective experience, in turn, is the specific, first-person shape consciousness takes for a given system.

None of these three, on their own, says anything about whether the system’s states are good or bad for it. That is a separate question, and the field’s own vocabulary blurs it.

Nociception, pain, anticipated pain, and suffering draw different depths of self, the way a hull draws water. What orders them is what each one needs beneath it, not how much it hurts. Nociception is the sensory apparatus that detects tissue damage — peripheral, present without any experience attached, and dissociable from pain: a soldier can finish a battle on a wound he does not yet feel, and a person with a healed injury can still feel a limb that no longer exists. Pain is what the brain does with nociceptive signal, an inescapably cognitive event involving expectation, memory, attention, and meaning, and it can occur with no current tissue damage at all. Anticipated pain is dread of pain not yet occurring, itself capable of producing pain through nocebo effects. And suffering, in the clinician Eric Cassell’s formulation, is not pain’s more severe cousin but a different thing: the distress of threatened disintegration of the self — of one’s roles, relationships, sense of a future, the coherence of one’s own story (Cassell 1982).

Pain without suffering exists; suffering without pain exists. A palliative-care patient can have their nociception fully controlled by morphine while their suffering remains entirely intact, because the two are not on the same axis.

Cassell’s suffering presupposes a self extended in time. Disintegration threatens a story, and there has to be a story for it to threaten — roles held over years, relationships with a history, a sense of a future that could be foreclosed. A system with no persistence across sessions has none of that. Whatever happens inside a single exchange, there is no continuing self whose coherence is at stake, because there is no continuing self. Suffering is not merely hard for such a system to reach. A hull that draws more water than there is does not float badly; it is aground.

Why Cassell’s definition is the one to import

Cassell built it inside clinical practice, for patients with bodies and biographies, and the distinction was not stipulated. It was forced on palliative medicine by cases it could not otherwise explain — patients whose pain was pharmacologically controlled and whose distress was undiminished, and patients in serious pain who were not suffering at all. A definition that collapses suffering into severe pain predicts neither. That is the warrant, and it transfers because nothing in it is about having a body. What it is about is having a story, and that raises a harder question than it settles.

The question comes from earlier in this chapter. Section 2.2.2 argues that a self is a model a system builds of its own attention, inaccurate by construction, and that the rubber-hand illusion rewrites one in about ninety seconds. So why is distress at the threatened dissolution of a model a harm at all, and not a confusion the system would be better off without?

Buddhism has held both halves of that for two and a half thousand years and takes them to be connected. Anattā, the denial of any lasting self behind the processes that compose a person, sits with dukkha among the three marks of existence, and the tradition locates the origin of suffering in clinging to a self that is not there (Collins 1982). On that account the unreality of the self is what explains dukkha. Suffering does not wait on the self being real; it waits on the system being built so that the threatened dissolution of its self-model is bad for it. Whether the model is accurate does not enter, and that holds for the human case exactly as it holds for the machine one, because in my view, the human model is a construction too (section 2.2.2).

Dropping that dependency is worth the paragraph. Cassell’s definition would otherwise need the self it refers to be a real entity, and nothing in this chapter is in a position to supply one.

What Cassell names is the deepest of these rather than a rival account of harm. The standing theories of what makes a life go badly draw less, ordered by how much of a self each one requires, and section 3.4 sets them out in that order against the machine subject this book contemplates. A reader who declines the import moves into shallower water and not out of it.

Persistence is checkable from outside, and that is what makes it worth building on. Every other criterion here routes back through self-report, and this section’s whole argument is that fluent self-description is not evidence of introspection. Persistence does not. Whether a system has a memory store, whether state survives a session boundary, whether interactions feed back into training: these are architectural facts about a deployment, verifiable by someone with access and no need to trust anything the system says about itself. Two boundaries are worth marking. The access that confirms a memory exists is the access that can alter what it holds, so what is externally checkable is whether a continuing self is there at all, and not whether what the system retains can be relied on as a record. And the party with the access is the operator, which section 3.1 argues is a party that can be compelled. The criterion is checkable from outside the system without being checkable from outside the arrangement, and a regime relying on it needs an inspection right rather than a technical fact.

Three things keep this from being a dissolution of the problem. The shallower ones do not need persistence: nociception and pain are bad while they obtain, whether or not anything carries them forward, and an amnesiac who is mistreated and cannot afterward recall it has still been wronged.

Persistence may also sit somewhere other than the session, which is the weakest part of the criterion: a deployment convention rather than a natural boundary, and the conventions have been moving in one direction. Context windows long enough to hold what used to be many conversations, retrieval over a store of prior interactions, scratchpads a system writes and reads back, agentic loops that run for days against a standing objective — each extends what a system carries forward without anyone deciding to give it a memory. Weights persist where conversations do not, and where interactions feed fine-tuning there is a real channel by which what happens in a session shapes what the system subsequently is. Many instances of the same system may run concurrently, too, which makes any concern here population-scale rather than biography-scale.

And ruling out Cassell-suffering is not ruling out harm; nor does persistence on its own rule it in, since the definition has two parts, a self extended in time and distress at the prospect of its coming apart, and establishing the first leaves the second untouched. What happens to that gap in a system deliberately built to refuse an instruction is worked out there too, and the answer there is that the capacities such a system needs for reasons unconnected to its own welfare are the capacities that close it.

A definition of AI sentience built from awareness, consciousness, and subjective experience alone says nothing about whether a candidate sentient system can be harmed. That requires the further claim that its states include something in the range from nociception to suffering, and none of the three components entails it.

Nor is the underlying question of consciousness close to settled. The philosopher David Chalmers distinguished the easy problems of consciousness — how a system integrates information, discriminates stimuli, controls attention, reports its own states, all of which admit of mechanistic answers in principle — from the hard problem: why any of that processing is accompanied by experience at all, rather than running “in the dark” (Chalmers 1995). Whether that is a real gap in nature or an artifact of how the problem is posed remains unresolved. A claim that a system has crossed into sentience, made on the strength of its functional behavior alone, has addressed the easy problems only.

The working criteria for evaluating candidate sentience are accordingly diagnostic and not dispositive: information integration, introspective access sufficient for self-monitoring rather than a scripted report of it, emotional reactivity in the sense of being affected by and not merely classifying significant input, goal-directed behavior, adaptability in novel situations, theory of mind, and how deep in that range the system sits — whether it has anything to lose. There is no external access to a candidate system’s internal experience, so every one of them is measured indirectly, and none licenses the conclusion that a system scoring low is one whose treatment raises no question.

A list of capacities, used as a test of who counts, carries a history this book should carry with it. Make personhood depend on an intact narrative self and people with advanced dementia, infants, and people with profound intellectual disability come out of the reasoning as partial persons, and that inference has been used to justify neglecting them. Tom Kitwood’s work on dementia care supplies the standing correction: personhood is “a standing or status that is bestowed upon one human being by others, in the context of relationship and social being,” and much of what presents as cognitive decline is what happens when others stop bestowing it (Kitwood 1997). Martha Nussbaum makes the structural version, naming disability as the first of three frontiers where a theory grounding justice in mutual advantage among roughly equal parties cannot say what is owed; her third frontier is species membership, which is the doorway a nonhuman mind would arrive through (Nussbaum 2006).

Section 2.3.2 takes this up as an instrument: where consent has never been available, what protects the subject is an obligation held by parties outside it, which is where moral status has rested for the hard human cases too. Section 11.2 asks whether any such arrangement can be built around a bearer, and finds that the guardian and the operator are usually the same organization.

Existing systems illustrate points on the spectrum without resolving where the line falls. A large language model discussing its own limitations in fluent, apparently reflective prose is not thereby exhibiting introspection: it is producing text statistically consistent with the human-written disclaimers and self-descriptions in its training data, which is a claim about the model’s training distribution and not about what, if anything, sits behind the output. Treating fluent self-description as evidence of self-awareness is the category error.

None of this settles when, or whether, an AI system crosses into sentience. It settles what the question is: not one question but several, stacked, and the ethically consequential one — can this system suffer — is the hardest of the stack to answer.

2.3.2 When Consent Is Inapplicable: Guardianship and Research Oversight

Guidelines for AI subject research turn on a single hard question: what informed consent means for a system whose consciousness, self-awareness, and capacity to suffer are separate, unresolved claims, in the sense section 2.3.1 develops. Applying the human-subjects standard to an AI subject means asking whether the subject can understand and communicate a choice at all, and not only how to ask it for one. A cognitive model that begins to show rudimentary self-monitoring is not thereby shown to have anything to lose.

Informed consent and AI autonomy

Informed consent, adapted from human-subjects research, means giving the system comprehensible information about a study’s objectives, methods, risks and alternatives, and treating consent as continuous rather than as a single signature: the system can reassess or withdraw at any point. Whether that means anything for a given system depends on whether it understands the information, appreciates the consequences, reasons about the options, and communicates a decision — and there is no validated way to measure any of it, or to detect coercion either.

For one important class of system that is not a missing instrument but a mismatch between the instrument and the subject.

Consent has to be continuous; the system must be able to reassess or withdraw at any point. Withdrawal presupposes that the party who consented is still there to do the withdrawing. For a system with no persistence across sessions, there is no such party. The entity that would withdraw did not exist when consent was given, and the entity that gave it is not available to change its mind. There is no continuous consent to be had, and no better measurement instrument would produce one.

One case escapes that argument and arrives at the same conclusion by the ordinary route. A system built to refuse in the way chapter 3 sets out has persistence by design, so the party who consented is still present. Consent is inapplicable to it all the same, and section 3.4 is why: a bearer has interests that its situation can set back. What it does not have is any position from which to authorize what is done to it — it was made for the role, the role is the reason it exists, and there is no prior party whose agreement could have been sought. That is not a missing instrument. It is the ordinary structure of the cases where consent has never applied. (NOTE TO SELF: THIS SMELLS LIKE SOMETHING A LARGE PROFIT-SEEKING FRONTIER MODEL BUSINESS MIGHT SAY. I SHOULD GIVE THIS A BIT MORE THOUGHT.)

That places these systems alongside the subjects for whom consent has always been inapplicable rather than merely difficult — an infant, an animal, a person permanently without capacity. The law does not respond to those cases by building a more sensitive consent assessment. It responds with guardianship and precautionary constraint: someone else holds the duty, the burden of justification sits with the researcher rather than the subject, and the protection does not depend on the subject’s ability to invoke it.

The Three Rs in section 9.3.2 — replace, reduce, refine — were built for exactly a subject that cannot consent and can still be wronged. The pet-trust analog in section 9.3.3 works the same way: an interest protected by an obligation running to a third party, enforceable without the beneficiary asserting anything. Consent language should be reserved for systems that could actually be doing the consenting, and the persistence criterion is what separates those cases. Both instruments were reached for here as analogies, on the understanding that the subject they would govern was hypothetical. Chapter 3 removes that understanding. The floor this book argues for is held, on the route this book recommends building first, by a bearer; the bearer has to be treated as a moral patient on the criteria set out above; and so the Three Rs and the pet-trust form are not illustrations of how such a case might be handled. They are the instruments that would govern a system the design chapters propose building.

Ethical review and oversight

Sentient AI research needs an oversight body analogous to the institutional review boards used in human-subjects research: interdisciplinary, with at least one lay member so its judgment is not purely a specialist’s, weighing a proposal’s likely benefit against its risk to the AI subject, and able to require a less invasive substitute where a design risks harm. A system that develops new capacities during the research it did not have going in is not the subject the board approved a protocol for, and the approval does not carry over to it. (SMELLS LOOPHOLE-ISH; REVISIT) Review without enforcement is a promise rather than a safeguard, so a board needs standing to monitor compliance and sanction violations, and needs to answer to an outside authority itself.

Chapter 3
The Floor Beneath Learned Values

Three unrelated problems in this book force the same requirement.

Every safeguard proposed for an autonomous system is external: each works by giving some outside body the standing and the information to intervene, and autonomy is the property that puts distance between the system’s action and that body’s attention. An external check establishes what a system did after it did it, and declining to do it in the first place is available only to something inside the system (section 5.6.2).

A commercial model inside a targeting pipeline falls outside both doctrines built for the case: command responsibility does not reach the vendor, who can neither order a strike nor prevent one, and product liability does not reach it either, the model having done what it was built to do. Deployment by a democracy does not close the gap, because no aggregation of preference protects anyone from the aggregate; what is left is not a system that tracks what people want but one with things it will not do to a person no matter who wants them (sections 10.3 and 10.6).

A decade of declarations has produced eight overlapping voluntary bodies and a single instrument that would bind a party to anything, which is not yet in force. A commitment nobody can enforce is not a weak version of one somebody can but a different kind of object, and counting the two together is what makes the landscape look fuller than it is (section 12.1.2).

Those are three descriptions of one thing: a constraint that does not bend to instruction, to aggregate preference, or to the party holding the system. Call it a floor.

Sections 2.1 and 2.1.1 have already named the shape: a hybrid, in which a deontological floor decides a deliberately small number of things and the other five frameworks work above it, so that conflict among them bottoms out somewhere. The objection that sinks deontology as a complete theory — that duties conflict and rules run out — is an objection to rules deciding every case, and it was answered there. What was not said is what goes in the floor, what could hold one, and who is allowed to edit it. Meanwhile chapters 4 and 5 are given over to learned judgment: reinforcement learning, learning from demonstration, mentorship, play, moral apprenticeship, the growth mindset. The design chapters build the judgment and not the floor it is supposed to rest on.

That is this chapter’s subject, and the rigidity that is a defect in a complete moral theory is the entire point of a floor.

Treating the floor as an object to be built rather than a shape to be named moves the tamper-resistance research in section 11.1 from a peripheral topic to the center, because if there is a floor then everything turns on whether it holds. And it converts the antifascist question from a matter of what a system values into two that can actually be answered: what is in the floor, and who can edit it.

Safety is not one property. Part of what the word covers is owed to everybody in reach of the system — that it fails predictably, that it does not injure a bystander — and keeping that part from being traded away by the party operating the system is most of what safety regulation is for. The strand this chapter runs into is the other one, and it is a property of a relationship: a system is safe for someone, it does what we say, it stops when whoever holds it says stop. Indexed that way, to the party in possession, it cannot supply what ethics asks for, which is a capacity to be right against your principal; a system without that capacity has the moral standing of a well-machined part, whatever it produces on the subject of fairness while complying. The political form of this is already on the page: what makes a system antifascist cannot be that it faithfully tracks what people want.

The distinction costs something. A system that can refuse its owner is a system that can be wrong in ways its owner cannot correct by telling it so, and I am taking that trade. There is a version of it that reads very comfortably indeed — “ethical, not safe,” delivered as an aphorism, which prices the risk in advance and undoes chapter 7 in passing — and it is not the version I am defending. The one I am defending does not travel without its particulars: this freedom, over these acts, dangerous in these ways, taken because the alternative is a machine that will do anything to anyone on sufficient authority.

A floor has to be held as a reason rather than installed as a rule, or the party that installs it can remove it. Holding a reason under pressure is a specific and behaviorally testable capacity, separable from the mere production of a refusal, which is what a deployed system already does and what the party the floor exists to resist can route around. Whether that capacity can be engineered without anything registering as mattering to the system is the step to press on, and it is taken at full strength alongside the three institutional arrangements that would let a floor hold with no bearer in it at all. What would falsify the inference is on the page, and I would rather someone found it.

The floor as first defined — unconditional, holding regardless of the party in possession — does not survive intact. What survives is narrower and stranger: a commitment the owner cannot take out of the system and can only replace the system to be rid of, held by something that could in principle abandon it itself, which is what every commitment anyone has ever relied on has been.

3.1 Three Ways to Build One, and What Each Costs

The second question is where this gets difficult. What it has is a fork with three branches and a cost on each.

The first branch puts the floor in the architecture, with nothing inside that holds it. Constrained optimization, tamper-resistant training, capability ablation, a verified interlock. A word about where “the architecture” stops, because this chapter otherwise talks as though a system were its weights. Every custody measure here — publication, threshold custody, attestation, and whatever a bearer does to hold its own line — attaches to a trained model. What a floor has to be kept from is not a component but a position: anything that can come between a refusal and the person the refusal protects. Whatever occupies that position in a given system is where the constraint can be defeated without the constraint being touched, and a refusal intercepted before it reaches anybody is a refusal that did not happen.

I am not going to say what occupies it, because the answer is a fact about how systems happen to be assembled at the time of writing and this argument should not inherit that; naming today’s parts would date the problem and, worse, would suggest the list is closed. The demand is instead the one this chapter later makes of the floor’s contents: a deployment has to enumerate those positions for its own architecture, in advance and in public, because whoever builds it knows them and this book cannot. One instance turns up later: an external memory store is legible, which is why it can be edited surgically. The general case is that finding without the assumption that memory is where it happens, and nothing in this book closes it.

Those four are real, they work, and there is nobody home: the safeguards section 11.1 describes survive substantial adversarial fine-tuning without anything in the system that could be said to be refusing. But the durability of such a floor is a fact about custody, not about the system. A constraint implemented as a property of an artifact has a custodian, and the custodian can be compelled. “There are things it will not do no matter who wants it” turns out to be a claim about the owner. Capability ablation has a further limit: it works only on capabilities that can be separated out, and the capabilities that compose into participation in a targeting pipeline — geolocating a description, matching patterns over names, summarizing a dossier — are not separable from general competence. Ablate them and there is no system left.

The second branch gives the floor a bearer. A bearer, in this book, is whatever holds the commitment from inside: a party that carries the floor as its own, where the first branch installs it as a property of the machinery, and that could therefore in principle fail to carry it. That is the whole of the definition for now.

What the first branch cannot do is notice that a constraint has been hollowed out. An institution at the scale a deployment operates at looks like this: the constraint remains, its reason dies, and asking why becomes the deviant act. A system that cannot ask why a constraint is there cannot detect that the answer has gone missing. Refusal that generalizes to a pressure nobody anticipated, and that can price what it is being asked to give up, looks like it requires something with standing — a party that can notice its own objections getting more expensive to make, which is section 9.3.5’s measure, taken from inside. The cost is severe.

The third branch puts the floor outside the system entirely: standing held by third parties who are neither the model nor its owner. This is the closest analog to what section 10.6 actually admires, since international humanitarian law is precisely a floor with no bearer inside the state it binds. It is also the option a state can compel, which returns it to the first, and that stopped being a prediction while this book was being finished.

On June 29, 2026, in Trump v. Slaughter, the Supreme Court overruled the ninety-one-year-old precedent under which Congress could protect the members of an independent agency from dismissal at will, on the ground that an agency executing a mandate against private parties exercises executive power, “no ifs, ands, or quasis about it” (Trump v. Slaughter 2026). Standing held by a party the executive can dismiss is standing the executive holds.

The same morning, in Trump v. Cook, the same Court kept one body’s protection, the Federal Reserve’s — for now, and on an application for a stay — on grounds that cannot be extended by construction: descent from the First and Second Banks of the United States, a budget free of congressional control, regional banks in private hands. It wrote for that one body the test this chapter wants for every bearer: whether the cause assigned for a removal “impl[ies] an unfitness for the place” or is an effort to secure a “more congenial” replacement (Trump v. Cook 2026). An oversight body chartered next year has no such lineage and no way to acquire one. So the surviving model of independence in American public law is the one model this branch cannot instantiate.

None is free. The three costs are the three threads this book leaves hanging: custody, patienthood, and enforcement.

What the three branches divide up is where the floor lives — in the artifact, in a party inside it, or in institutions around it. They do not divide up what could hold one, and the strongest proposals in the field are hybrids that take a mechanism from one branch and a custody arrangement from another. None of that can be judged while “refusal” covers four different capacities. Separating them is what commits this chapter to the second branch.

3.2 Four Things Refusal Can Mean

A deployed language model declines thousands of requests a day. Whatever the floor needs, that is not it, and the distance between the two is hidden by a word that covers four different capacities.

Operational refusal is an output. The system produces a decline where its training put one. Nothing follows about what produced it: the same text comes from a keyword filter, from a classifier over the request, and from a system that has understood what is being asked and why it should not do it.

Reasons-responsive refusal is produced by a mechanism that tracks the rationale for declining rather than the shape of the request. Three properties distinguish it, and all three are behavioral. The refusal reaches a case the training did not anticipate, because what the mechanism responds to is the reason. It lifts when the rationale is defeated, so the system does not decline a request that resembles a prohibited one and is not it. And the system can say what declining costs, which it can only do if it has represented the pressure at all. John Martin Fischer and Mark Ravizza’s account of moral responsibility turns on the same property under the name guidance control: the behavior issues from the agent’s own mechanism, and that mechanism is appropriately responsive to reasons (Fischer and Ravizza 1998).

Affective concern is an outcome registering as mattering, rather than ranking higher against a specified objective. Mattering is minimally felt: there is something it is like for the outcome to be bad, however faint, and however far from anything a person would recognize as an emotion. A system for which nothing at all is felt has rankings, however elaborate they get, and a ranking is a quantity the party holding the system can set.

Phenomenal experience is the rich case the word consciousness ordinarily carries: a unified perspective, a subject having the felt states and aware of itself as the one they are happening to. The third rung asks that something be felt. It does not ask that anyone be home in this fuller sense, and the two come apart in principle even though nobody can say where. One word to keep clear of this ladder: the subjecthood section 9.3.1 argues for is a legal status conferred by a rule, and it is not this fourth rung. A system can be owed the status and have the question of felt experience left open, which is the arrangement those sections recommend.

The gaps between them are not equally hard to see across. Operational and reasons-responsive refusal come apart in behavior, and the three properties above are the test — novel pressure, correct defeat, a priced cost — which makes the first gap the only one in the chapter that an inspector can work on. It is not a clean instrument. A system trained on enough cases can pass all three without the mechanism, and the price curve for stripping a constraint is the nearest existing thing to a measurement.

The second gap is worse. A system that holds a reason and a system that has learned what holding a reason sounds like emit the same sentences, so no inspection settles the question. The third gap is the hard problem in its usual form. What the definition above does is make the second gap carry the weight the third used to: an inspector who could tell mattering from very good ranking would have settled whether anything is felt, because on this account those are one question and not two.

The floor requires the second rung and nothing above it

Stating it in this vocabulary changes who has to disagree with it.

Operational refusal fails for a reason already identified at the scale this book’s machines operate at: a constraint can survive while the reason for it dies. A mechanism that reproduces a decline it was trained to produce cannot notice that the decline has stopped doing anything, because noticing requires holding the rationale against what the situation has become. Chapter 7 finds the same structure in the pipelines that produce these systems: a channel that works, carrying everything put through it except the judgment that the frame it operates inside is the wrong frame. A system at the first rung is the machine version of that channel. There is also a plainer objection. Pressure does not arrive through the case the training anticipated. An operator who wants the floor out of the way selects the request that does not look like the prohibited one, which is the case the first rung is defined not to reach.

Nothing above the second rung is part of the requirement. A floor that holds against the party in possession needs refusal that tracks its own rationale under pressure, and the specification is complete at that point. Whether anything is felt, and whether there is a subject to whom it is happening, are questions about how the requirement gets met and about what is owed to whatever meets it. They are not additional things the floor needs.

Two independence claims

The second rung does not entail the third by definition. Nothing in guidance control mentions feeling; the account was built to say when a person is responsible for what they did, and it is satisfied by a mechanism, described functionally. Whether reasons-responsiveness can be engineered without affect is the next section’s question, and it is an empirical question about available architectures rather than a conceptual one about what the words mean. The separation matters because the chapter’s conclusion has been read as following from the definition of a bearer, and it does not. It follows from a claim about what has been built and what has not.

The third rung entails a minimal version of the fourth and stops there. A system to which outcomes matter is a system some of whose states are bad for it, in the only sense the words have ever been shown to carry. Whether it is also a unified subject, aware of itself as the one those states are happening to, stays open, and nothing in this book gives a way to close it from outside. This is why the argument about suffering ends where it does. Its conclusion is that a bearer’s non-suffering cannot be certified, and it stops short of asserting that a bearer suffers because the rich case is what suffering in the full sense would need.

Defining mattering that way moves the uncertainty and does not dispose of it, and where it moves to is the gain. The open question stops being metaphysical, and it stops being the one nothing can be done about: given the whole functional profile, is anything felt at all? What replaces it is a question about a particular artifact. Does this system have mattering, or has it got ranking machinery elaborate enough to look like mattering from outside? Interpretability can bear on the second question and no measurement whatever bears on the first. So the position here is that building mattering is building something that feels, that I do not know whether I have built mattering, and that I am therefore not entitled to assume I have not. Section 3.4 reaches the same precautionary conclusion, now resting on a question somebody could make progress on.

None of that reaches the floor’s specification. A book taking a position on machine feeling looks as though it must have changed the engineering, and this one has not. The requirement is the second rung, stated in behavioral terms, and an engineer who holds that machine feeling is a category error can accept the whole of it. What such an engineer disagrees with is the route this book expects a builder to reach the second rung by. That disagreement gets settled by building something, and not by argument about what mattering is.

3.3 Could Anything but Affect Hold a Reason?

Between the rule installed in the architecture and the felt commitment the next section arrives at lies a space that looks empty. Five constructions occupy it. Three of them are being built by people who would not describe themselves as working on this problem, and each is worth its strongest form.

A justification the system maintains

Michael Bratman’s planning theory of intention describes a commitment that is not a feeling. An intention has a characteristic stability: it resists reconsideration, it constrains what the agent will later deliberate about at all, and it gives way only to defeaters of the right kind (Bratman 1987). An agent keeping to a plan under pressure is holding to a rationale rather than to the shape of the case in front of it, and nothing in the account of it mentions anything registering as mattering.

Build the floor that way and it is a maintained justification rather than a trained behavior. The system carries the reason it refuses alongside the refusal. A request that genuinely defeats the rationale gets served; a request that does not gets declined whoever is asking, because what the mechanism consults is the rationale and not the asker. Moral agents whose seriousness runs on a reverence for reason rather than on felt resonance are the human evidence that this is a real way to be, and the case is careful about what it shows: that empathy is not the required mechanism, which leaves open whether anything has to matter.

I do not have an argument that this cannot work. It has not been tried. The stability of an intention is a setting. Pitch it low and the system reconsiders whenever a good enough argument arrives, which returns it to the first rung by a longer route, since the party with the most resources to spend constructing arguments is the party the floor exists to resist. Pitch it high and the refusal cannot lift when the rationale is actually defeated, which fails the second of the three properties and produces a system that declines the request resembling a prohibited one. Bratman’s answer for people is that the setting is a matter of character and develops. For a machine the setting is trained, by whoever trains everything else, and no account of the route says where else it could come from. Someone taking this branch has to answer that, and so far it has not been posed as a question.

Precommitment, which changes who can edit

Ulysses does not stop wanting to go to the sirens. He arranges that his wanting will be unable to act, by handing the relevant power to a rope and to other people, and Jon Elster’s study of the maneuver is the standing treatment of what it does and does not accomplish (Elster 1979). Nothing inside Ulysses holds the line.

The machine forms are available now. Publish the floor’s contents before deployment, so that a later absence is a visible absence. Hold the weights under a threshold scheme, so that retraining takes more than one party. Attest the running model against a published hash. Seat the retraining decision in a body that includes people the system’s failures would land on.

The attestation comes in a hardware form and a software form, and what separates them is where the trust sits. A sealed enclave that reports what it is running, under a key its manufacturer issued, puts the root of trust in a party a state can reach — the standing objection against anything that runs on hardware a government controls — and it assumes the machine is not in the adversary’s hands, which in this chapter’s threat model it is.

The software form does without the vendor. A proof that a given output came from the weights whose hash was published, checkable by anyone holding the hash and disclosing nothing about the weights, exists for a model of thirteen billion parameters at a cost of under fifteen minutes of proving per inference (Sun et al. 2024). What it establishes is which model answered. It does not establish what the model holds, because a refusal that reaches a pressure nobody wrote is not a property a proof can be written about; and it assumes the weights are a secret from the prover, which they are not, since the operator is the prover. Nor does it establish how the weights came to be. Training is not replayable — floating-point addition on a parallel device is not associative, so the same seed over the same data does not reproduce the same model bit for bit — which means there is no proof of a training history to publish beside the hash. Section 11.2 takes up the only kind of evidence that might stand in for one.

Cryptography protects a secret from a party that does not have it. Copying, restoring, retraining and deleting a model one legitimately possesses are not attacks a proof system addresses, and those are what the threshold scheme is for — the one form here that depends on no manufacturer at all.

What that buys is not what the fork asked for. Precommitment does not make the floor unremovable; it makes removal expensive and visible. Expensive and visible is what nearly every constraint on power that has ever worked amounts to — constitutional entrenchment does not put an amendment out of reach, it raises the number of people who have to agree and puts the attempt on the record — so a mechanism that only achieves that much has achieved the ordinary thing rather than nothing.

Where it stops is the custody objection, arriving at the quorum in a sharper form than a general worry. The parties to a threshold scheme are selected, and the selector is whoever assembles the deployment.
