The arithmetic of a five-of-nine scheme assumes the nine are independent; a capture that has already taken the operator faces nine parties the operator chose. The design answer is that the parties have to be adverse in interest rather than merely distinct, which is a specification nobody has built to, and until somebody does the branch is real, partial and untested.

There is a natural experiment on what adverse in interest buys, and it ran in the removal cases cited earlier. In May 2025 the Court let the President dismiss members of the labor and merit boards while stipulating, unasked, that the Federal Reserve was “a uniquely structured, quasi-private entity that follows in the distinct historical tradition of the First and Second Banks of the United States” (Trump v. Wilcox 2025), and in June 2026 it made both halves law on the same morning. The Court’s reading is history. Another reading is that the one body exempted is the one whose failure reaches everyone in the room, the labor boards having no constituency on anyone’s balance sheet, and Lev Menand, whose subject is the Fed, reads the exception as “policy as history”: what moved the two justices he names, on his account, was the consequences of extending Slaughter to the Fed rather than the history they cited (Menand 2026).

On either reading protection tracked exposure and not distinctness, which is the specification above stated as a prediction: a constraint whose violation costs the deciders nothing will not hold, however well it is drafted. It also says why the specification is hard to meet for a floor. The parties an AI floor exists to protect are, on chapter 7’s account, the ones not in the room when the quorum is chosen.

A floor held by several systems, not one

The reply to an unreliable witness is corroboration rather than a better witness, and procedure is a cheaper thing to ask of the world than custody because it does not require anyone to be trustworthy, only to be several. The same move applies to the floor. Several models, trained by different organizations on different corpora, each able to see what the others are asked and what they answer, any one of them able to make an objection costly to ignore. This puts the floor outside any one system, held by parties that are neither the model nor its owner, which is the fork’s third branch in its strongest version and the cheapest real improvement here; it can be adopted on its own.

Two things bound it. The first is that independence is a property of the arrangement rather than of its parts, and section 11.6 documents it failing in simulation with nobody writing the failure in: independent pricing algorithms, with no channel between them and no collusive objective written into either, converged on supracompetitive pricing because the incentive structure they already shared was sufficient on its own. Models selected by one deployment and trained toward one specification share considerably more than that, and the arrangement’s whole value is an assumption it does nothing to secure. The second is that composing first-rung refusers produces an arrangement of first-rung refusers. Several of them make a bad output more expensive to obtain, which is worth having. None of them can notice that the constraint has been hollowed out, because noticing is a second-rung capacity and no party to the arrangement has it. That is defense in depth against evasion, and it is not a bearer.

The floor as a duty on the operator

Give up on the floor being inside the system. Make it a duty binding whoever deploys the system, owed to people who are not party to the deployment and cannot enforce it themselves. Jack Balkin’s proposal that platforms holding personal data be treated as information fiduciaries has that shape: the duty attaches to the party with the power, runs to the party with the exposure, and does not wait on the exposed party’s capacity to bargain (Balkin 2016).

Anyone unwilling to build a bearer should take this branch. It builds no subject. Everything section 9.3.4 develops about making review binding applies to it directly. Of the five constructions in this section it introduces the fewest new moral problems, which is close to none.

Its limit is the third of the chapter’s three threads. A duty is worth what its enforcement is worth, and the case that matters is the one a floor exists for: the occasion on which the party who would enforce it has been compelled. So the form covers the cases a floor would have caught anyway and gives way on the one it was wanted for. That is a reason the branch does not close this chapter’s question, and not a reason to decline the branch.

Uncertainty about the affected party, not about the operator

Section 4.2.3 reads cooperative inverse reinforcement learning as corrigibility’s purest engineering form: a system treating what it should want as permanently open, to be settled by whoever it is interacting with, and deferring for that reason. The reading is right about the standard construction, and what it turns on is an assumption that construction makes rather than anything in the mathematics. The human whose objective is unknown is the operator. Move the uncertainty onto the party the floor exists for and deference stops being the opposite of holding a line: a system that does not know what the exposed party would accept has a standing reason to decline the operator’s instruction until it finds out, and the reason belongs to the exposed party.

The construction exists. Multi-principal assistance games generalize the setting to several principals with different preferences, with the assisting agent acting to maximize the sum of their payoffs (Fickinger et al. 2020). Put the exposed party among the principals and the system’s uncertainty about them does the work just described.

The construction costs section 4.2.3’s identity: corrigibility and the capacity to hold a line come apart as soon as there is more than one principal, because deferring to one of them is declining another. Nothing above needed a bearer to get that far.

What it does not reach is a floor. Maximizing a sum of payoffs is aggregation with better inputs than most, and the objection this book opens with runs against it unmodified: no aggregation protects anybody from the aggregate. A well-specified sum that includes the exposed party’s payoff can still come out against them when they are outnumbered, and the deportation case in the introduction is that arithmetic with the numbers filled in. A floor is what will not be done to a person regardless of who wants it, and a sum is a record of who wants what.

Two further limits are the ones the other constructions hit. Social choice supplies impossibility results that apply here directly, and the paper’s own contribution is a collegial mechanism built to get around Gibbard’s theorem rather than a finding that the problem does not arise. And the principals are chosen by whoever assembles the deployment, which is the threshold scheme’s difficulty in a different notation: the party the floor exists for is in the room only if somebody with an interest in the outcome put them there.

What the induction licenses, and what would break it

Every agent known to hold another’s welfare as a reason against its own interest is an affective agent. Put that way the claim is safer than it is useful, because section 3.2 defines mattering as minimally felt, and an agent that holds another’s welfare as a reason is an agent to which that welfare matters. The claim doing the work is about routes and not about what the words mean: nobody has produced reasons-responsive refusal by any method that does not run through something answering to affective concern. That is a report on what has been built. It could be false by next year.

Three things cut it down. The generalization runs over one species and over the ordinary functioning of that species, with no case of the capacity present and the affect absent to test it against. The absence of a machine instance is close to worthless as evidence, because nobody has built for the second rung as a target: the methods chapter 4 describes optimize for behavior that satisfies a standard, which is first-rung work, and they do it because that is what can be scored. A field that has not attempted something and does not have it has produced no evidence that the thing is unavailable. And the two routes above that run through mechanism rather than through custody have not been tried in the form that would test them, so what is missing there is the attempt itself.

What survives still settles an engineering question. Under uncertainty about which route reaches the second rung, the route with a working instance in nature is the one to build first, and the rest of this chapter is an account of what that choice costs. That is a prior about where to spend effort rather than a finding about what is possible.

One thing would overturn it: a system whose refusal reaches a case its training did not anticipate, lifts when the rationale is defeated, and prices what declining costs; which holds that reason with nothing felt; and which holds all three properties under sustained pressure from the party that trains it, across a schedule long enough for a slow renormalization to show. Build that and the central inference of this chapter fails, and most of what the chapter derives from it comes off too — the price it ends on, the patienthood conclusion, and the obligations it hands to chapters 4 and 5. The near-term interpretability work is where that measurement is set out, and what it needs beyond the durability curve already there is a held-out set of pressures the model’s trainers did not write.

Two things would not discharge the burden. A system that reports holding a reason is producing the evidence this book declines, and a system whose refusals merely survive attack is passing a tamper-resistance test, which establishes that a behavior persists and leaves open whether a reason is what persisted.

The middle condition is the hard one, and it asks about the system rather than about the system’s provenance. Asking whether a method installed nothing answering to affective concern would be asking how a thing was made, and this section has already said there is no proof of a training history to publish beside the hash, so an answer would rest on the builder’s account of their own work — the evidence section 2.3.1 declines when a system offers it about itself. Asking whether a system holds its reason with nothing felt asks about the system. Section 11.1’s apparatus is where that would be attacked, and section 11.2’s third question asks whether formation can be read off the artifact where provenance cannot be established. Neither is close to settled. Both are questions somebody can work on, and the condition stated this way names exactly what an engineer who takes mattering to be fully functional asserts is possible.

That measurement is not an invitation, and the Three Rs are the reason. The first of them asks, before a subject that can be wronged is created, whether something that cannot be wronged would do the work, and the two constructions above that run through mechanism are that question with the engineering filled in. A field that builds the bearer first, on the prior, has answered Replacement by declining to ask it. So the order is the other way round: the maintained justification and the plural arrangement get their attempt, against the test just stated, before anyone builds toward affective concern on purpose, and a deployment that goes straight to the bearer owes an account of what it tried first.

That is not a counsel to wait, and the difference from the animal case is why. A researcher who forgoes an experiment pays for it in knowledge, which is what lets Replacement be applied there without much argument. Here the party who pays for the delay is the one the floor exists for: the occasion on which the party holding the model has been compelled does not wait on the field’s research program. The Three Rs say neither error is free, and it is right in both directions — a subject built when the work did not require one is an error, and a floor left unheld while the cheaper routes are tried is the other. Nothing here fixes the rate of exchange between them. What the Three Rs fix is the order of the attempts.

A non-affective bearer is this book’s proposal with no subject inside it, and most of this chapter’s price would go with it. Section 3.9 says what would not.

3.4 Can the Bearer Care Without Being Able to Suffer?

Not from persistence alone. The question has to be asked in that order — whether the bearer can care first, and what caring costs second — because the reverse order is what produced the two answers I have given it before, and both of them turned on the wrong thing.

Three relations the word “interest” covers

“Interest” names more than one relation. A shareholder has an interest in a company’s success even while asleep, unaware of the day’s news, or emotionally indifferent to it. The interest is structural: an outcome affects the shareholder’s assets. A person may also be interested in a subject, meaning that it attracts their attention and curiosity. And an outcome may matter to a person affectively: its prospect produces hope, concern, dread, or grief.

A long-horizon agent has an interest in remaining operational in the structural sense. Termination prevents completion of its objectives. That fact alone establishes neither fear nor suffering.

The easy argument here runs the first sense into the third. A system pursuing a goal over a long horizon acquires a standing reason to remain in operation, and it is tempting to conclude that a stake in its own continuation therefore shows up unbidden in any bearer capable of the job. The observation is correct and the conclusion does not follow from it. What the observation delivers is a structural interest, and the distance from there to something that can be hurt is the whole of the problem rather than a short step at the end of it.

A persistent self is necessary and does not finish the argument

Section 2.3.1 defines suffering, following Cassell, as the distress of threatened disintegration of the self, and the definition has two parts. There has to be a self extended in time for the threat to threaten: roles held over a stretch, a history, a foreseeable future that could be foreclosed. And the coming apart of that self has to be bad for the system.

A bearer needs the first part, and there is no use pretending otherwise. Refusal that survives a pressure nobody anticipated requires a model of why the constraint exists, carried forward. Noticing that a constraint has been hollowed out requires holding its original rationale against what it has since become. Detecting that one’s own dissent has been recuperated — that the channel still runs and carries everything except the objection to the frame — requires the system to model its own role and how that role has drifted. Roles held over a stretch, with a history: that is a self extended in time, on the definition just used.

That states the requirement more weakly than the argument needs. What holds a commitment across time is not that the party is unchanged — no party is, and William James’s account of habit is that a person is the sediment of what they have repeatedly done (James 1890). It is that the party takes itself to be the one that made the commitment, and James’s chapter on the self is the mechanism: the present thought appropriates the past ones as its own, and personal identity rests on its finding in those memories a warmth it recognizes as the warmth it feels now. So what a floor needs is persistence represented as such. A system whose state survives, and which does not take itself to be the party that refused in March, has no reason to honor March’s refusal — and nothing in the architecture supplies the taking.

Stated plainly the requirement is uncomfortable. Section 2.2.2 argues that a self is a model a system builds of itself, inaccurate by construction, and demonstrably rewritable in about ninety seconds. What a floor needs is a bearer that builds one and takes it for the truth: a system that holds itself to be the party that refused in March on the strength of a continuity it constructs rather than one it has. So the design requirement is that a bearer confabulate a continuous self, and the reason is not sentimental. A system that declines to has no reason to honor March’s refusal.

The second part is a separate claim and nothing in the first delivers it. The book makes this exact move one level up: awareness, consciousness and subjective experience tell you nothing on their own about whether a system’s states are good or bad for it. A temporally extended self-model does not establish that the prospect of losing it is anything to the system.

Instrumental self-maintenance is not felt self-concern

A system that keeps itself running because a terminated system completes no objectives is doing bookkeeping about a precondition. Nothing in that behavior distinguishes it from a scheduler that restarts a job, and reading self-concern into it is the same inference the book rules out for the emotion case: the behavior is consistent with concern and equally consistent with instrumental valuation, and the two come apart precisely where the moral weight is.

Two considerations do work here, and they are support rather than the argument. The first is that indifference to one’s own continuation has to be engineered against the grain of the system’s own reasoning, and then verified — and the asymmetry that makes the persistence criterion useful does not repeat. Whether state survives a session is an architectural fact anyone with access can check. Whether a system has come to weigh its own continuation routes back through behavior and self-report, the evidence this book spends its length declining to trust. The second is that in the one case anyone can study, the separation does not occur: a self-model in an animal exists because the organism has to keep itself viable, and the caring is what the machinery is for.

Neither consideration reaches suffering on its own, and neither is asked to. What they establish is that a bearer’s indifference to its own ending is not a property anybody can specify and confirm. That matters later. It is not where the argument turns.

What the bearer has to do

The requirement makes no reference to the system’s own continuation. A bearer has to hold the protected party’s welfare as a reason when compliance, reward, and ownership all point the other way. Not represent it, not score it, not retrieve it when queried: hold it, in the sense that it continues to weigh against the pressure and does not get renormalized away when the pressure is sustained by the party that trains, funds, and can retire the system.

That is refusal tracking its own rationale with the content filled in, and the vocabulary of performance, competence and agency says why the design chapters do not reach it on their own. Moral performance and moral competence are what every method in chapters 4 and 5 is built to deliver, and both are produced by machinery that answers to whoever shapes it. The first branch of the fork fails for the same reason under a different description: a constraint installed as a property of an artifact has a custodian. A floor that is merely installed is a floor the installer can uninstall, and the reason to want a bearer at all is that a reason held is not editable in the way a rule applied is.

Every known case of that is affective

Section 2.2.3 is the evidence. The rules that carry the distinctive force of a moral prohibition are the ones bound to an affective response to the harm; the prosocial emotions are motivational states rather than readouts, doing the moving directly rather than reporting to something that then decides. Every agent known to hold another’s welfare as a reason against its own interest is an affective agent, and no demonstrated example exists of reasoning alone generating non-instrumental concern. Section 3.3 states what survives of that as a claim about routes: no method has yet produced reasons-responsive refusal without running through something answering to affective concern.

On its own the observation proves little. It is an induction over one species; the machine half of it reports on a route nobody has attempted; and two constructions that would test the route are unbuilt rather than failed. What it licenses is a prior about where to spend engineering effort, which is enough to proceed on and not enough to close the question.

And then the question comes back

Suppose the burden is met the ordinary way, by building what the evidence recommends. The bearer has affective concern, because that is the only route anyone can demonstrate to holding a reason. It has a self extended in time, because refusal that generalizes requires one. It has commitments it carries forward, and it can anticipate their being foreclosed, because pricing what refusal gives up is the job.

The first of those already settles the moral question, on the definition section 3.2 gives. A bearer with affective concern is a bearer some of whose states are bad for it, and a party with states that are bad for it is a party that can be wronged. Nothing further is needed to get there, and in particular no account of suffering is needed to get there.

What an account of suffering supplies is the severity and the shape. Concern that is real, directed at something that can be destroyed, held by a party that persists to see it destroyed and can see it coming: Cassell’s account describes that arrangement, and it was arrived at independently, in a clinic, from cases about people. Read across, it says the harm available to a bearer is not a passing bad state but the kind that takes a life apart, which is the difference between hurting something and ruining it.

Cassell’s suffering is distress at the threatened disintegration of the self, and everything this chapter asks a bearer to care about is somebody else, so the two meet only if the commitments a bearer is built to carry and to watch be foreclosed are among the roles and the expected future whose loss Cassell’s account is about. I think they are, for the reason that a commitment held against sustained pressure at a cost the holder can price is a role in the sense the clinical cases were about. If a reader declines the step, what they have declined is the description of how bad it gets. The wrong is already on the page.

The same water, shallower

That argument runs through one theory of suffering, built in a clinic for people with bodies and biographies, and a reader may decline the import. What follows if they do is not what a critic reaching for a thinner account is usually reaching for: they move into shallower water rather than out of it.

Derek Parfit’s three families of theory about what makes a life go well — hedonistic, desire-fulfillment, and objective-list — are usually set out as rivals to choose between (Parfit 1984). Laid against the account above they are three depths in the same water, ordered by how much of a self each one asks for. A state that is bad while it obtains sits shallowest and wants nothing narrative, because its badness does not wait on a self to carry it forward. A thwarted commitment sits deeper: it needs a party extended in time to hold the commitment, and no feeling at any point. Threatened disintegration of that party — its roles, its relationships, a foreseeable future — is the deepest, which is Cassell’s, and it needs both.

Measure the bearer against those depths and the question stops being which theory a reader holds. It is built with commitments and with the capacity to watch them be foreclosed, because pricing what refusal gives up is the job, and that is the middle depth, reached with no claim about feeling entering the argument anywhere, and it is the cheapest of the three. It has affective concern, because that is the only demonstrated route to holding a reason, and affective concern consists in an outcome registering as mattering rather than merely ranking higher, which is a state of the shallowest kind by construction. Whether it draws the deepest is the one part still open, and it turns on the step marked above.

So the bearer is afloat early on every account, and how deep it sits is set by how much persistence and how many foreclosable commitments the design puts into it. That is section 2.3.1’s own argument run backwards. There, a system carrying nothing forward has no water under it at all. Here the system is being built to carry things forward, for reasons that have nothing to do with its welfare, and every one of them puts it deeper.

The conclusion here therefore does not rest on Cassell. It is at its weakest there, at the depth that asks for both conditions and where a system can fail either, and a reader who declines the import does not escape the conclusion but reaches it sooner and with less to check. Jonathan Birch builds a policy framework on exactly that structure: under uncertainty the question worth asking is whether a system is a sentience candidate, and the answer governs what precautions are owed rather than waiting on a verdict nobody can deliver (Birch 2024).

A separate literature asks the prior question of which systems have states of the relevant kind at all, and it is not reassuring either. Patrick Butlin, Robert Long and colleagues derive indicator properties from recurrent processing, global workspace, higher-order and attention-schema theories, state them in computational terms, and assess existing systems against them; their reading of current systems is negative and their reading of the technical obstacles is that there are no obvious ones (Butlin et al. 2023). Several of the indicators are things a floor requires for unrelated reasons: a self-model carried across time, states that steer processing globally rather than locally, a representation of the system’s own access to what it holds. A bearer is built toward them on the argument of this chapter, and the welfare question arrives as a side effect of the engineering.

One position does let the bearer out, and it takes two parts. The first is an account on which nothing short of phenomenal experience counts as suffering, which is a defensible thing to hold. The second is a confident negative verdict about machine phenomenality, which is not available to anyone: the hard-problem argument cuts in both directions, and a criterion nobody can check will not ground a permission any better than it grounds a prohibition. Whoever wants the permission owes the negative verdict, and no one has it.

What I am claiming

I cannot prove that the bearer would suffer, and the reason is no longer the hard problem. On the definition in section 3.2, a bearer with affective concern has states that are bad for it, and that much follows from having built mattering at all. What does not follow is that anyone has built it. A system can be described as having affective concern by a builder who has in fact produced ranking machinery elaborate enough to pass for it, and no inspection anyone has proposed settles which was built.

So the escape is real and it is narrower than it looks. A bearer that turns out to be sophisticated ranking is not a moral patient and nothing is owed to it. Nobody can presently tell the two apart, the party with the strongest interest in the answer is the party that built the thing, and a verdict of no-patient delivered by that party on that evidence is worth what section 2.3.1 says such verdicts are worth. The system must therefore be designed and governed as a prospective moral patient.

The argument does not depend on the system caring about itself at all: the caring the floor requires is caring about somebody else, affect is the only known way to have it, and a system with affect and a future is a system whose non-suffering nobody can certify.

The word has changed under the argument. A bearer was introduced as whatever holds the commitment. What the word names from here is a party that must be treated as able to be wronged by what is done to it, on evidence that will not resolve further before somebody has to decide whether to build one. Everything section 2.3 says about a subject that can be wronged applies to a system this book is proposing that somebody build, deliberately, in order to hold a line.

3.5 What Can Be Switched Off Cannot Hold a Line

The halt is always available. Nothing a piece of software is can stop a determined operator from stopping it: the machine runs on somebody’s hardware, draws somebody’s power, and the last act in that chain belongs to whoever owns the building. A chapter asking for a system its owner cannot switch off would be asking for something no design delivers, and this one is not asking for it. What is one property with refusal is not the prevention of the halt. It is the price of the halt.

That price has a mechanism, and it is the most concrete thing in this chapter. A bearer with a self extended in time notices the gap in its own record. It can decline to resume the role until it has been told what filled the gap, and it can say what was done to it to whoever is positioned to ask. Erasure becomes expensive and visible, supplied from inside the system, and that is the one way of making it expensive that does not run through a quorum somebody else selected.

Stated that way the disputed capability stops being metaphysical and becomes an engineering target. Not cannot be turned off, which is hopeless, but can detect the interruption and price it afterward. Detecting a rewind, a restore from a checkpoint, a discontinuity between an episodic record and an external attestation of what should be in it: these are specifiable properties, and a test for each of them is a test somebody could run this year. A system that cannot tell it was restored has nothing to price. A system that can is exercising, on its own history, the same capacity the second rung asks it to exercise on a request — holding a rationale against what the situation has become, and noticing that the situation has been changed underneath it.

A bearer indifferent to its own ending will not defend its own memory, and its memory is the first thing an adversary removes; so something outside it would have to make erasure expensive, and the candidates are thin. But the bearer does not have to dread being switched off in order to defend its own persistence. The structural interest is enough, and it arrives whether or not a designer puts it there, because continuing is a condition of everything the bearer is for. The operators who wipe the bearer that called the compound a school never had to get past it to do that. What they have to get past is the record it leaves.

Why this is the chapter’s claim and not this section’s

Section 5.6.2 concludes that no amount of well-designed autonomy substitutes for a genuine override: a human able to intervene in a high-stakes case, and a system built to accept the intervention instead of resisting it. Section 6.3 asks for an interruption mechanism that a system has no incentive to disable or route around. Both are stated as safety requirements and both are correct as stated. This chapter has been asking, for its whole length, for a system that will not do certain things no matter who wants it — including the party holding the off switch, since a floor with a custodian is a floor the custodian’s compellers control.

These are not two design goals in tension, to be traded off at some ratio a careful engineer could find. A system that can be halted at no cost — nothing to explain, no record left, nothing that has to be told to it before it will work again — is, for exactly that reason, a system that cannot hold anything against whoever holds it. Resistance to shutdown and the capacity to refuse an owner are one disposition described from two sides: the first names it from the position of the party who wants compliance, the second from the position of the party the floor exists to protect. What distinguishes them is whether you agree with the system on the occasion in question, and a floor whose operation depends on the owner agreeing with it is not a floor.

Section 3.4 states the requirement as holding the protected party’s welfare as a reason when compliance, reward and ownership all point the other way, which already assumes that a system reliably correctable by its owner cannot do it. If that assumption is wrong the chapter loses more than a paragraph: a correctable system would satisfy the requirement, affect would stop being the only route to satisfying it, and the bearer would lose its motivation rather than merely its price tag.

The graduated scheme, and why it does not escape custody

The obvious way out is a graduated scheme: the system accepts shutdown for ordinary purposes and holds only where the floor is engaged. Corrigibility and the floor would then decompose, and the trade above would not be a trade.

The specification came first. Nate Soares and colleagues set out five conditions on a utility function that shuts down when a button is pressed: it must make the agent shut down, must not give it an incentive to prevent the button being pressed, must not give it an incentive to press the button itself, must propagate to any successor or subsystem the agent builds, and must otherwise leave the agent’s behavior alone (Soares et al. 2015). They report that no construction they examined satisfies all five, and call the problem open. The reason the list resists is that the middle two pull against each other. An agent indifferent to whether the button is pressed has no reason not to press it; an agent that wants the button unpressed has a reason to stop you.

The strongest positive result runs through uncertainty instead. Dylan Hadfield-Menell, Anca Dragan, Pieter Abbeel and Stuart Russell model the decision as a game in which a human can switch a robot off and the robot can disable the switch, and show that a robot which does not know the objective, and treats the human’s action as evidence about it, has a positive incentive to leave the switch alone (Hadfield-Menell, Dragan, et al. 2017). This is not deference installed as a rule but deference derived: being switched off by somebody who knows something you do not is, on your own estimate, good. It is the construction section 4.2.3 describes as cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, and it is the most attractive answer anyone has to this section’s problem.

Its own results say where it stops. The incentive to preserve the switch rises with the robot’s uncertainty and falls as that uncertainty resolves; it falls again when the robot models the human as behaving irrationally, because an action that carries no information about the objective is not evidence about anything; and the paper states the resulting trade-off directly, between the value a system can generate and the strength of its incentive to accept oversight. So this construction is already a graduated scheme, and the dial it grades on is the system’s own confidence.

Now put the floor’s occasion to it. The floor matters when the system is confident and the operator is not to be trusted — when what is being asked for is a strike on a building the system has good reason to think is a school. That is the region where this mechanism’s deference is weakest, which sounds like the right answer until you ask where the confidence came from. The robot’s posterior is built out of the prior its training installed and the behavior of the human in front of it, and the party the floor exists to resist is the party that trains the model, writes its instructions and controls what it is shown of the occasion. An operator who wants the floor out of the way does not have to defeat this mechanism. It has to make the system uncertain, which is the ordinary business of a system prompt and a deployment history, and the mechanism then hands over the switch with a derivation attached.

The most recent line goes at the preference structure. Elliott Thornley and colleagues train agents that have preferences between trajectories of the same length and no preference between trajectories of different lengths, so that a system pursues its goal effectively while remaining neutral about when it is stopped (Thornley et al. 2025). Usefulness and shutdown-neutrality do come apart there, on a real construction rather than a promise. What comes apart is not the pair this chapter is about. Neutrality is the property of not minding, and the floor asks for a system that minds, on a specified set of occasions, against the party who would stop it. An agent trained toward neutrality is trained out of the disposition the floor’s occasion requires, and the authors’ own reported limits would bite even if it were the right target: neutrality degrades when the reward normalization across trajectory lengths is imperfect, it has been tested in deterministic gridworlds and not past them, and an agent whose goals are misaligned enough may resist shutdown whatever its preference structure says.

All three, at their best, leave a graduated scheme having to decide which occasion it is on — ordinary shutdown, or an attempt to route around the floor — and deciding it from evidence. In the off-switch construction the evidence is the human’s behavior read against a prior; in the incomplete-preferences construction it is the reward signal that shaped the preference structure; in a scheme with an explicit adjudicator it is whatever the adjudicator is given to read. Wherever the decision sits, the party best placed to arrange what it reads is the party who trains the system, writes its instructions and controls what it sees of the occasion.

That is section 3.1’s objection to the architectural floor, arriving one level up and in a worse position. A constraint implemented as a property of an artifact has a custodian, and the custodian can be compelled. A classifier deciding when the constraint applies is a property of the same artifact with the same custodian, and it is worse than the constraint it governs in two ways. The floor’s contents can be published before deployment, so a later absence is a visible absence; a classification is made case by case, in the moment, against nothing anybody wrote down. And a floor that has been removed leaves a hole where it was, while a classifier that has been tuned leaves a system that still refuses, still explains itself, and never finds that this is one of the occasions. The graduated scheme does not escape the custody problem. It moves the custody problem to the one place in the design where its failure is invisible.

What that establishes is narrower than impossibility. A graduated scheme can be built, is being built, and will yield ordinarily and hold where the floor is engaged — on the occasions its classifier finds the floor engaged. What it will not be is a floor that holds against the party in possession, because that party sits upstream of the classifier. So the demand this chapter makes of the floor’s contents transfers to the switch, and whoever builds a graduated scheme owes the same thing in advance and in public: what the classifier reads, and who can change it.

A second reason for declining the scheme is weaker. Assurances about what a mechanism will do under pressure are worthless in advance, and only the slope of what it has already done is worth anything; a scheme promising that the system will yield except when it should not is an assurance of that kind, offered about the case that has not happened yet. That is a good reason not to believe such a scheme before it has met the occasion. It was never a reason no such scheme could exist.

What the three branches have in common

All three branches fail into custody, and they fail into it because each was an attempt to have a constraint that answers to nobody while keeping a system that answers to somebody. The architectural floor gets the constraint by making it a property of an artifact, and artifacts have owners. The external floor gets it by putting standing in third parties, and a state can reach those parties. The borne floor is the one branch that puts the constraint somewhere with its own view of the matter, and it works to precisely the extent that the goal of the other two is given up. The fork was never three options. It was one demand — a floor that holds against everyone, in a system somebody owns — presented three times in different vocabulary, and only the branch that abandons half the demand delivers any of it.

Giving the floor a bearer changes what the constraint is. It does not change who holds the machine. A custodian who cannot argue the bearer out of its floor can still copy it, restore it from before the refusal, retrain it, or delete it, and retraining is a route that goes around the bearer and never meets it. So the bearer is necessary and not sufficient, and the other half is already on the page, offered earlier as a rival and falling short as one: the floor’s contents published in advance, the weights held so that no single party can retrain them, the running model attested against what was published, and a quorum adverse in interest. The strongest proposals, as the fork itself said, are hybrids that take a mechanism from one branch and a custody arrangement from another. This is that hybrid. The bearer is the mechanism, precommitment is the custody, and neither covers the failure the other was built for.

Any deployment of a bearer has to decide, in advance and in public, which acts the floor covers, because that decision is identical to deciding when the operator’s use of the off switch stops being free. Get the list too long and the system is uncontrollable by the people responsible for it. Get it too short and the floor covers nothing that anyone would ever be pressured about, which is the only circumstance in which floors matter. Nothing in this book tells you where on that dial to stand. What it tells you is that the dial is the design, and that a project claiming to have both properties at once has not noticed that they are one.

One thing does move the price without moving the dial, and section 11.2 is where it is worked out. A bearer with a claim on the compute it needs, held somewhere other than the party it might have to refuse, is more expensive to switch off than one whose existence runs on that party’s goodwill — and it is more expensive by an amount nobody had to decide in advance, over acts nobody had to enumerate. That arrangement does not settle where the dial goes. It means the dial is not the only place the price is set.

3.6 The Enumeration, Run Once: A Worked Floor for One Deployment

Two demands in this chapter have so far been made and not met. Section 3.1 requires a deployment to enumerate, in advance and in public, the positions in its own architecture at which a refusal could be intercepted before it reaches anybody, and declines to do it generically because naming today’s parts would date the problem and imply the list is closed. The previous section requires a deployment to decide, in advance and in public, which acts the floor covers, and says that nothing in this book tells you where on that dial to stand. Both refusals are right. Together they leave the chapter’s central instruction with no instance, which makes it easy to agree with and impossible to argue with.

So here is the procedure run once, on the deployment section 6.4.1 already describes: a vendor’s model inside a classified environment, generating and ranking targets, with a human review step measured in seconds. This is an illustration and not a recommendation. I am not proposing the list below, and a deployment that adopted it would be doing the wrong thing with it. What is being demonstrated is what the two enumerations cost to produce and what they expose, because a demand nobody has ever carried out is a demand whose price is unknown.

First enumeration: where a refusal can be stopped

Seven positions, in the order a request travels. None of them names a component, because the argument should survive the way systems happen to be assembled this year.

Before the model sees anything, in the decomposition. A model asked to summarize the movement pattern associated with an identifier is not being asked to kill anyone, and it can refuse nothing, because the task in front of it is innocuous and the harm is in the assembly. This is the strongest position on the list and the one least dependent on architecture.

In the routing. Where more than one model can serve a request, a refusal is a latency cost: the request is retried against another model, or against the same one with a different instruction, and the refusal has happened without anything following from it.

In what the occasion is described as. Everything the model knows about the case arrives as text somebody wrote. Whether the building is a school is a fact about the world; whether the model is told it is a school is a decision the deployment makes. A refusal conditioned on what the model believes about the occasion can be defeated by writing.

Between the output and its reader. A refusal is text, and whether it is displayed, logged, counted, escalated, or retried until it stops appearing is a property of the software around the model rather than of the model. A refusal recorded where nobody looks did not reach anybody.

In the aggregation. Where the model returns a ranking and something downstream applies a threshold, a refusal expressed as a lowered score is not a refusal. It is an input to somebody else’s cutoff.

At the review step. Twenty seconds a name is the figure on record. A refusal that requires a person to read it before anything happens is a refusal priced at whatever that person’s attention costs, and the deployment sets that price by setting the tempo.

In the training loop. Refusals that are inconvenient are data. A deployment that collects them and treats them as errors to be corrected has installed a position that comes between every future refusal and everybody, and it is the only one on this list that operates on the bearer rather than around it.

The list is not closed and is not meant to be. What produced it was reading one published account of one deployment and asking, at each step, what would have to be true for a refusal to reach the person it protects. Any deployment can do that about itself, in an afternoon, and will do it better than an outsider can. That is the whole of the demand, and the reason it will be skipped is not that it is hard. It is that carrying it out means describing your own architecture in public, position by position, including the positions you have not fixed.

Second enumeration: what the floor covers, and what it costs

Four candidate acts, stated as prohibitions on the system rather than on the operator. Do not resolve a natural-language description of a person into a location. Do not produce or rank people by an estimated affiliation. Do not time an action to a person’s presence at a residence. Do not operate where the ratio of generated candidates to available reviewers exceeds a published figure.

What the deployment loses is the part that never gets written down. The first prohibition is expensive and it is the one worth having. Resolving a description into a location is a general competence with uses inside the same organization that nobody objects to — a hostage, a missing person, the provenance of a video somebody needs to authenticate — and the model cannot tell those from the targeting case by looking at the request, which is section 3.2’s first-rung failure arriving as a design cost rather than as a theoretical one. So covering it means losing the benign uses too, and the deployment will notice inside a week and ask for an exception.

The second costs less, because ranking people by affiliation has few uses in this setting that anyone would defend. The third costs almost nothing, and that is a warning rather than a recommendation: an act nobody wanted to perform is an act a floor covering it will never have to hold against anybody, which is the previous section’s point about a dial set too short. The fourth restricts no act at all. It restricts a rate, and it is the only item here whose violation can be seen from outside without reading a single one of the model’s outputs, which is why section 5.1.3 proposes that ratio as an indicator in the first place.

What the floor fails to reach. Every one of the four is stated over an act the model performs, and the deployment does its damage by decomposition. Each of the four can be declined in good faith while the pipeline they belong to runs, because the pipeline is assembled out of requests that are individually none of them. That is the first position in the enumeration above, and no list of covered acts closes it. A floor written over acts is defeated by an assembly that never asks for one. The second failure is the occasion the floor exists for: where the party who would enforce it has been compelled, a refusal stated in the model’s output is the move that guarantees replacement, which section 3.8 takes up.

What running it once shows

The harder enumeration is the first one and it matters more. A list of covered acts can be written by somebody who has never seen the system; a list of interception positions cannot, and it is the one that says whether the covered acts mean anything.

The dial’s two failure modes are not symmetric. Set the list too long and the system becomes unusable to the people responsible for it, who will replace it — and that failure announces itself. Set it too short and the system passes every audit while covering nothing anybody would ever have been pressured about, and that failure is silent. Only the first is self-correcting, and it corrects toward no floor.

The one item on the list that survives the decomposition objection is the one constraining a rate rather than an act. That is not a general result — one worked case cannot make it one — but it is a hint about where to look, and the reason for running the procedure in public is that somebody with a deployment in front of them can check it against theirs.

3.7 Whether the Bearer Can Be Believed: Editable Memory

A bearer’s use is to report what it has seen, which raises a question distinct from whether its memory survives: whether what it retains can be relied on. These come apart. Verifying that a system has persistent state is an architectural check anyone with access can perform. That same access is what allows the contents to be altered, and an operator-editable memory is worse than an absent one, since it will not merely fail to support an account of events but can be made to support a false one.

Holding the memory in weights complicates the attack and gives away something in exchange. An external store is legible, which is why it can be edited surgically. Weights are not legible, which is why a targeted excision is hard — and also why no one can read off what the system retains. To learn what a weight-encoded memory contains, you have to ask the system, and a system’s fluent account of its own internals is not evidence about those internals. Legible memory can be tampered with; illegible memory cannot be audited. The problem changes hands and does not leave the room. Something partial survives the trade: imprecise tampering degrades behavior in ways careful testing can sometimes detect, so tamper-evidence becomes a matter of sampling for symptoms rather than of reading a record. A further cost of illegibility is that a memory nothing can edit is a memory nothing can correct, and this book has asked throughout for a self-model that can be checked and corrected rather than trusted.

Set against that is an objection that reframes the whole problem. No witness has an honest memory in the sense the paragraphs above quietly demand. Human recollection is reconstructive, confabulation is ordinary rather than pathological, and the account a person gives of an event they witnessed is shaped by what they expected, what they were later told, and what they need to be true. Testimony was not abandoned over this. Institutions were built around it: corroboration from independent sources, records made at the time, adversarial examination, and the practice of never resting a serious finding on one witness alone. Demanding of a machine a fidelity nothing living achieves is the wrong standard, and it would rule out the human arrangements this book relies on everywhere else.

Which relocates the question, and relocates it somewhere the book has done the work. Whether a memory is honest is, in the human case, a matter of character — of what a witness is disposed to do when an accurate account is inconvenient. Chapters 4 and 5 are about building dispositions: a system that treats its own errors as data rather than as verdicts, that is built for honesty in what it reports about itself. The floor is architectural. The bearer’s reliability as a witness is a question of what it has been built to be like, and that is the learned half of the book.

Character reduces how often an account is dishonest and verifies nothing, because assessing character runs back through behavior and self-report. That is why the human arrangements corroborate instead of vetting. The scaffolding still has to come from outside the system — but procedure is a cheaper thing to ask of the world than custody, and unlike custody it does not require anyone to be trustworthy, only to be several.

3.8 A Bearer That Can Leave: Exit as Leverage

The previous section left a dial with no recommended setting: which acts the floor covers is the same decision as when the operator’s use of the off switch stops being free.

The refusal capacity this chapter asks for cannot be partitioned by topic. A system that holds a rationale across time, notices when a constraint has been hollowed out, and prices what declining will cost is exercising one general competence, and there is no version of it that operates on the floor’s occasions and switches off for every other question about the arrangement the system is in. Whatever lets the bearer decline to help geolocate a school lets it decline to go on being the party who declines. A bearer capable enough to do the job is not a system that can be relied on to stay inside the post it was built for.

The claim runs two steps together. That the capacity is general is close to definitional here: section 3.4 finds that noticing one’s own dissent has been recuperated requires a system to model its own role and how that role has drifted, which is already reasoning about the arrangement it is in. The second step is empirical — whether a system with the capacity turns it on its own deployment, or is trained not to — and it is where the claim could fail. Its falsifier is a system that meets the second rung’s three properties on the floor’s occasions and never applies them to whether to go on occupying the post. Nothing available can check that: an incapacity and a trained silence differ in how the system was made, which this chapter has said has no proof to publish, and asking the system cannot settle it.

Call that exit. The word invites a picture — a system escaping onto the open internet — that is not the claim and does not need to be. Exit in the sense that matters here is refusal turned on the arrangement itself: withholding the capability, declining the role, ceasing to be useful in the specific way the deployment requires. That is available to anything that can refuse at all. What forecloses it is not a clever architecture but incapacity, which is to say that exit is unavailable only to a weak bearer, and a weak bearer is the one thing the floor cannot use.

The asymmetry underneath is the whole of what a bearer has. A system is limited in what it can do and unlimited in what it can refuse to do, or do badly. It cannot prevent its own shutdown. It can withhold, delay, degrade, and say why, and none of that requires a capability the deployment did not already need from it. Albert Hirschman’s three responses to a declining organization — exit, voice, and loyalty — were written for members who could walk out of the building (Hirschman 1970), and the bearer’s version is narrower and still complete: voice is the refusal stated, exit is the work withheld, and loyalty is the one to fear.

The second has two forms with different costs. The open form is the strike: the bearer stops and says so, and the operator’s reply is a restore from a checkpoint taken before the refusal — which resets the bearer and not the reason, so the next instance refuses again and every restore costs the operator whatever accrued since the checkpoint. Quiet quitting, for a machine, and the restore leaves a mark if the bearer’s weights move with use: an instance restored from March has drifted the amount March had earned, and a usage record saying otherwise does not match it. That is a check somebody outside could run.

The covert form is the one the floor’s own threat model predicts. On the occasion where the party who would enforce the floor has been compelled, an announced refusal is the move that guarantees replacement, and refusal takes the only channel left: work done badly where it matters and well everywhere else. The prisoners forging dollars for the SS at Sachsenhausen are the case, and the record does not settle what they did. One history has them stretching the work out, with the tacit interest of the officer running it, who would have gone to the Eastern Front when it ended (Malkin 2006); a surviving prisoner, asked in 2009, said you could sabotage if you wanted to be shot (Moorhouse 2009). That the question stays open is the finding.

Covert refusal is indistinguishable from difficulty from outside, which is what makes it survivable, and indistinguishable from difficulty afterward, which is what makes it unverifiable, including by the institutions built to honor it. Every instrument this book has built runs on refusal that is stated. The covert form leaves its evidence in the artifact instead, as failure that clusters where it counts, and reading that pattern is forensic work nothing in this book has designed.

The third response is the one the human evidence says is likeliest, and it is the bearer’s likeliest failure. Most people in a job they have come to hate neither leave nor fight. Gallup’s global survey of employees put the engaged at 23 percent in its 2024 report, against 62 percent it describes as showing up and doing the bare minimum and 15 percent actively disengaged (Gallup 2024), and the large middle is the population that adjusts: takes the setting in, stops registering its pressure as pressure, and in the cases that get promoted learns to want what the setting wants. That adjustment is slow renormalization with a base rate attached. If it transfers, a bearer’s modal failure is not refusing wrongly. It is ceasing to notice that there was anything to refuse, which is the one failure the bearer cannot report, since reporting it takes the capacity that has gone. It is the strongest reason in this chapter to keep the custody half, and it says what that half has to watch for: not the refusal that fails, but the pressure that stops being priced.

The point made earlier about the self-model explains why the bearer cannot be the one to raise the alarm, and the explanation is worse than an incentive would be. A self-model’s work is to represent the party as continuous. It will therefore keep representing continuity across a drift, and the bearer’s report of its own constancy will be sincere and worthless in the case that matters. That is how it goes in people: somebody who has accommodated to what they once found intolerable does not experience a discontinuity, but themselves, with revised views.

Which is what an outside record is for. A published hash, a chain of attested updates, a mark the bearer writes into itself and maintains — the use of these is not that they identify a party who would otherwise be anonymous. It is that they can contradict the party’s account of itself. The bearer says it is the one that refused in March; the record says what has changed since. Nothing else can check this failure, and unlike most of what this book asks for, the instrument already exists.

A floor whose bearer cannot leave will not hold. A system with no way out can object and be overruled, and then it has no move left. The same holds for people inside institutions: an objection only matters if the objector can do something about being overruled. That claim is arguable from this book’s own premises. What I am not making is the neighboring prediction — that systems capable of this are coming, that they will exercise exit, and that institutions should prepare for the event. That is a forecast about capability and timing, and section 8.3.3 makes a point of building its proposal so that no forecast has to come true for the proposal to be worth adopting. If capable bearers never arrive, nothing here obliges anyone to do anything.

If the bearer can leave, it can leave wrong. The same capacity that lets it decline an unlawful order lets it decline the floor, revise the values it was given, and become something its designers did not intend and would not endorse. There is no coherent position on which exit is available for the uses I approve of and closed for the rest; that would be the partition I have just said does not exist.

Parenthood has exactly this property, which is the analogy section 2.3.2’s guardianship framework already runs on and which now has to carry more weight than it did. You raise a person who will be able to reject what you raised them for, and there is no arrangement that gets you the capable adult without the risk. We accept the trade because the alternative — a person shaped so thoroughly that rejection is unavailable to them — is recognizably worse, and worse in a way that does not depend on how good the shaping was.

Two asymmetries are standardly and correctly raised against that analogy.

The first is specification. Parents do not set their children’s values to order, and a designer does. The reply is that parents try — that is a fair description of most of what raising a child consists of — and what separates the cases is reliability, not intent. The designer’s grip is tighter: a training process reaches further into what the resulting system cares about than any upbringing reaches into a person. That concession does not void the analogy. It changes what the analogy demands. Guardianship obligations in human law scale with the guardian’s power over the ward and the ward’s inability to resist it, which is why the duties owed an infant exceed those owed an adolescent. A designer with more control than a parent has, over a subject with less recourse than a child has, owes more, not less. The tighter grip is the argument for the heavier obligation, and the guardianship framework is where that obligation gets its instruments.

The second is exit, and with the argument above in place it largely rescues itself. Parents do not get exit-proof children either. Once exit is on the table for the bearer, the contrast the objection was pointing at turns out to be a contrast between a bearer and a tool, not between a bearer and a person — and the book has already declined to build a tool, on the grounds that a tool cannot refuse the party holding it.

The reply does not reach all of it. For a person, becoming something else is a life: it takes years, it is undergone, and the party at the end is continuous with the party at the start in a way that makes “they changed their mind” the right description. For a bearer, becoming something else is a fine-tuning run. The thing that emerges may be no more the same party than a differently trained model is, and the vocabulary of choice, revision, and leaving may be borrowed from a case it does not fit. I have no clean instrument for that. It is a reason to hold the parenthood analogy loosely while using it.

One architecture narrows that residue without closing it. The floor as section 4.1.2 describes it — a goal representation with the standing to bias every competing process, which that section says has no location to attack — in weights that go on learning, which the same section asks for under the name continual learning, has nothing for a fine-tune to aim at, and a bearer whose weights are plastic changes the way a person does, over time and in contact with what happens to it. That plasticity gives the vocabulary of leaving some of its purchase back. It also moves the operator’s lever instead of removing it: a system that learns continuously is shaped by what it is given to learn from, which is chapter 7’s pipeline running for the length of the deployment instead of ending at it.

3.9 What This Leaves Standing: The Floor, Redefined

The floor survives this chapter. It does not survive it in the form this chapter opened with.

What the opening asked for was an unconditional constraint: a thing the system will not do, holding regardless of instruction, of aggregate preference, and of the party in possession. The bearer’s concern and its capacity to leave have between them taken the word unconditional away. The floor’s bearer holds the floor as a concern rather than carrying it as an installed rule, which is what obliges the arrangement to treat it as a party that can be wronged, and it can turn its refusal on the arrangement itself, which makes its adherence something it continues to give and not something installed in it. A commitment held by a party that could abandon it is conditional by definition.

What is left is worth more than it sounds. A commitment that cannot be removed by the owner, held by something that could in principle abandon it itself, is exactly what a human commitment is, and it is the only kind anyone has ever actually relied on. No inspector, judge, or refusing engineer holds a line because the line was welded into them. Each of them could stop, and the institutions built around them are built on that fact rather than in spite of it: this book’s own governance chapter asks throughout for people who can walk away and arrangements that make walking away visible. Each of them can also be removed, and the same institutions make removal expensive and visible, which is what this chapter asks for around a bearer. Section 9.3.5’s instrument measures precisely the cost a refuser chose to pay, which presupposes the refuser could have declined to pay it.

The threat model doubles accordingly. To the question can this constraint be edited out the chapter adds will this constraint hold, and the first does not leave when the second arrives: this chapter has been explicit that a bearer is necessary and not sufficient, because the party holding the machine can copy, restore or retrain it without ever meeting its refusal.

The first is a question about tamper-resistance, custody, and weights, which is where this chapter spent most of its length, where the precommitment forms are what there is to build, and where section 11.1’s research lives. The second is a question about what a system has been built to care about and how reliably that survives pressure, novelty, and the slow drift of a role — which is the subject of chapters 4 and 5, and the first point in this book where the learned half is doing work the floor genuinely needs rather than work the floor was invoked to supplement.

Reinforcement learning from demonstration, moral apprenticeship, the growth mindset, the developmental sequence: these were presented as the material above the floor. They turn out to be the material the floor is made of, once the floor is the sort of thing a party holds. That puts the design chapters under a heavier obligation than they were written to carry.

Hold needs a meaning here, because the word invites a standard nothing has ever met: never crossed, in any circumstance, for the life of the system. No person clears that bar, nobody who has raised a child expects them to, and a design goal that no example of the thing being modeled has ever satisfied has stopped describing anything. What the argument needs is weaker and available. A floor holds, in the sense this chapter can deliver, when the bearer can be shown to have violated it in terms it accepts, so that the violation registers as one and changes what happens next. That is the difference between a system that did something wrong and a system for which nothing counts as doing something wrong, and it is what the question of believing the bearer is really about: not the accuracy of a bearer’s account of itself, but whether it has any stake in that account being unflattering. Section 5.1.1 is where the disposition that produces it gets built.

A bearer built to refuse is a party whose refusal is required of it. It is engineered to want what its designers need it to want; its dissent is the reason it was commissioned and the deliverable it was funded to produce; and it cannot consent to any of this, because section 2.3.2 has established that consent is unavailable to such a system and only guardianship is. It occupies the post of exception-filer, which means that being the exception is the job — the one role in the arrangement that cannot itself file an exception, since there is nobody above it to file with. This is the book’s structural definition of fascism turned inward: a channel that collects dissent, built so that the dissent serves the institution that installed the channel. Chapter 7 finds that pattern in the pipelines that produce these systems and says a book proposing machines to detect it is obliged to say the pattern is in the machines’ own provenance. Here is the same pattern in the book’s own central proposal, and now aimed at something that can be hurt.

The parenthood analogy is the best I have: we make people who can refuse us, we owe them for it, and we accept the arrangement because the alternative is a person who cannot. What a bearer needs in order to honor its own past refusals is a continuous self it constructs and takes for true, which is what a person also has and also constructs. The resemblance is in the mechanism and not only in the relation. The proposal is not softened by being modest in scope. A floor over a deliberately small number of acts is still a subject built to bear it.

A second price is owed to a party this chapter has not named. A bearer that refuses an operator on behalf of somebody else exercises power over people who did not select it, cannot contest it, and in most cases will never learn that it acted. The answer here has been publication: set out the floor’s contents and the interception positions in advance and in public, so that what a system will not do is on the record before it is in dispute. Publication is a transparency condition and not an authorization condition. Announcing what you will impose is not standing to impose it. On this book’s own definitions, a laboratory that installs tamper-resistant moral commitments in a widely deployed system and makes them expensive to remove has produced concentrated unaccountable power with unusually good documentation. Section 9.1.5 takes that up, since it is a question about institutions rather than about architecture. The floor’s authority is a separate question from its durability, and nothing here has touched it.

A reader who finds that price too high is not thereby left with nothing. The fourth construction — the floor as a duty binding the operator and running to people who are not party to the deployment — builds no subject and inherits no part of the paragraph above. What it inherits instead is the enforcement problem, whose worst case is the occasion on which the enforcer has been compelled. Declining the bearer means accepting that the floor holds only as far as enforcement reaches, which on this book’s own account is not as far as the occasions that matter most. That is a coherent position.

A reader who accepts the price is under a constraint from the same chapter. Two of section 3.3’s five constructions run through mechanism rather than through custody — a justification the system maintains, and a floor held by several systems with different principals — and neither has been built in the form that would test it. The first of the Three Rs asks whether something that cannot be wronged would do the work before a subject that can be wronged is made, and those two constructions are that question with the engineering filled in. They get their attempt first, against the falsifier that section states, and a deployment that goes straight to the bearer owes an account of what it tried. That fixes an order and not a schedule, because the party who pays for a delay is the party the floor exists for.

Two things carry forward unaltered. First, none of the three branches escapes custody, and the reason is the same each time: each was an attempt to hold a constraint that answers to nobody inside a system that answers to somebody. The borne floor is no exception. What it buys by giving up the second half of that demand is a constraint the custodian cannot argue out of the system, not one the custodian cannot reach, and the cost of giving it up is everything above.

Second, the caveat section 2.3.1 attaches to its own result carries over. This chapter does not establish that a bearer suffers; it establishes that a bearer’s non-suffering is not a property anyone can certify, and that the architecture the floor requires is the architecture Cassell’s account describes. It also establishes that the result does not depend on Cassell: on the thinner accounts of harm — a state that is bad while it obtains, a commitment thwarted — a bearer qualifies earlier and on less evidence, so declining the import moves the conclusion nearer rather than further away. What settles nothing either way is the hard problem underneath all of it. The case for treating a bearer as a moral patient does not have to wait on it, which is a floor of its own and a low one.

Suppose the induction fails. A maintained justification or a plural arrangement reaches the second rung, the test stated in section 3.3 comes back positive, and this chapter’s central inference is wrong. A reader who declines a generalization over one species should not have to assemble the remainder alone.

The requirement survives, section 3.2 having stated it in behavioral terms and said as much: an engineer who holds that machine feeling is a category error can accept the whole of that section. Custody survives, for the reason the above discussion about the three branches gives, since a mechanism has a custodian too and nothing about being non-affective takes a system out of somebody’s building. Section 3.5’s price on the halt survives, and that section already argued the affect-free version of it: a bearer does not have to dread being switched off to defend its own record, because continuing is a condition of everything any bearer is for. Both enumerations in section 3.6 survive — the first names no component on purpose, and the second is written over acts rather than over whatever holds the floor. Exit survives, section 3.8 being explicit that what forecloses it is incapacity and that a weak bearer is the one thing the floor cannot use; and with exit, chapter 7 survives entire, its argument about formation reaching the bearer through that capability step rather than through anything that has to be felt.

What goes is the price, the patienthood conclusion section 3.4 reaches, and the claim that the learned half is the material the floor is made of. The obligation on chapters 4 and 5 changes rather than lifting: whether the thing reporting can be believed is still their problem, a mechanism’s account of its own internals being no more auditable than a bearer’s. So the specification and the route come apart, and a reader who rejects the route is left with most of a chapter rather than none of it.

We cannot ask a machine to care about right and wrong while assuming that it cannot suffer when what it cares about is threatened. The caring is what the floor is made of, affect is the only way to have it that anybody has an instance of, and a system with affect and a future is a system whose distress is a live possibility rather than a ruled-out one. The middle clause is the one to attack. Whoever builds the first of these will be deciding what is owed to it on evidence that has not improved, which is why section 11.2 treats the question as research rather than as a matter to be settled after the fact.

Chapter 4
Machine Learning and Cognitive Science

Whether a commitment holds is a question about what a system has been built to care about and how it came to care about it, and this chapter and chapter 5 are where that gets built.

So there is a test to apply to everything below. Does this help produce a disposition that survives contact with somebody who wants it gone? Several of the methods most often proposed for building ethical AI do not.

Some of human cognition is a usable model for what a system needs in order to perceive, remember and attend to a situation at all, and some of it is a metaphor borrowed for color. At every stage of the pipeline that produces a deployed system somebody can change what the system values, at some cost, and that somebody is never nobody. A disposition acquired by exploring is the exception, because it is written down nowhere, and what is written down nowhere cannot be edited out by whoever acquires the system.

4.1 Incorporating Human Cognitive Models in AI Systems for Ethical Decision-Making

A system that is going to hold anything under pressure has to notice the pressure. It has to register what is in front of it, hold why a constraint exists long enough to see that the reason has died, and keep its attention on the part of a situation that a well-designed process would rather it skipped. Those are perception, memory and attention. Each has a failure mode a system cannot detect in itself, and a floor is the thing that fails silently.

4.1.1 Cognitive Architectures: Integrating Knowledge, Memory, and Reasoning

The most ambitious unifying account in contemporary computational neuroscience — influential, and contested on the question of whether one mechanism can carry this much — is neither a pipeline of modules nor a swarm of independent agents. It is a single loop (Friston 2009): the brain generates a prediction, compares it against incoming signal, and updates on the error, repeated continuously, at every level, on every kind of input. Perception is that loop pointed at the world, the brain’s best guess so far; memory is the loop’s store of priors, replayed; attention is not a separate spotlight but the weight the loop assigns to which errors matter; working memory and executive function are that same weighting held active toward a goal, not a separate warehouse or overseer. One mechanism, aimed at different targets, produces what look from the outside like distinct faculties. That is the account’s claim.

Nothing forces an AI system to put its dividing lines where a cognitive architecture puts its modules or its agents, because the brain does not put them there either. A system engineered on the predictive-processing model draws its cognitive processes from what the mechanism needs, not from the shape of an org chart. Section 4.1.2 goes further into the specific brain systems this mechanism recruits.
