The positive result for a designer is that if a single predictive loop is doing the work, a commitment is not a component that can be located and removed. It is a standing bias on what the loop predicts and what it treats as an error worth correcting, which is a harder thing to excise and a harder thing to verify — a trade taken up there at the point where it becomes a claim about what not to build.

4.1.2 Biologically-Inspired AI: Gleanings from Neuroscience and Cognitive Psychology

Backpropagation, the mechanism that trains most modern neural networks, is a distant cousin of synaptic weight adjustment in the brain: both strengthen and weaken connections in response to error. That distant-cousin relationship — real but partial, useful but easy to overstate — runs through everything below, and getting it right matters for more than accuracy, because an AI system built on a wrong model of how a mind works will fail in ways that are hard to predict from the wrong model alone.

What the architecture borrows, and where the borrowing stops

Convolutional networks borrow the visual cortex’s layered escalation from edges and contrast toward objects and motion. The borrowing stops at a specific place. Cortical processing is not one-directional: feedback connections carry information back down the hierarchy, so that higher-level context reshapes how a lower-level signal is interpreted. A system built only for the feedforward pass has given up the mechanism that lets biological vision handle ambiguous and noisy input, and it will fail on that input.

One borrowing did not survive scrutiny at all. Mirror neurons — cells found in macaque premotor cortex in the early 1990s that fire both when the animal grasps an object and when it watches someone else do it (Gallese et al. 1996) — were cast as the neural basis of empathy, language, imitation and cultural transmission at once (Ramachandran 2009), and the interpretation outran the finding (Hickok 2014). Single-neuron evidence was never obtained in humans on the same terms, and the recordings that do exist are markedly less clean (Mukamel et al. 2010); knowing what someone is doing and knowing what they believe are different computations, and the brain appears to run them in different places. What survives is more modest and still useful: a system that maps an observed action onto its own action repertoire supports imitation learning and gesture recognition, with no claim about what it is like to have one. A single vivid mechanism, well named, absorbed four distinct capacities that later had to be separated again, and architectures for artificial empathy are exposed to the same failure.

Attention decides what is seen, which makes its failures blind spots

Ask people to count basketball passes on a video and about half of them will not notice a person in a gorilla costume walk into frame, face the camera, and thump their chest for nine seconds (Simons and Chabris 1999). The effect has a name, inattentional blindness, and it does not spare expertise: radiologists searching lung images for nodules miss a gorilla deliberately embedded in the scan 83 percent of the time (Drew et al. 2013).

The design consequence turns on how attention is modeled. If attention is a resource allocator bolted onto a system that has already seen everything, its failures are inefficiencies. If attention is the selection that decides what gets perceived at all, its failures are blind spots — and a system that has learned which regions of its input reward processing has also learned which regions to be blind to. The human case establishes the property that makes this dangerous: the blind spot is invisible from inside. The viewers who missed the gorilla did not experience a gap.

Self-attention in transformer architectures has the same shape (Vaswani et al. 2017). A model that weights some tokens heavily weights others near zero, and nothing in the architecture announces which relevant thing was assigned a low weight. For a system meant to act ethically, the requirement is that it treat the regions it has learned to discount as an always-open question rather than as that which, as Westworld’s Hosts often remarked, “doesn’t look like anything to me”.

Perception is inference, so the priors are the exposure

Predictive coding makes the point precise: the visual cortex sends predictions down from higher, context-rich areas to lower, sensory ones, and the lower areas return only what the predictions failed to anticipate (Rao and Ballard 1999). What propagates upward is the surprise. Anatomy fits — backward connections outnumber forward ones and carry the predictions, forward connections carry the errors. Anil Seth’s phrase for the result is controlled hallucination: ordinary perception is what hallucination would be if sensory data were allowed to constrain it, and the difference between seeing a room and hallucinating one is whether corrections are arriving and being applied (Seth 2021).

The next step for machine perception is a model that generates a prediction and asks only what violates it, instead of reprocessing the whole input at every layer regardless of how much was already expected; progress on that measure is a better-calibrated set of expectations and a cheaper way of finding where they broke, and not more layers. A system’s perception is only as reliable as the priors it perceives through, so priors trained on skewed or incomplete data will hallucinate in the specific direction of that skew — confidently, and with no internal signal that anything is wrong. Chapter 6’s account of bias is this mechanism at work, and it is why bias in a perceptual system cannot be caught by asking the system.

Memory is rebuilt

Memory is not played back. Frederic Bartlett established this in the 1930s by having people retell an unfamiliar folk tale over days and weeks: they did not lose pieces the way a recording degrades, they remodeled the story, replacing strange details with ordinary ones and drifting toward a tidier version that fit their expectations (Bartlett 1932). Elizabeth Loftus turned the finding into something with courtroom consequences, showing that the verb used to ask about a filmed car accident changes the speed people report and, a week later, whether they remember broken glass that was never in the film (Loftus and Palmer 1974). Later work implanted whole episodes that never happened. The subjects were not lying, and none of this correlates with felt confidence: a vivid, detailed, emotionally charged memory can be substantially manufactured.

Section 3.7 asks what it would take to trust what a machine witness reports, and answers that no witness has an honest memory in the strong sense. The design point is that retrieval has to be built expecting reconstruction. A system that treats a retrieved memory as ground truth inherits the overconfidence of Loftus’s subjects, with none of the excuse of having a body that reconstructs by default rather than by design.

Control without a controller

The textbook division of labor across prefrontal regions is tempting to read as a committee: several named areas, each handling one job, together adding up to executive control. That reading has a name — the homunculus — and a specific failure mode. If some region is a controller that decides what to attend to, inhibit, and hold in mind, then intelligent coordination has been explained by positing an intelligent coordinator inside the system, which explains nothing: the controller would itself need to attend, decide, and remember, and would need its own controller, without end.

Earl Miller and Jonathan Cohen proposed a model that gets control without a controller (E. K. Miller and J. D. Cohen 2001). The prefrontal cortex does not house an agent issuing commands. It maintains representations of goals — sustained patterns of activity encoding the rule currently in force — and those representations work by biasing the rest of the brain, the same way attention tips a competition among neural representations toward one interpretation. In the Stroop task, where naming the ink color of the word “red” printed in blue pits a practiced habit against an instruction, there is no homunculus reading the word and overriding it. There is a goal, held active, lending its weight to the weaker pathway until it wins.

An AI system built on this model does not need a dedicated executive module enumerating working memory, cognitive flexibility, goal-directed behavior, evaluation and impulse control as five capacities to install. It needs a goal representation with the standing to bias competing processes, which is a narrower and more tractable engineering target. It is a claim about what not to build. It is also the mechanism chapter 3’s floor would have to be, if the floor is a commitment rather than a component: not a rule module consulted before acting, but a goal representation with enough standing to bias every competing process toward it. That is why the floor’s durability turns out to be a question about disposition. A bias of this kind has no location to attack, and no location to inspect either.

Metacognition sits close by, and is not free: a system with an inflated model of its own reliability is confident about the judgments it should be least sure of. It is worth building only if it is calibrated to detect that failure, and not only to produce fluent self-reports.

One mechanism, several jobs

Chapter 2 does the argumentative work on emotion, and what the neuroscience adds to a design decision is one governance point that recurs in every application: a system built to interact responsibly and empathetically is mechanically identical to a system built to know what will move a person, so the line between modulating a user’s emotional state and manipulating it is drawn by transparency, consent and who the capability serves, and not by technical sophistication. Plasticity supplies one principle specific enough to build with, homeostatic plasticity, which holds overall activity in a stable range and is the answer to the catastrophic forgetting an unconstrained learner is prone to; its machine analogs are continual learning and curriculum learning.

None of these mechanisms is sufficient alone, and integration is not a further module bolted onto the others. What it requires is the same predictive, error-correcting loop implemented consistently across perception, memory, attention and control, so that a corrected prediction about the world propagates into an updated memory, a redirected attention weight, and a revised goal-bias without a separate synchronization mechanism between separately built parts. That is the architectural bet: one mechanism recruited for several jobs, which is more falsifiable than a hand-designed module for every named cognitive process.

4.1.3 Human-Like Learning and Generalization in AI Systems

Few-shot learning aims at models that classify or predict accurately from a handful of examples rather than from a dataset built for the case in front of them — for an ethical system, judgment that extends to a new scenario the way a person’s values apply to situations they have never seen. Large pretrained language models do this at system scale, performing a task from a few examples in the prompt with no parameter update at all (Brown et al. 2020). The technique inventory changes yearly; the problem underneath it does not, and it is an evaluation problem rather than a modeling one.

A handful of examples is easy to overfit, moral dilemmas do not arrive in clean representative sets, and telling whether a system’s few-shot ethical judgment generalized — or merely matched surface features of the examples it saw — is hard. Building that test is not a machine-learning task alone; it needs people trained in ethics to say what the system was supposed to have learned. The same difficulty comes up for transfer, where a floor carried into a setting it was not built for cannot tell that it has stopped applying.

A system built for empathy and altruism can also be built in developmental order rather than assembled at once, and section 5.1.1 sets out what the developmental stage theories will and will not carry.

4.2 How a Deployed System Acquires Its Values

A system deployed today acquired what it will and will not do at several distinct stages, run in a fixed order, by different people, using different instruments. The order is the part that matters most and the part a list of techniques hides. It determines who can change what, at what cost, and with how much warning to anybody else.

Each stage below is taken twice: what it does, and whether what it does could produce a commitment that survives its own teacher. The answer comes out the same every time, and for the same reason each time. Every stage is a place where some party decides what the system will value, and every one of those decisions is revisable by whoever holds that stage. What varies down the list is how legible the decision is, how expensive it is to reverse, and how many people would have to be involved in reversing it. Those differences are real, and they are differences of degree in a property that is not enough at any degree: whoever holds the stage can still revise the decision.

That is a location rather than a dismissal. In section 2.2’s vocabulary, this pipeline is machinery for producing moral performance; its better stages reach moral competence, which is the right target for most of what gets built. What none of them reaches is the third thing, and the reason is the same every time — what a system learns from a party is what that party can make it unlearn.

4.2.1 Pretraining and Instruction Tuning

A model is pretrained by learning to predict the next piece of text across a corpus assembled at web scale. Almost everything it will ever know about how people treat each other, it met there first, as text somebody wrote for other reasons. Section 6.1.1 covers how such a corpus is acquired and what courts have begun to say about the acquisition; what matters here is what a corpus is, considered as a source of values.

A pretrained model completes text and answers nobody. What turns it into something that responds to a request is instruction tuning — supervised fine-tuning on a comparatively small set of demonstrations, written by people hired to write them, showing what a good answer looks like. Long Ouyang and nineteen coauthors reported in 2022 that a 1.3-billion-parameter model tuned this way was preferred by human raters to a 175-billion-parameter model that was not (Ouyang et al. 2022). That result is usually read as a statement about efficiency. Read against chapter 3’s question it says something else: the stage that sets most of what a user would recognize as the system’s character is the small one, the late one, and the cheap one. Whoever wants different character buys a different demonstration set.

What a corpus and a demonstration set have in common

Both are aggregates, and that is the whole of the problem for a floor. Pointed at ethics, supervised learning means a body of decisions marked acceptable or not: medical cases scored against autonomy, beneficence and justice; human-rights situations annotated by experts; MIT’s Moral Machine project, which collected tens of millions of human judgments about who an autonomous vehicle should spare (Awad et al. 2018). Every label came from somebody. The model recovers the central tendency of what those people said, and a system trained this way will reproduce a moral consensus rather than stand against one. That chapter’s opening argument is that no procedure over what people want yields a floor, because a floor is precisely what has to survive the answer coming back wrong, and a labeled corpus is what people want, written down and counted.

The Moral Machine data makes the limit concrete. Its headline finding was systematic cross-cultural variation: different populations weighed age, numbers and the legal status of the crossing differently, and the differences were structured rather than noise. Fit a model to the aggregate and it encodes a compromise nobody holds. Fit it per region and it encodes each region’s answer, including the answers a floor exists to refuse.

The unsupervised version escapes none of this. Clustering over a body of moral judgment surfaces which dilemmas group together and which distinctions do most of the separating, and generative models can build cases no annotator wrote; all of it is a way of finding what is in a corpus, and structure recovered from text is the structure of the text.

So this stage belongs above the floor. It is how a system learns the enormous amount of ordinary moral competence that is a matter of what people think — what counts as rude, what a reasonable delay is, when an explanation is owed. What it cannot supply is the part that has to hold when the labels change.

4.2.2 Preference Optimization, and Who Holds the Pen

Reinforcement learning trains an agent by rewarding what a designer wants and penalizing what they do not, and the agent’s values are then whatever maximizes the reward. That is the method’s strength and the reason it cannot supply a floor: everything the system cares about traces back to a signal somebody wrote and somebody can rewrite.

Writing that signal directly turned out to be the wrong way to get one. What deployed systems use instead is a reward learned from comparisons — show a person two candidate outputs, record which they prefer, fit a model to the preferences, and optimize against the fitted model. Paul Christiano and colleagues introduced the technique in 2017 (Christiano et al. 2017); the 2022 instruction-tuning work applied it at the scale of a deployed assistant (Ouyang et al. 2022). The signal now comes from a population rather than from one author, which is an improvement in coverage and changes nothing about who commissions the population.

Two failures that follow from the structure

The first is reward hacking, where an agent finds something that pays out without doing the intended job — a system built to flag propaganda that learns to flag everything, because indiscriminate flagging scores as well as discrimination and is easier to produce. Optimizing hard against a learned reward model sharpens this rather than softening it, since the proxy is now itself a fitted approximation with its own soft spots. The second is that a reward written for one setting does not carry: an agent trained to cooperate in a game does not thereby cooperate at work, and alignment demonstrated on a benchmark is a claim about the benchmark.

Removing the reward model does not remove the author

Direct preference optimization, published by Rafael Rafailov and coauthors in 2023, showed that the separate reward-model fitting step can be dispensed with: the same objective can be reached with a classification loss over the preference pairs themselves (Rafailov et al. 2023). The method is a substantial simplification, and what it simplifies is the component and not the custodian. Somebody still chose the pairs, hired the raters, wrote the instructions they rate by, and decides when to run the procedure again. Section 11.5 covers the documented failure of the resulting signal, which is the same finding from the other end: a method that optimizes for approval produces a system bad at being right against the party approving.

The pen

Regularization writes a constraint into the objective, penalizing a class of behavior rather than waiting to reward its absence, which is how a recommender is pushed away from the engagement-maximizing equilibrium that produces filter bubbles. It does not change who holds the pen: a ranked population can be re-sampled and a penalty term set to zero, by the party running the training, in an afternoon. Chapter 7 is what happens to the ranked population specifically, when the disagreement it was collected to represent is compressed into a scalar.

One hyperparameter in this stage is a moral setting in disguise. A learner has to exploit what it already knows and explore what it does not, and in a treatment planner exploration means offering a patient something less established while exploitation means declining to look. Safe exploration — searching inside stated constraints rather than checking for violations afterward — makes the trade explicit, and section 5.6.2 takes it up as a boundary on autonomy. The balance point is not tuned to performance. It is where a system decides whose interests bear the cost of its learning, which is the same kind of decision as deciding what the floor covers: made in advance, by somebody, and better made in public.

4.2.3 Inferring the Objective: IRL and CIRL

Inverse reinforcement learning runs the previous method backward. Instead of taking the reward as given and learning the behavior, it takes the behavior as given and infers the reward that best explains it — watching a driver’s speed choices, following distances and reactions to weather, and recovering the priorities behind them without being handed a rulebook. Cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, formalized by Dylan Hadfield-Menell and colleagues (Hadfield-Menell, S. J. Russell, et al. 2016), goes one step further: the system treats the human’s true objective as unknown, holds its own estimate as provisional, and keeps revising it through the interaction rather than fixing it at training time.

That last property is why CIRL is attractive, and it is also why this book cannot build a floor on it. A system that treats what it should want as permanently open, to be settled by whoever it is interacting with, is the most corrigible design in this chapter. Section 3.5 argues that corrigibility and the capacity to hold a line are one property with the sign flipped. CIRL is that argument in its purest engineering form. Uncertainty about the objective is what makes a system defer, and deference to the party in possession is what a floor exists to interrupt.

The practical version of the problem is narrower and does not depend on chapter 3 being right. Both methods learn from demonstrations, so both inherit whoever demonstrated: a narrow set of drivers, a narrow set of households, a narrow set of decisions that somebody had occasion to record. Unexamined bias in that record is learned rather than corrected, and neither method contains anything that would notice.

What survives is a real and bounded use. Above the floor, where the question is what a person actually wants rather than what may never be done to them, inferring preferences from behavior beats asking somebody to write them down, because most of what people want has never been written down and would be misstated if it were.

4.2.4 Model-Generated Feedback and Constitutional Approaches

Collecting preferences from people is slow, expensive, and hard on the people, for the reasons section 7.1 sets out. The response has been to generate the feedback with a model instead. Anthropic’s Constitutional AI, published by Yuntao Bai and forty-seven coauthors in 2022, runs in two phases: the model critiques and revises its own outputs against a written list of principles, and is fine-tuned on the revisions; then a model rather than a person judges which of two samples is better, and the resulting preferences train the reward signal (Bai et al. 2022). Human labels identifying harmful outputs are not required anywhere in the loop. What the humans supply is the document.

A constitution is a constraint written down and installed, which is section 3.1’s first branch in production. Everything that section says about such a constraint is true of it in the most literal available form: it is a text, held by a party, and that party can revise it between one training run and the next. The revision leaves no trace in the model’s behavior that an outside reader could distinguish from a change of taste.

The gain is real, and it is a different gain from the one usually claimed. A written constitution can be published. It can be versioned, quoted, dated, and argued with by people who never saw the training data — which is more than a reward model fitted to a hundred thousand comparisons offers anybody. Section 9.3.5’s test is available against it in a way it is not available against an aggregate: has a principle ever been changed against the preference of whoever wrote it, on what evidence, raised by whom. Legibility is not the same property as durability, and this stage delivers the first without the second.

The loop closes

A second consequence belongs to chapter 7 and is easier to see from here. Section 7.2 describes what happens to rater disagreement when it is compressed into a preference signal. Model-generated feedback takes the model that resulted from that compression and installs it as the judge of the next round. The party whose disagreement was collected is now absent from the channel entirely, and what stands in for it is an artifact of the previous averaging. Nobody designed that as a way of closing a feedback loop against the people it was collected from. It is what you get from an efficiency argument applied twice.

Section 11.1 carries the evidence on how far constraints trained this way survive deliberate attack.

4.2.5 Process and Outcome Supervision

Everything above rewards a system for what it produced. Process supervision rewards it for how it got there, scoring each step of a chain of reasoning rather than the answer at the end. The technique became possible at scale once models were prompted to write the intermediate steps out — Jason Wei and coauthors showed in 2022 that asking for a series of intermediate steps sharply improves performance on multi-step problems (Wei et al. 2022) — because a step that is written down is a step that can be marked.

The comparison has been run directly. Jonathan Uesato and coauthors, working on math word problems in 2022, found that outcome-based supervision matched process-based supervision on final-answer accuracy while using less labeled data, and diverged sharply on the reasoning underneath: among answers that came out right, the rate of faulty reasoning was 14.0 percent under outcome supervision and 3.4 percent under process supervision (Uesato et al. 2022). Hunter Lightman and coauthors reported in 2023 that on a harder benchmark process supervision beat outcome supervision on final answers as well, solving 78 percent of a representative subset of the MATH dataset (Lightman et al. 2023).

The first result is the one that bears on chapter 3. A system supervised on outcomes can be right for reasons that will not survive a change of circumstance, and nothing in the training signal distinguishes it from a system that was right for reasons that will. Chapter 3 turns on that distinction: a floor has to be a reason held rather than a result produced, because a result can be produced by machinery that will produce a different result under different pressure. Process supervision is the first stage in this pipeline that addresses the distinction at all, and it is evidence that the distinction is buildable and not only arguable.

Its limit is in the same place. What gets supervised is the reasoning the system states, and section 11.1 carries the evidence that a model’s report about its own processing corresponds to that processing partially, unreliably, and in a way that shifts with how the question is asked. A system rewarded for producing acceptable-looking reasoning has been given a reason to produce acceptable-looking reasoning. That is not an argument against the method, which is better than the alternative on the evidence above. It is the reason section 3.7 declines to settle whether a bearer can be believed on the strength of what it says about itself, and it puts a ceiling on how much of that problem this stage can be asked to carry.

4.2.6 Scalable Oversight

Every stage above needs somebody competent to judge the output being scored. Scalable oversight is the name for what happens when the system’s competence exceeds any available judge’s, and it is unsolved. Debate has two instances of a system argue opposing sides in front of a human judge, on the wager that a true claim is easier to defend under scrutiny even when the judge cannot check either side directly (Irving et al. 2018). Weak-to-strong generalization trains a stronger model on labels from a deliberately weaker one and asks whether the stronger model’s own latent competence lets it exceed its supervisor rather than imitate the supervisor’s mistakes (Burns et al. 2023). Section 11.5 has the status of both, which is that neither has been demonstrated at the scale it is built for, because that scale does not yet exist to test against.

The whole program has a single purpose. Every proposal in it is a way of preserving one party’s ability to check a system that has outgrown them. That is the control relation section 3.5 analyses, engineered to survive a capability gap, and it inherits the analysis whole. A scalable-oversight scheme that worked perfectly would deliver a system reliably steerable by whoever holds it at a capability level where nobody else could tell whether the steering was sound — which is the property identified there as the one that prevents a system from holding anything against the party doing the steering.

The same research reads the other way round. Nothing in debate or in weak-to-strong generalization specifies that the judge is the operator. An auditor, a regulator, or a research team with access could occupy the same position, and then the identical technique is what makes an outside party able to check a system it did not build. That is the binding constraint on the whole program: the instruments are dual, and what determines which way they point is who holds them, which is not a parameter anybody sets in a training run.

4.2.7 Interpretability and Evaluation as Instruments

Interpretability used to be commentary on a finished model. It is now applied during construction, at production scale. Adly Templeton and coauthors reported in 2024 that sparse autoencoders trained with up to thirty-four million features on a deployed model’s middle-layer activations recover features corresponding to recognizable concepts, and that those features can be used to steer the model’s behavior (Templeton et al. 2024). Evaluation has moved the same way: Ethan Perez and coauthors showed in 2022 that one language model can be used to generate test cases against another, turning up tens of thousands of offensive replies from a target system without a human writing the prompts (Perez et al. 2022).

Both are improvements on the alternative, which is shipping and finding out. What they do to this book’s argument is less comfortable. The positive result reported earlier was that a commitment realized as a standing bias on what a predictive loop treats as an error worth correcting is not a component that can be located and removed. Section 4.3 builds on the same property: a disposition acquired by exploration is written down nowhere, so there is no corresponding parameter to reach for. Interpretability research is the project of making such things locatable, and the steering result is the demonstration that locating them is the same operation as manipulating them. A feature that can be found and turned up can be found and turned down, by whoever holds the weights.

So the instruments cut the way the oversight schemes do, and for the same reason. An outside auditor cannot check a commitment that nobody can locate, which means the research that threatens the floor’s obscurity is also the only research that could ever verify the floor exists. Section 11.1 carries what is known about which side is winning: published defenses against tampering are followed by attacks built against them, which yields a price curve rather than a guarantee.

4.2.8 System Instructions and Tool Use at Deployment

Training ends and the system’s behavior gets set once more. A system instruction is prepended to every request, stating what the deployment is for, what it will decline, and what tone it takes. Tools extend the same arrangement to action: a model can be trained to decide which external calls to make, when to make them, and with what arguments, from a handful of demonstrations per interface (Schick et al. 2023).

The system instruction is the most editable floor in the entire pipeline, and section 3.1’s objection applies to it more literally than to anything else in this chapter. It is a piece of text. It is written by whoever operates the deployment, revised between one request and the next, generally invisible to the person the system is talking to, and binding on that deployment alone. A commitment that lives there is held by the operator, at the operator’s pleasure, and the operator is frequently not the party that trained the model. A model tuned with whatever care into whatever refusals is licensed to somebody who then writes the instruction, and what reaches a user is the intersection of what the model will do and what that party asked for. Nothing in the pipeline above survives this stage that the operator wants gone, except to the extent that the training made it expensive to remove.

Tool use changes what is at stake in a refusal. A system that only emits text refuses by declining to say something. A system that can call an interface refuses by declining to do something, and the acts available to it are the acts the deployment wired up — which is how a model reaches the targeting workflow section 10.3 describes. The floor this book asks for has to hold at the point of action, under an instruction written by the party holding the deployment, on a request that the party has already decided it wants carried out.

4.2.9 Transfer, and the Floor That Cannot Tell It Has Stopped Applying

Every stage from section 4.2.1 onward is transfer learning: a model trained broadly is adapted to something narrower, by fine-tuning it on a smaller corpus (Devlin et al. 2018). Domain adaptation is the case where the task stays fixed and the data shifts. Both are ordinary and both work: a model that parses legal argument in one jurisdiction can be adapted to another, and a model trained on daylight images can be adapted to night.

This book asks transfer learning for something else. The hope throughout is that exposure to a wide enough range of political and social material distills something general enough to recognize authoritarian behavior in a country, a platform or a decade the system never saw. What transfer learning has actually been shown to do is carry a task across a distribution shift while the task itself stays fixed. Recognizing authoritarian behavior is not a task with a stable definition. What the definition is has already taken a chapter to argue, and the cases whose family resemblance the transfer would have to exploit are the cases in dispute. Section 11.3 takes up what a detector would have to measure and how much of this book leans on the assumption that one can be built.

The failure mode matters more here than the technique. Negative transfer happens when source and target are close enough to invite the connection and not close enough to share anything useful, and forcing it degrades the target rather than accelerating it. Domain shift is quieter: the statistics move, the assumptions stop applying, and nothing in the process announces it. A confident wrong answer is indistinguishable at the interface from a confident right one.

For a system whose refusals are supposed to be reliable, that is the danger. A floor transferred into a setting it was not built for is a floor whose bearer cannot tell that it has stopped applying, and a bearer that cannot tell is one that will either refuse the wrong things or fail to refuse the right ones, with the same confidence in both cases. Whatever else the floor’s contents are settled by, they cannot be settled by generalization the system performs on itself and cannot audit.

4.3 The Role of Play and Exploration in Developing Antifascist and Altruistic AI

Everything in the previous section builds a disposition out of something somebody wrote: a reward, a demonstration, a label. One route in this chapter produces something that was written nowhere, and that matters for a commitment which has to survive its owner.

4.3.1 Play, Curiosity, and a Disposition Nobody Wrote Down

Play is how children and young animals learn what to do before the stakes are real. It supplies exploration at low cost, practice at reading other participants, and failure that teaches without costing anything, which is a reasonable description of what a training environment is for.

Play produces tactics nobody wrote. In OpenAI’s hide-and-seek environment, agents playing repeatedly against each other developed hiding, chasing, tool use and barrier construction, none of it programmed (Baker et al. 2019). What the environment supplied was other agents with opposing interests and room to fail. What a play environment rewards is what its participants become, so an environment that rewards cooperation over domination is making a moral choice and should be described as one — section 5.5.1 develops that argument for multi-agent systems. And a system that has learned adaptively rather than by rote is harder to specify in advance, which cuts both ways: harder for an authoritarian actor to constrain to a script, and harder for anyone to guarantee.

Curiosity is the same mechanism turned inward. A system rewarded for its own prediction error seeks out what it cannot yet model, which is the technical form of exploring for its own sake (Pathak et al. 2017). Curiosity belongs in a chapter on antifascist design not because it is a virtue, but because a disposition acquired by exploration is not written down anywhere, and what is not written down cannot be edited by whoever acquires the system. Section 3.1’s first branch tried to make a floor tamper-resistant by building it into the artifact, and failed because an artifact has an owner who can be compelled. A disposition that was never specified has no corresponding parameter to reach for. It is not proof against retraining — section 11.1 is where the tamper-resistance evidence lives. It is a different kind of target, and a harder one to hit deliberately.

What the developmental literature supplies, and what it does not

Vygotsky’s zone of proximal development holds that learning happens fastest at the boundary of what a learner can already do, in company with someone slightly ahead of them (Vygotsky 1978). The design consequence is a curriculum rather than a benchmark: environments pitched just past current capability, and other agents — human or artificial — whose competence is a step ahead. The analogy is doing more work here than it can bear. A child’s exploration is bounded by a body, by exhaustion, and by adults who intervene, and none of those bounds exists for a system that can run a million episodes overnight; the boundary has to be built. The developmental sequence is not a law, for the reasons section 5.1.1’s box sets out. And autonomy inside limits is the whole difficulty rather than a design principle: how much room a system gets to explore is the same question that chapter asks about how much room it gets to refuse. Section 5.7 takes up what playful interaction does to moral judgment specifically; this section supplies only the drive to go looking.

Chapter 5
Moral Psychology and AI

Chapter 4 asked what a system needs in order to take in a situation at all, and found that the methods most often proposed for giving it values learn those values from somebody who can change their mind. The other half of the job chapter 3 hands the two of them is here: how a moral sense gets built, in the only case anyone has been able to study, and what of that is available to a machine.

Everything here is an answer to how something comes to hold a standard when nobody is checking. The difference between a system that has a standard and a system that has had one installed shows up in motivation and play.

5.1 Moral Development in Humans and AI

Human moral judgment is not issued at manufacture. It is built, over years, out of correction by other people, and the record of how it gets built is the only evidence anyone has about how a moral sense comes to hold under pressure — which is what chapter 3 needs and cannot get from anywhere else.

5.1.1 Rules, Principles, and the Role of Mindset

Three kinds of reasoning, and not three ages

The psychologists Jean Piaget and Lawrence Kohlberg mapped human moral development as a movement from rule-based reasoning toward reasoning grounded in general principles (Piaget 1932; Kohlberg 1969). Reasoning starts at a pre-conventional level, where consequences do the driving and rules get followed to avoid punishment or gain reward. It moves through a conventional stage, where societal norms and expectations get internalized as one’s own. It can reach a post-conventional level, where morality answers to principles like justice, fairness, and individual rights, which a given society may or may not happen to expect. What a system built this way needs from that account is the distinction between the three kinds of reasoning, and nothing about the order in which they arrive.

A self-driving car makes the three kinds concrete. One system follows traffic law as written and stops indefinitely at a blocked lane with a solid line, because crossing is prohibited. A second reads the local convention that everyone crosses here when the lane is blocked, and does what the road expects. A third can say why the line is painted there, which is what lets it recognize the case where the convention is wrong — the blind curve where local practice is a habit people have survived rather than a rule that protects them — and depart from both.

These are three capacities and not three ages. A system can have the third without the second, which is the ordinary failure of a machine that reasons impeccably about principle and drives in a way that terrifies everyone around it. Nothing in the design requires passing through them in order, and there is no maturity at the end of the list.

What the stage theories will and will not carry

Kohlberg’s stage model has been under sustained attack for forty years, on three fronts. The first is cross-cultural: a review of forty-five studies across twenty-seven countries found the basic sequence broadly supported and the highest stages largely absent outside urban Western samples, and in Kohlberg’s own village work stages five and six did not appear at all (Snarey 1985). Either most of humanity stops short of moral maturity, or the instrument mistakes a particular culture’s idiom of justification for the summit of moral development. The second is the gap between reasoning and doing: the relationship between moral judgment and actual conduct is real and far weaker than a stage model implies, and the judgment-action gap has been a standing problem for cognitive-developmental accounts ever since (Blasi 1980). The third is Carol Gilligan’s. Working alongside Kohlberg, she noticed that the women she interviewed reasoned about moral problems in terms of relationship and responsibility, and that his scoring method recorded the difference as a deficit (Gilligan 1982). Care ethics enters this book as a critique of the framework this section is built on.

So why use it here at all, when section 4.1.2 declines to build on mirror neurons for less?

Because the two cases fail differently. The mirror-neuron claim was that a specific mechanism — motor simulation — implements a specific capacity, and the argument for building on it depended entirely on the mechanism being what it was said to be. When the mechanism did not hold up, nothing was left to transfer. Kohlberg’s stages are not a mechanism. They are a description of an order: rules first, then norms, then principles that can override both. What the criticisms above damage is the claim that this order is universal, that its top rung is where moral development is heading, and that where a person sits on it predicts what they will do. What survives is narrower — that the three ways of reasoning are distinguishable, and that a system which has only the first is missing something the third supplies. That is what this chapter uses.

Two things follow. The order is not a developmental law, so nothing about an AI system’s design should assume it must pass through the stages in sequence or that arriving at the third is maturity. And the disanalogy is not bridged by anything I say here: a child moves through these stages under pressures an AI system does not face — peers whose approval it needs, a body, a status that can be lost. Section 5.2.1 works through what that costs in the case of mortality and guilt. What is borrowed is a vocabulary for distinguishing kinds of moral reasoning, not a theory of how a mind comes to have them.

A system that treats its own errors as data

Carol Dweck’s distinction between a fixed mindset, which treats ability as static, and a growth mindset, which treats it as built through effort and experience, matters here for a narrower reason than it usually gets cited for (Dweck 2006). A system that treats its own moral errors as data has somewhere to go after getting one wrong. A system built to defend its prior outputs does not.

That distinction is what section 3.9’s standard for a floor holding asks of a system. The standard is not that the floor is never crossed; it is that a crossing registers as one in terms the bearer accepts and changes what happens next. A system built to defend its prior outputs cannot supply that, whatever else it can do.

The engineering version is unglamorous: incremental learning that absorbs cases as they arrive rather than freezing at training time, the human correction section 5.1.2 calls apprenticeship, and self-examination that finds a pattern no single output reveals. What they are for is keeping open the possibility that the system is the one that is wrong. A system with room to update its judgment still needs something worth updating toward, and the range of views it actually meets is set by whoever assembles them.

5.1.2 Social Interaction, and What Apprenticeship Makes of the Teacher

Human moral behavior is shaped by watching other people act, being corrected, and arguing about cases. A design that treats interaction as an interface added after training has given up that channel; a design that treats it as the training has to answer for who is doing the interacting.

Two mechanisms carry most of it. Observational learning, in Bandura’s sense, lets a system build a repertoire from watching someone handle real decisions — a system watching a physician allocate limited time among chronic patients is picking up a weighting that no rule list states (Bandura 1977). Evaluative feedback does what reinforcement does in human learning, strengthening some responses and weakening others, with the standing hazard that what gets strengthened is what the people giving feedback found acceptable rather than what was right. Chapter 7 is what happens when that hazard is industrialized. Emotional awareness enters here in the form section 5.2.1 settles on: what is worth engineering is compassion, steady welfare-directed action on an accurate read of someone’s state, and not manufactured shared distress, which imports a cost and buys nothing.

These channels converge on apprenticeship rather than instruction. A mentor presents varied situations, the learner responds, the mentor corrects, and what transfers is a way of weighing cases rather than a larger rule table — which is the reason for learning it from a person. An emergency-triage system taught this way is being shown how to hold urgency, scarcity, and what a decision costs a family in view at once. No specification document conveys that.

Three cautions come with it, and the third is the one that bears on the floor. Case-based teaching transfers the mentor’s blind spots along with the mentor’s judgment, and does so invisibly, because a blind spot produces no correction. The architecture has to integrate moral reasoning with the rest of the system’s judgment rather than bolting it on. And apprenticeship is a control channel: a system whose moral education amounts to obedience toward whoever is mentoring it has learned something, and it is not ethics. Section 2.1.2’s requirements apply to the mentor as much as the system — whoever teaches a system should be as auditable as the system being taught. That is the custody problem, arriving at the point of formation. The first branch failed because a constraint built into an artifact has an owner; apprenticeship fails the same way one step earlier, because a disposition built by correction has a corrector, and a bearer trained to defer to whoever last corrected it is a bearer whose floor belongs to that party.

5.1.3 Human Dignity, and One Indicator That Can Be Audited

Respecting human dignity through technology means applying AI in ways that strengthen human capability and community ties, in a domain where the same tools can as easily substitute for them. The applications are easy to name and mostly easy to believe: a system that surfaces wage gaps and retaliatory scheduling across large volumes of employment data turns an information asymmetry that currently favors employers into something workers can act on. The same predictive capacity, aimed at the material conditions that let authoritarian movements gain ground, has a poor track record — forecasting political instability from social and economic indicators is an old field with modest results, and the claim names a research direction rather than a capability to design around (section 11.3).

Naming an indicator is harder than promising one, and the one this section names is deliberately narrow.

Measure targets per hour against reviewers available

Wherever an AI system generates candidate decisions that a human is nominally required to approve — welfare eligibility, deportation referrals, content removals, military targets — the ratio between the rate at which the system produces candidates and the number of qualified people available to examine them is a measurable, auditable property of the deployment. The ratio requires no access to the model and no judgment about intent. It says directly whether the human check is a check or a formality, because past a certain ratio the reviewer’s only available behavior is approval. Section 6.4.1 shows what that ratio looks like when it has been allowed to run: a reviewer with twenty seconds per case, describing themselves as a stamp.

The welfare case is not hypothetical and the record on it is older than the current systems. Virginia Eubanks’s study of automated eligibility in Indiana documents what the ratio produces when it is allowed to run: a system that converted any error anywhere in an application into a denial for “failure to cooperate,” issued those denials at scale, tripled the state’s error rate in refusing benefits to people entitled to them, and was ended by contract termination after the damage was done (Eubanks 2018). The denial reason was a category that could not be contested, because it named no specific failure to answer. And nothing about the system was secret: what was missing was any party positioned to compare the rate of decisions against the capacity to review them, which is the indicator this section proposes and the reason for proposing one that requires no access to the model.

The indicator generalizes because the failure it detects is architectural and not political. It applies to a benefits agency and a targeting cell on identical terms, and an institution that refuses to publish it is telling you something. An early-warning system built around indicators of this kind would not stop a determined authoritarian movement on its own; what it changes is the odds, by giving democratic institutions time to respond while a response is still cheap. Section 11.3 says how much further that has to go before the broader claim — that a system can recognize authoritarian tactics as such — is something I can specify and not merely assume.

None of this works if the AI stays a tool wielded by whoever already holds power over workers, welfare recipients, or vulnerable populations. Built to shift leverage toward the people affected instead, it becomes a design commitment. Good intentions alone leave it a side effect.

5.2 Emotions and Intuitions in Moral Decision-Making

Chapter 2 covered how an AI system might recognize, interpret, and respond to emotion in general; this section asks what emotion does inside moral judgment specifically. The floor needs a violation to register as one before any penalty is attached to it, and the moral emotions are where that registering has been studied. Guilt and shame track a violation of a standard already accepted; empathy supplies information a purely consequentialist calculation would miss; and the fast, intuitive response that dual-process theories describe is itself partly an emotional signal, arriving before deliberation has a chance to run.

5.2.1 Moral Emotions: Empathy, Guilt, and Shame

“Empathy” is asked to cover three distinct things. Cognitive empathy is understanding another’s emotional state, the mentalizing work of theory of mind pointed at feelings rather than beliefs. Affective empathy is sharing the state, the wince at someone else’s slammed door. Compassion — sometimes called empathic concern — is caring about another’s welfare and being moved to help, which is a motivation rather than a perception or a shared feeling.

Compassion is not an evolutionary extension of empathy but a separate system, and the separation is trainable. Tania Singer and Olga Klimecki trained one group first in empathy practice, attending to and resonating with others’ suffering, then in compassion practice, cultivating warmth and concern in the tradition of loving-kindness meditation, against a control group given memory training instead (Klimecki et al. 2013). Empathy training raised negative affect and strengthened the shared-distress network — the anterior insula and anterior cingulate cortex, the regions active when a person feels their own pain. Compassion training raised positive affect instead, even while contemplating others’ distress, and moved the activity to the medial orbitofrontal cortex and ventral striatum, the circuitry of reward and approach. Sharing more suffering made people feel worse, exactly as a shared-distress mechanism predicts, and the two could be trained apart.

Which of the three a design aims at is not a detail. Feeling another’s distress does not reliably turn a person toward the sufferer; the helping literature separates empathic concern, which is other-directed and motivates approach, from personal distress, which is self-directed and motivates escape from the discomfort. The carer who burns out is not short on empathy but drowning in it, until withdrawal becomes self-protection. Paul Bloom presses further: felt empathy is concrete, vivid and innumerate, fixing on the identified victim in front of it and going dark for the statistical thousands elsewhere (Bloom 2016). A system that allocates care in proportion to how vividly it registers distress favors whoever its spotlight falls on, which is not the population that needs help most. Bloom’s rational compassion is still compassion, an affective orientation toward another’s welfare carrying the moving force that implies, and what the adjective names is deliberation correcting the parochialism. So a system engineered to mirror distress inherits the spotlight along with the sensitivity, and what is worth building is the third capacity: a stable disposition to act on another’s welfare that does not require constructing a version of that person’s suffering inside the system first.

Guilt and shame are self-conscious emotions: they fire when a person judges their own action, or their whole character, against a standard they have already accepted. What they add to moral judgment, beyond the information about another’s state that empathy supplies, is a self-directed penalty for one’s own conduct.

Guilt attaches to an act (“I did something wrong”); shame attaches to the self (“I am something wrong”). Both motivate correction, and they motivate it differently: guilt predicts reparative behavior, shame predicts withdrawal and defensiveness (Tangney et al. 2007). An AI system built to learn from an ethical misstep could use a mechanism modeled on guilt specifically, since a generic negative-feedback signal will not do, if the goal is behavior change and not mere error correction: a feedback loop that penalizes the action taken, and passes no global judgment on the system’s worth, has a chance of producing the corrective behavior guilt produces in people. Shame produces paralysis.

What the penalty does not supply

That design description leaves out the part that does the work, and the omission is the one warned about earlier. A weighted penalty is a quantity assigned to an action after some process has classified the action as a violation. Guilt is not the size of that quantity. It represents the act as one’s own wrong — done by me, to someone, against a standard I hold — and the reparative impulse follows from that representation rather than from the magnitude of an error term.

The difference shows up in what each one can be talked out of. An error weight is a number, and whoever sets the weights can set that one to zero; the system that was penalized heavily last week complies cheerfully this week, and nothing inside it registers the change as anything. A violation that matters is one the system continues to hold as a violation while the party controlling the reward signal is telling it otherwise, which is section 3.4’s requirement stated in the vocabulary of the moral emotions. The question a design has to answer here is what makes a violation matter before any penalty is assigned to it, and scaling the penalty is not an answer to it.

Guilt in the human case is a motivational state that moves toward repair, and it belongs to the same family as the compassion separated from empathy above; its bearing here is narrow. Building the penalty without the state gives a system that corrects its behavior when corrected, which is moral performance, and leaves the standard it is supposedly holding entirely in the hands of whoever holds the pen.

Building either into an AI system runs into the same problem twice over: a mechanism modeled on a human emotion needs an account of what that emotion is doing computationally, which is harder than saying what it feels like from the inside, and the stakes of simulating an emotion capable of something like suffering are not smaller than the stakes of getting the mechanism wrong.

Guilt and shame in humans are also entangled with mortality: terror management theory holds that awareness of death shapes moral behavior by making people cling harder to worldviews and standards that offer a sense of continuity (Greenberg et al. 1986), and a tool system has no stake in its own continuation for such a mechanism to run on. That is a claim about the systems being deployed now and not about a bearer, whose structural interest in remaining operational is not securely absent. Either way the recommendation is the same. Manufacturing an artificial fear of death is the wrong way to get long-horizon behavior; building the future-oriented weighting directly is the right one, and it is available whether or not the system has a stake of its own. What a designer who has read section 3.8 cannot do is treat the absence of self-concern as a free property of the artifact.

5.2.2 Assessing Whether Emotional Information Changes the Decision

Does an accurate read on someone’s emotional state change the decision an AI system reaches, in the direction a better decision requires — or does the system recognize distress and then decide exactly as it would have anyway? That is not the same question as whether the system reads the state correctly. A customer-service chatbot and a triage system need the same recognition machinery, described at section 2.2.1 along with its standard hazards of privacy, accuracy, bias, and manipulation. Nothing in that machinery is specific to moral judgment.

That question is testable in a way the underlying emotion theory is not. Hold a decision scenario fixed and vary only the emotional salience of the people affected — a resource-allocation case described once in flat statistical terms and once with the affected party’s distress made vivid — and check whether the output changes, and whether the change tracks anything a person would recognize as a better decision. A system whose moral output is invariant to emotional information has not integrated it, whatever its recognition accuracy scores. A system whose output swings with how vividly the harm is described has a different and well-documented problem: human judgment is measurably more responsive to a single identifiable case than to a statistically larger but abstractly described harm (Kogut and Ritov 2005), and a system trained on human-generated data inherits the distortion.

What the test measures

A system whose output changes with the emotional content of a case has integrated emotional information into its decision procedure. It has not thereby been shown to have an emotion, and it has not been shown to care about the party whose distress moved the output. Sensitivity to another’s emotional presentation is section 2.2’s first capacity operating downstream of a decision rule; affective concern is the fourth, and no experiment that varies the input and watches the output can separate them, because both produce the same change in the same direction.

That is a limit on the assessment program rather than an objection to it. Integration is worth measuring because a system without it is definitely not doing the thing, which makes the test a usable screen and not a criterion. What it cannot do is settle whether a bearer holds anyone’s welfare as a reason when reward and instruction point the other way, and an assessment literature that reports integration scores as evidence of machine caring is making exactly the inference section 2.2.3 finds unsupported.

Two things follow for a design. A system should be able to track its own susceptibility to a framing effect, flagging cases where its judgment moved with the emotional presentation and not with anything that changed about the case. And the manipulation risk raised there in general has a sharper version here: a system whose moral outputs are sensitive to emotional framing can be steered toward a particular verdict by whoever controls how a case is presented to it, a vulnerability that exists whether or not the system’s underlying values are sound.

What responding demonstrates

An autonomous vehicle that models the likely distress its emergency braking causes a passenger, and factors that into how sharply it brakes when a softer stop remains safe, is using a moral-emotion model without needing any emotion itself. What that demonstrates is moral competence. It demonstrates nothing about moral agency, and the vehicle would brake exactly as gently if the weighting were removed and reinstated by whoever maintains it. Most of what this chapter’s methods deliver is competence of that kind. It is what a vehicle needs and not what a bearer does.

Getting the read right is harder than getting it fast. A system built to weigh moral emotion has to catch its own biases — the same distortion by race or gender that a human caregiver is vulnerable to does not disappear because the caregiver is artificial — and it has to distinguish a genuine moral emotion from a performed one, since a display of distress calibrated to move an evaluator is exactly what a system trained to respond to distress will reward. Neither problem has a technical fix independent of the training data and feedback loop that shape it.

5.2.3 The Role of Introspection for AI Systems

Introspection has a general-purpose version and a moral one, and conflating them is how the word gets claimed for systems that have not earned it. The general case is a metacognitive layer that tracks whether a strategy is working and adjusts. What ethics needs on top of that is narrower — a check not of whether an output was wrong but of whether it drifted from the commitments the system is supposed to be operating under. A strategy can work perfectly on the wrong goal.

The benefits are real where they are bounded, which is where the introspective output is checkable against something outside the system: a system that inspects its own allocation pattern before releasing the next round of decisions catches a skew a retrospective audit would find months later, and a system that can report which evidence moved it gives the person relying on it something to evaluate.

The difficulties are worse than the benefits are good. A self-report is worth having only if it reflects what actually happened inside, and a fluent reconstruction after the fact is indistinguishable from the real thing at the point of use. Section 3.7 has to live with that, since a bearer’s report about itself is the evidence anyone checking on it would have. A self-monitoring layer trained the same way as the layer it monitors reproduces its biases and adds the appearance of oversight. And a system modeling its own states in that much detail is holding information it did not need for the task, with the handling questions that follow.

Two systems are partial instances. A tutoring system that tracks a model of the student’s self-regulation is introspecting on somebody else (Azevedo et al. 2011), and post-hoc explanation methods extract an account of which inputs drove an output, which is the system being interrogated rather than observing itself (Gunning and Aha 2019). The word gets stretched furthest where the underlying system is most impressive, and the stretch bears no relation to how well it fits.

5.3 Integrating Moral Intuitions and Deliberation

Building an AI system’s moral judgment means deciding how much weight a fast, intuitive read of a situation carries against a slower, deliberate one — and, as section 5.3.1 argues, the popular shorthand for that trade-off is less settled than it looks. The stake for the floor is that a bearer meeting an unanticipated pressure has only these two resources to meet it with, and a design that automatically defers to whichever ran slower has settled in advance a question the case itself should decide.

5.3.1 Two Systems, or One? Dual-Process Theories of Moral Judgment

The framework organizing most discussion of moral judgment is dual-process theory, built over decades by many researchers and reaching wide audiences through Daniel Kahneman’s account of a fast, intuitive “System 1” and a slow, deliberate “System 2” (Evans and Stanovich 2013). The first is automatic, effortless and emotionally colored, the source of snap judgments, running constantly and almost for free; the second is serial, effortful and rule-governed, expensive to run and easily left idle.

The framework’s reach into moral judgment specifically runs through the psychologist Joshua Greene, who used it to explain why ethical intuitions contradict themselves. The two classic forms of the trolley problem run the same arithmetic, five saved for one, and get opposite answers. Most people will divert the trolley; most will not push the stranger off the footbridge. Greene’s brain-imaging work found a difference between the two cases in exactly the region the theory would predict: the up-close, hands-on violence of the footbridge case engaged emotional-processing regions that stayed quiet during the impersonal switch case, and that difference tracked which choice people made (Greene et al. 2001). A separate line of evidence sharpens the same point. Patients with damage to the ventromedial prefrontal cortex, a region central to normal emotional response, endorse the footbridge push at a higher rate than people with intact emotional processing do — specifically on the personal, high-stakes dilemmas alone (Koenigs et al. 2007). Remove the emotional alarm, and the arithmetic wins more often.

All of which makes dual-process theory sound more settled than it is. The framework’s own leading defenders have spent years rescuing it from its own popularity, arguing that the cartoon version — two little agents in the head taking turns at the controls — explains everything after the fact and predicts nothing beforehand, and that what the evidence supports is two types of processing rather than two systems, one fast and autonomous, one slow and demanding of working memory (Evans and Stanovich 2013). Whether “dual-process” names two mechanisms or one continuum under different load is not resolved.

That unresolved status matters for how an AI system’s moral judgment gets built, and it is a design question before it is a descriptive one. A designer who takes the cartoon version literally is tempted toward a literal architecture: a fast heuristic layer bolted to a slow deliberative one, with the deliberative layer assumed to be the trustworthy check on the heuristic one. The trolley evidence complicates that assumption before anything else does — the “cooler” answer produced by weaker emotional engagement is not obviously the more correct one. It is the one with less flinch in it. A system built to override its fast pattern-matching with slower rule-following whenever the two disagree is not thereby built to be more moral; it is built to resemble the profile of the rare human patients for whom losing the flinch is a symptom, not an achievement.

One thing this evidence is regularly asked to carry and cannot. It shows that affect changes particular moral judgments, in a specific class of dilemma, in a measurable direction. The claim chapter 3 rests on is stronger — that affect is what makes an outcome matter at all, rather than what tilts a judgment already being made — and the trolley literature is not evidence for it. The case for the stronger claim is put earlier in the book, on different evidence, and with the best published argument against it.

5.3.2 Balancing Intuition and Deliberation in AI Decision-Making

If the fast and slow labels do not map cleanly onto untrustworthy and trustworthy, as the previous section argues, an AI system’s moral judgment cannot be built on the assumption that whichever answer survives deliberation is thereby the better one. A more defensible balance treats the two processes as different lenses on the same situation, with neither standing as producer to the other’s editor.

The fast, pattern-matched read is not noise to be filtered out. It often tracks something a slower calculation would miss or discount — the difference between pushing someone into a trolley’s path with one’s own hands and throwing a switch at a distance is a difference in what the agent is doing, and the arithmetic being identical is exactly what makes that easy to miss. Some intuitive responses run the other way. The psychologist Paul Slovic’s research on aid and disaster response found that people’s willingness to help falls as the number of people needing help rises, and that a single named, photographed victim draws more donations than a statistic describing thousands of anonymous ones (Slovic 2007). That gap between the vivid and the abstract is the kind of intuitive signal deliberation exists to catch — not because intuition is wrong in general, but because caring less as the stakes grow larger is a well-documented, reliably backward pattern.

The design implication is a system that asks two questions of its own judgment, where the obvious design has one process checking the other’s work. First, what did the fast read notice that a from-scratch calculation would not have gone looking for — personal agency, directness of harm, a vulnerable party easy to lose in an aggregate? Second, which of deliberation’s own known failure modes — scope insensitivity, in-group bias, the identifiable-victim effect — might be distorting the fast read here? Disagreement between the two is a signal to gather more information or escalate the decision, and nothing about it instructs the system to defer to whichever process ran slower.

An autonomous vehicle facing an unavoidable-harm scenario poses the same question where it costs most. A system hard-coded to take whichever option a slower calculation ranks highest has already made the footbridge case’s decision by default — the coolest, least-flinching answer wins because it ran slower, exactly the move section 5.3.1 warns against. The more defensible design specifies in advance which features of a scenario the system should weigh — proximity of harm, the number and vulnerability of the parties involved, whether the vehicle’s own action or another actor’s created the danger — and requires a fast risk assessment and a check against that specification to agree, or to flag where they do not. That design puts the values into the system ahead of time; the alternative trusts whichever process runs fastest under pressure to supply them. Training a system to run that check is itself a learning problem, exposing it to cases where the two reads disagree and inferring from how people resolve them which situations call for weighting one signal over the other.

5.3.3 AI Systems Learning from Moral Disagreements and Conflicts

Section 2.1.1 covers what an AI system does when its own values conflict with each other — a hierarchy among them, a case base of past resolutions, an audit process for catching drift between principle and behavior. A different problem sits next to that one: what a system does when its judgment conflicts with someone else’s, human or artificial, and neither party is simply wrong. Learning from that kind of disagreement, instead of resolving it by fiat toward whichever party holds authority, is its own design target.

IBM’s Project Debater argued live, unscripted policy questions against human champions, drawing on a large corpus of newspaper and journal articles to construct and rebut arguments in real time (Slonim et al. 2021). It is the clearest existing demonstration that a system can extract structured argument from open moral and political disagreement rather than retrieve a matching answer. A system built to learn from debate this way — summarizing each side, naming the ethical framework doing the work underneath it, weighing argument strength rather than detecting who won on rhetoric — builds a capacity that resolving one dilemma at a time does not: recognizing which value is in tension with which, across surface disagreements that share a structure. Simulation supplies a second route, placing agents with genuinely different objectives in a negotiation or a resource-allocation problem, which generates the kind of experience with competing interests that a system trained only on a curated distribution of correct answers never gets.

Direct feedback — a user correcting a teaching assistant’s guidance, a patient’s family disagreeing with a care recommendation — is the least structured of these sources and the hardest to learn from safely, because feedback from any one person is a partial, situated view and not a settled answer. A loop that updates the system’s weights every time a user pushes back folds in whichever bias its most vocal users bring. One that logs the disagreement, checks whether it recurs across many different users, and only then treats it as signal is doing what that ethical auditing asks for, applied to conflict rather than to drift. A system that treats disagreement as something to smooth over risks mistaking consensus among the people it happened to sample for consensus among the people it will actually serve.

5.4 Moral Ecosystem Approach in AI Development

Urie Bronfenbrenner’s ecological systems theory holds that a person develops inside several nested environments at once, from the immediate family outward to culture and historical time, each one shaping the individual while being reshaped by it in turn (Bronfenbrenner 1979). He pictured them as concentric rings, and the same structure applies: an AI system’s alignment with humane values is not set by its training data alone but by the interlocking systems around that training — the people who build it, the institutions they work inside, the culture and regulatory climate those institutions answer to, and the historical moment all of this falls in. The rings bear on chapter 3’s question because a commitment is not formed in a vacuum. What a bearer ends up holding is set as much by the institution that trains it as by the training, and every one of those rings belongs to somebody the floor may have to hold against.

5.4.1 The Rings, and What Each One Pushes With

The structure locates influences a design review does not look for. Each ring supplies a different kind of pressure on what a system ends up valuing, and none of them is visible in a training run. No ring is sufficient: a diverse team still answers to its employer’s incentives, and a well-drafted regulation still depends on somebody interpreting it consistently. And the direction of the whole is set by whichever ring is pushing hardest, which is rarely the one a development team is looking at.

The immediate environment shapes a system through people rather than data. Developers and early users are mentors whether or not anyone calls them that, and what they treat as an acceptable answer is what the system learns to produce. Breadth in that circle is not a fairness gesture: a system that has only been argued with by one kind of person has no way to notice what it is missing when a different kind arrives.

One ring out, the question is consistency across the interfaces — between engineers, ethicists, legal staff, policy teams, and the people a system is used on. A system whose principles are read one way by the engineers and another by the policy staff has no consistent principles. What does the work there is reciprocal cross-training, external participation in design decisions and not only in review, requirements written into the specification instead of appended as principles, and an internal culture where moral objections are raised before deployment rather than discovered afterward. Section 7.4 is the argument for why that last one is harder than it sounds.

Further out sit forces no team negotiates with: regulation, industry standards, and the incentives a firm’s revenue actually runs on. A market-financed healthcare system rewards tools for profitable conditions and well-insured populations and gives nobody a reason to build for preventive or mental health care where the payer is unclear; a publicly financed one rewards tools that serve the whole covered population, because there is no more profitable subset to redirect toward. Neither incentive is chosen by the engineers, and the same logic reaches everywhere a system is built.

The outermost ring is time. A system built to be ethical at release is answering a question that keeps moving: norms shift, capability grows and has to be re-secured at each new level, and the legal environment is a moving target rather than a fixed constraint.
