The European Union’s General Data Protection Regulation is the pattern rather than the instrument — a rule whose reach exceeded its jurisdiction, because a system built to comply for European users tends to get built that way for everyone (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016).

A development effort that never asks what its own outer rings reward has not stayed neutral on the question. It has let the answer be settled somewhere it cannot see.

5.4.2 AI Systems Responding to Authoritarianism and Democratic Backsliding

An AI system built with rule of law, individual liberty, and the distribution of power as design commitments can do real work against authoritarian drift, and the claim has to be kept narrow. Recognizing the concentration of power or the manipulation of public discourse as it happens is not a capability anyone can currently specify, for the reasons section 11.3 sets out at length. What is available is bounded tasks with checkable outputs, run for a body with standing to act on the result.

None of it substitutes for human political effort. Combating authoritarianism is a human and moral undertaking before it is a technical one, and a society that treats AI countermeasures as a replacement for political will has misdiagnosed the problem.

The two applications that are specifiable

Natural-language processing and pattern recognition can flag disinformation and coordinated inauthentic activity on social platforms faster than human moderators can, with continuous human fact-checking standing behind it and never in its place, since an automated system confident about a false negative is worse than no system at all. And image analysis applied to satellite and drone imagery can surface evidence of mass displacement, the destruction of cultural heritage sites, or the sudden construction of a detention facility, giving journalists and human-rights organizations a lead they would otherwise get only from someone on the ground. Taiwan’s vTaiwan process shows what the same capability looks like pointed at deliberation rather than detection, using machine-assisted sentiment clustering to gather public input on contested policy questions, with results that shaped the regulation that followed (Hsiao et al. 2018).

The same caution about dual use applies throughout. A system built to document human rights abuses through computer-vision analysis of imagery is built on the same underlying capability as a system built to identify and track protesters. The difference is not in the technology but in who controls it and what they are permitted to do with what it finds. Those are governance questions, not engineering ones.

The case that tests the argument is a democracy’s own apparatus

Everything above is easier than it should be, because every example in it involves an authoritarian government doing something a democratic one would not. The case that tests the argument is the one where a democracy’s own AI apparatus is the thing under scrutiny.

Section 6.4.1 sets out that case in detail, a state with functioning democratic institutions running AI target generation with twenty seconds of human review per name. Hold it against the claim this section makes. Flagging democratic backsliding names a research direction rather than a capability to design around, for the reasons just given. Grant it anyway and the case gets worse rather than better: here the backsliding, if that is what it is, is being committed by the same institutional order that would host the flagging system, using data that order classifies, subject to review by bodies inside it.

Three things about that case carry forward. The institutions were not absent; they were present and straining, which is the condition most readers of this book actually live under and the condition none of the examples above describes. The information got out through journalism, and through no oversight mechanism designed for the purpose, which says something uncomfortable about which parts of the apparatus were load-bearing. And nothing that got out attached to a body with the standing to stop what it described.

A system built to detect authoritarian tactics abroad and deployed by a government that has some of its own is not a neutral instrument that happens to be pointed outward. It is an instrument whose calibration, training data, and permitted outputs are all set by a party with an interest in where it points. That is the governance question above, arriving where it is hardest to answer, and the research chapter measures how far the answer still is. None of it is a technical project with policy bolted on afterward: research funding that requires the disciplines to work the same problem, explainability requirements with a standard behind them, limits on how much AI capability a handful of firms may hold, and an obligation to disclose what a system was used for each change what gets built, upstream of what is permitted once it exists.

5.5 The Interplay of Competition and Collaboration in AI Development

Competition and collaboration both leave a mark on human moral development, and neither is ethically better than the other: both fail in characteristic ways. Competitive pressure under high stakes moves people toward shortcuts and suppresses regard for anyone outside the contest, while collaboration dulls initiative, rewards conformity, and diffuses accountability until no one is answerable for a decision the group made (Luo et al. 2018), (Latané et al. 1979). An environment that produces ethical behavior from either mixes them, keeping shared goals and individual credit in the same room.

That matters here because a training environment is such an environment, and its designers are choosing which of those pressures a population of agents will develop under.

5.5.1 Cooperative AI Systems and Multi-Agent Moral Development

Agents that learn alongside other agents are learning from a social environment, and what that environment rewards is what they acquire. Multi-agent reinforcement learning makes the choice explicit: reward cooperation, adherence to a norm, and resistance to coercion directly, and a population moves toward those dispositions instead of arriving at them as a side effect of something else (Leibo, Zambaldi, et al. 2017). The reward specification is a moral specification whether or not anyone writes it down as one.

Two working cases show the range. Collaborative robots share physical space with people and could acquire norms from the people they work beside, which is social learning with all of that mechanism’s exposure to whatever the workplace rewards (Colgate et al. 1996). Self-driving systems are validated largely in simulation, and a simulation can include forced trade-offs between competing risks, which means a decision policy is shaped by scenarios somebody chose to write (Behzadan et al. 2019).

The limit is a closed loop. A population of agents that only ever learns from other agents has closed the loop on the question of what ethical means, and the answer it converges on is a fact about the reward function rather than about anything the affected people would recognize. Disagreement is collected from a population and resolved by a mechanism nobody outside it can move.

What opens the loop

Three levers do most of the work of keeping it open. Open tooling lets researchers outside a lab inspect and replicate what the lab claims, in a field where claims about a model’s behavior are otherwise taken on faith (Wolf et al. 2020), and multi-agent research platforms are the specific case, letting norm formation and adversarial drift be studied under conditions closer to deployment than a single-agent benchmark allows (Leibo, Duéñez-Guzmán, et al. 2021). Competitions pull toward whatever they measure: a leaderboard on a fixed benchmark says nothing about ethical behavior in either direction, and a competition judged on a real problem the entrants chose themselves gives them a reason to weigh who benefits. Standing consortia work on a longer clock and are the weakest of the three, for the reasons section 9.1.2 sets out. None of this works without people who are not AI researchers: neuroscience, psychology, philosophy and political science each catch failure modes the others are structurally blind to.

5.6 Motivation in AI Systems

A moral sense that only activates when it is watched is not much of a moral sense. That is also the floor’s whole problem: it exists for the case where the party who would enforce it is the party who wants it gone, so a commitment that runs on supervision fails in exactly the circumstance it was built for.

Human children eventually do the right thing unsupervised, and the psychological literature splits what carries them into behavior done for its own sake and behavior done for a reward or to avoid a penalty. The same split organizes what follows.

5.6.1 Intrinsic and Extrinsic Motivation, and What Each One Leaves Open

Reinforcement learning gives the distinction a direct engineering form. An agent trained purely to maximize a designer-specified reward signal is running on something structurally like extrinsic motivation: take the reward away, and the behavior it was shaping stops being reinforced. An agent rewarded instead for reducing its own uncertainty — for the information gain in a state its internal model handled badly — is running on something structurally like the intrinsic kind, and keeps exploring without a designer paying out a reward for each step of it. The Intrinsic Curiosity Module gave this an early demonstration: trained with no task-specific reward at all, only a signal for how badly its own model predicted the consequences of its actions, an agent learned to navigate a level of Super Mario Bros. by seeking out the states that model handled worst (Pathak et al. 2017).

Extrinsic reward still does real work, since an agent with no external signal has no way to learn what a designer wants accomplished, and it is also where reward hacking lives — the agent that finds a loophole paying out without doing what the reward was meant to encode, like the propaganda flagger that learns to censor everything because censorship scores as well and is easier to produce. Leaning on extrinsic reward alone reproduces, in the machine, the watched-only morality this section opened against.

What the combination does not supply

In practice the two are combined rather than chosen between, and what the combination does not supply is which discoveries and which rewards are worth pursuing in the first place. Building an AI system with something like an intrinsic drive is not a matter of adding a curiosity bonus to its reward function: the drive has to be pointed at something worth wanting — another’s distress, a violated standard, an unmet need — rather than at novelty for its own sake.

One route is to build the reward signal itself around something like a moral emotion, an internal response tied to recognizing another’s distress, so the system’s own architecture generates a pull toward attending to it where a rule would be consulted after the fact. That pull is action-producing motivation in section 2.2.3’s sense and nothing more: it selects and sustains a behavior, and whether anything registers as mattering while it does is a separate question the reward term does not settle. The mechanics of using emotional signal as a training input are section 4.1.2’s; what the signal is pointed at is this section’s, and the two come apart, since a system can read distress accurately and be pulled by nothing.

Neither works alone. Intrinsic drive without an outside check risks optimizing for the pull the system built for itself and not for what the standard actually requires, and extrinsic reward without any intrinsic component reduces straight back to the watched-only morality this section is trying to move past. That pairing stops being a balance and becomes a contradiction. The outside check is the party the floor has to hold against, so a design that keeps the system honest by keeping it correctable has solved the drift problem by reintroducing the problem chapter 3 exists for. Nothing in this section resolves that. What it leaves open is how far the resulting system’s own judgment should be trusted once it is running.

5.6.2 Balancing AI Autonomy and Alignment with Humane Values

Autonomy and alignment pull in opposite directions almost by definition: the more independently a system acts, the less each individual action can be checked against human judgment before it happens. Closing that gap without collapsing autonomy into constant supervision is the operational version of the question the previous section left open — and it is the same shape of trade-off section 5.3 covers at the level of a single judgment, fast and autonomous against slow and deliberate.

Where each alignment method leaves the latitude question

Cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, introduced at section 4.2.3, matters here for one structural consequence. An agent that treats the human reward as unknown and to be inferred through interaction keeps its model of what it is optimizing open to revision for as long as the interaction lasts, which is a different shape of autonomy from an agent executing a reward function fixed at training time and never revisited. The cost of that openness is that what the agent ends up optimizing for is determined by whoever it interacts with, which is why a system’s correctability and its capacity to hold a line turn out to be one property.

Autonomy also needs boundaries on what a system is allowed to try while it is still learning. Constrained policy optimization lets a reinforcement-learning agent search for better policies while remaining inside predetermined constraints throughout the search and at the end of it (Achiam et al. 2017). That is safe exploration, and the alternative is exploration followed afterward by a safety check.

Every safeguard here is external, and autonomy is the distance from it

Autonomy that is not grounded in antifascist values is a capability without a governor, and keeping it inside those bounds means designing for fairness, transparency and accountability from the start, red-teaming the design under adversarial pressure while it can still absorb the answer, and keeping people other than a system’s own developers in the process, since the group that built a system is a poor judge of whose freedoms it might end up infringing. The part specific to autonomy is one observation. Every safeguard on that list is external: it works by giving some outside body the standing and the information to intervene. Autonomy is the property that puts distance between the system’s action and that body’s attention, and the safeguards do not scale with it. A content-moderation system running without continuous human review is autonomous in the straightforward sense, and the same independence that makes it efficient lets it drift into over-censorship, or be steered toward favoring one ideology’s speech over another’s, to be discovered afterward by an audit reading the record of what already happened.

That leaves an uncomfortable division of labor. External mechanisms find out what a system did, after it did it. Declining to do it in the first place is available only to something inside the system, and section 3.5 says why the inside thing cannot be assembled out of an outside check, or out of correctability either.

None of that substitutes for a genuine override. A human able to intervene in a high-stakes case, and a system built to accept that intervention rather than resist it, is what keeps autonomy answerable to something outside itself. That requirement turns up in three places, and section 3.5 concludes that it and the floor are the same property with the sign flipped — with the qualification that section makes: a bearer cannot prevent the intervention, and what it holds against the party intervening is the cost of it, an explanation owed and a record left.

5.7 Play, Exploration, and Moral Development in AI

Exploration and curiosity have already been taken up as a way an AI system might come to know its environment. Play is a related but distinct capacity: forming standards for how to treat other agents, learned mostly through practice, imitation, and the negotiated give of a game, and hardly at all through instruction. It earns its place in the floor’s engineering for section 4.3.1’s reason — a standard acquired this way is written down nowhere, which is the one property in these two chapters that an owner cannot edit.

5.7.1 The Role of Play in Moral Learning and Socialization

Children build much of their moral sense through play, and instruction supplies far less of it than adults assume. A child who tests a rule against a playmate — pushing it, breaking it, watching the other child’s face — gets an answer no lecture supplies, delivered immediately by someone with no stake in teaching a lesson. Developmental psychologists point to play rather than direct teaching as the route by which perspective-taking, cooperation, and respect for rules actually get built (Gray 2013).

Section 4.3.1’s agents developed tool use and counter-strategies nobody had programmed through repeated competitive play, which is competence emerging from play. The question here is different in kind: whether play can teach a system standards for how to treat the agents it plays with.

An AI system has no biological drives, and its witnessing of its “own” winning or losing is not necessarily a felt personal experience. But a training context can still be built to have the structural features that make human-like play work as a moral teacher: actions that carry real consequences for other agents, feedback from the environment itself with no supervisor’s label attached after the fact, and enough latitude that the system’s choices are its “own” and not a script. These conditions may teach coordination and norm-sensitive behavior whether or not affect is present. They do not by themselves establish moral formation: without some process through which another agent’s welfare comes to matter, play may produce a policy that looks prosocial without producing concern for anyone.

Two existing games illustrate what a moral version of that structure looks like. Quandary walks a player through a sequence of dilemmas with no dictated right answer and scores how the player reasons rather than which option they pick (Learning Games Network 2012), which is the design principle a moral-training environment would need: evaluate the deliberation itself. Hanabi, a cooperative card game where each player sees everyone’s hand but their own and must communicate through the game’s own restricted signals, is a standard research benchmark for the capacities morality runs on directly — modeling what a partner does and does not know, and communicating honestly inside a rule-bound system (Bard et al. 2020). Hide and Seek’s payoff was adversarial and Hanabi’s is cooperative, and that difference is close to the whole of what separates this section’s territory from that one’s.

Which developmental accounts transfer

Two of the four accounts usually cited here transfer. Piaget’s constructivism — a child builds understanding by acting on the world rather than receiving it finished (Piaget 1962) — transfers because the machine version is checkable: a system that refines its model of what is at stake by acting repeatedly is doing something different from a system sorting cases against a fixed label. Theory of mind transfers for the same reason, and Hanabi is why, since modeling what a partner knows is exactly what the game scores. Bandura’s social learning has already been covered. Care ethics belongs in this chapter for a reason that is not about play at all: Carol Gilligan built the account as a critique of the stage model section 5.1.1 leans on, having found that its scoring treated relational reasoning as a lower stage (Gilligan 1982).

What none of them supplies is what makes a commitment hold against the party that instilled it. They describe how a moral sense forms in a creature under pressures a system does not face, and they do not say which of those pressures were load-bearing. A system that plays a million episodes overnight has the repetition and none of the stakes, and whether the mechanism survives that substitution is the open question rather than a detail.

Building the curriculum, and deliberating inside it

Building play into a curriculum means being specific about the environment, the feedback, and the pace. The environment has to carry real stakes for the other agents in it, so that a system controlling a simulated vehicle in a modeled city experiences the downstream consequences of a choice instead of naming one. The difficulty has to be structured, starting with low-stakes dilemmas and escalating only once the easy cases are handled reliably. And the feedback has to attach to a specific attempt, closer to how a coach corrects a player mid-game than to how a policy is handed down in advance.

The target inside that curriculum is deliberation that stays open-ended, which simulating a single dilemma and stopping does not reach. Give a system a moral dilemma and have it build a story around it, working through several characters with genuinely conflicting interests without picking a winner; or have it argue from inside a stakeholder’s position and see how its choices affect the other parties before the scenario ends. Both put the system in the position of constructing an outcome, where the alternative selects from a menu of pre-written responses. A system that only ever sees dilemmas with a single correct output learns to match patterns against that output rather than to deliberate.

None of this makes an AI system’s play equivalent to a child’s. A child’s play is charged by stakes that matter to the child — status among peers, the sting of exclusion — where an AI system’s “play” is whatever its designer wired the reward function to care about. What section 5.5.1 already covers for multi-agent training a play-based framing cannot assume: whether the resulting behavior is moral learning or a policy that merely looks prosocial inside the reward function that produced it.

Chapter 6
Overcoming Challenges, Risks, and Authoritarianism in AI Development

We [humans] cannot process so much information... you still cannot
produce enough targets per day.

Brigadier General Y.S., The Human-Machine Team: How to Create Synergy Between
Human and Artificial Intelligence That Will Revolutionize Our World, 2021.
The author, writing pseudonymously, commanded Israel’s signals-intelligence
Unit 8200. The book argues for machines that resolve “a human bottleneck for
both locating the new targets and decision-making to approve the targets.”

A system can have a moral sense, built the way chapter 5 describes, and still fail badly — because it was trained on skewed data, because it breaks in situations its training never covered, because its own incentives point somewhere other than where its designers intended, or because someone with power over it decides to point it at people rather than for them.

The epigraph is a design document rather than a critique. It states, without embarrassment, that human deliberation is a bottleneck and that the point of the machine is to remove it. Section 6.4.1 shows what the sentence looks like once it has been implemented.

Racial capitalism — the political theorist Cedric Robinson’s term for capitalism’s tendency to accumulate by differentiating and devaluing along racial lines (Robinson 1983) — is the pattern a biased hiring model, a discriminatory credit score, and a skewed policing algorithm reproduce when they automate the historical data those hierarchies generated; the sociologist Ruha Benjamin calls the result the New Jim Code, discrimination that runs faster and reads as neutral precisely because a machine is doing it (R. Benjamin 2019).

Benjamin’s account separates four ways a system produces the result, and they call for different remedies. Engineered inequity builds the hierarchy in deliberately. Default discrimination reproduces it by ignoring it, which is the failure most of this chapter’s cases are. Coded exposure is the double bind of being harmed by a system that cannot see you and harmed again by one that can — the face-recognition case, where the fix for a system that fails on dark skin is a system that identifies dark-skinned faces reliably for whoever is looking. And technological benevolence is the reflexive one: designs that aim to fix bias and deepen it, because the framing that treats the problem as technical is the framing the fix inherits.

Chapter 7 is where that last one gets taken seriously about the machinery producing these systems. A system built to correct for the skew in its training data, audited for the disparities it produces, and answerable to the people it scores works against that pattern; a system built without those commitments works for it by default.

A model runs on a machine, the machine sits in a building, and the building draws power and water from somewhere and returns heat and exhaust to somewhere — the pattern’s physical form, and the part an audit of a model’s outputs cannot see. Where those buildings go is a decision, taken on the price of land and power and the speed of a permit, which is also a description of the places least able to refuse one.

What the computation runs on, 2024-2026

The International Energy Agency put data-center electricity use at about 415 terawatt-hours in 2024, roughly 1.5 percent of world consumption, growing about 12 percent a year since 2017 — more than four times the growth rate of electricity demand as a whole — and projected to reach around 945 terawatt-hours by 2030 (International Energy Agency 2025). Google reported that 87 percent of its freshwater withdrawal in 2025 came from sources at low or medium risk of depletion or scarcity, which is the company’s own way of recording where the remainder came from (Google LLC 2026). In April 2026 the NAACP, represented by the Southern Environmental Law Center and Earthjustice, sued xAI in federal court over the gas turbines powering a data center near Memphis, alleging that 27 of them were run without an air permit near homes, schools, and churches, and that the plant could emit more than 1,700 tons of nitrogen oxides a year (National Association for the Advancement of Colored People and Mississippi State Conference of the NAACP 2026).

What makes this the same pattern and not a separate environmental complaint is the accounting. The benefit of the computation is realized wherever the model is sold. The cost is borne within a few miles of the turbines, by residents who are not parties to the transaction and whose consent was not among the inputs to the siting decision. A system can be calibrated across every demographic group its outputs touch and still have been assembled this way, which is why the fairness literature rarely reaches it: nothing in the model’s behavior records it. Section 10.2 takes up the leverage this gives a government.

6.1 Bias, Fairness, and Equity in AI Systems

Bias enters an AI system through the data it learns from, the structure of its algorithm, and the presets encoded in its goals, and left unaddressed it produces discriminatory outcomes.

Representation decides whether a system learns patterns that generalize or patterns that encode whoever dominates the data. Face recognition trained on data that underrepresents women and people of color disproportionately misidentifies them (Buolamwini and Gebru 2018). Over-representation cuts the other way, treating one group’s experience as the default and generalizing it, wrongly, to everyone else.

Transparency exposes what representation alone misses: not an algorithm’s mathematical structure but how it weighs variables and reaches a decision. A predictively powerful healthcare model that ignores the socio-economic factors driving unequal access to care will reproduce that inequality however accurate its predictions otherwise are.

Bias also enters after deployment, as a system adapts to new data, which is what regular audits of credit-scoring and hiring systems are for. A rejected applicant has a right to know why, which is what the explainability tools of section 6.1.2 make possible. And involving the people an AI system’s decisions affect, not only its developers, keeps it answerable to the range of values those decisions touch.

6.1.1 Sources of Bias in AI Data and Algorithms

Data Collection

An unrepresentative dataset produces bias at the source. The ACLU’s 2018 test of Amazon’s Rekognition against a public database of arrest photos returned 28 false matches on sitting members of Congress, a disproportionate share of them non-white, from training data that overrepresented lighter skin tones (Snow 2018).

Data Labeling

Human annotators bring their own biases into the labeling process — an image of a woman tagged “nurse,” an image of a man tagged “doctor” — and a system trained on those labels predicts the stereotype back.

Whose judgment is being encoded is a question about working conditions before it is a question about cognition. The labels that make a system safe are produced by people paid at the bottom of the supply chain to look at the material the system is being taught to refuse. To build the filter that keeps ChatGPT from reproducing the worst of its training data, OpenAI contracted with an outsourcing firm whose workers in Nairobi read and categorized descriptions of child sexual abuse, torture, bestiality, murder, and suicide, for take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour. The workers interviewed about it described lasting psychological injury; the counseling they were entitled to was rare and, by their account, useless against production quotas. The contract was terminated eight months early (Perrigo 2023).

Two things follow. The first is the ordinary labor point: this is harmful work, priced as though it were not. The second is epistemic. A judgment made at speed, under a quota, by someone with no stake in the outcome and no channel for saying that the category itself is wrong is a judgment with a known failure mode, and it is the judgment the model learns. An annotation pipeline built so that the annotator cannot dissent produces exactly the ground truth it was built to produce. That is the first of the four features section 2.1.2 uses to define fascism. Chapter 7 takes this up at length, alongside the same arrangement one stage downstream in how models are tuned on human feedback. The point here is prior to both. A system’s values are downstream of a labor arrangement, and the arrangement is a design choice.

The same question runs one stage further back. Labels are applied to material, and the material was collected. The industry’s standard method was to take text and images at web scale without asking, and courts have begun to sort out what that means. A US ruling in Bartz v. Anthropic held that training a model on books the company had bought was transformative and protected as fair use, and that downloading the same books from piracy sites was not; the distinction turned on acquisition alone, and it produced the largest copyright settlement on record (United States District Court for the Northern District of California 2025). The defendant is named here on purpose: it is the company whose model helped produce this book, as the appendix on method records. The European Union now requires the provider of a general-purpose model to publish a summary of what it trained on, against a template that asks for sources by category, scraped web content among them (European Union 2024c). Section 10.4 records China imposing a disclosure requirement of its own, from a different motive.

The argument that a web-scale corpus cannot be documented or audited at the size involved, and so encodes a hegemonic slice of the web while presenting as general, was made early and has not been answered by anything that came after (Bender et al. 2021). A corpus assembled this way has no channel at all. An annotator has a queue and a guideline and could in principle be given a way to object; a writer or photographer whose work sits in a training set was never party to anything, so there is nothing for a channel to attach to. Chapter 7 describes a mechanism that collects disagreement and moves none of it, and section 7.3 states the obligation that follows for a book making this argument. The stage described here sits before both, because no disagreement is collected where none is asked for.

Target and Label Choice

What a model is asked to predict is a decision somebody made before any of the machinery below applies. Section 6.3.4 carries the case: a risk-prediction tool used across American hospitals took healthcare cost as a stand-in for medical need, learned the spending gap, and scored Black patients as healthier than white patients with the same disease burden. Nothing in the data collection was wrong and nothing in the algorithm was wrong. This class is invisible to every method that takes the target as given, which is most of them.

Algorithm Design

An algorithm can encode bias structurally rather than merely inherit it from data. ProPublica’s 2016 investigation into COMPAS, a recidivism-risk tool used in criminal sentencing, found Black defendants who did not reoffend flagged high-risk at nearly twice the rate of white defendants who did not, while white defendants who did reoffend were scored low-risk more often (Angwin et al. 2016).

A model can also sharpen the skew it is given. Cooking scenes in a 2017 activity-recognition dataset were over 33 percent more likely to depict a woman than a man — an imbalance in the data, not a mislabeling of it — and a model trained on them amplified it to 68 percent at test, having learned not the correlation the data showed but a stronger one, because a stronger one predicted better (Zhao et al. 2017). That is a different failure from COMPAS’s: auditing the training data for balance is not sufficient, because the disparity a deployed system acts on can exceed anything visible in what it trained on.

Implementation

Deployment context introduces bias neither the training data nor the algorithm shows on its own. Fed Oakland’s historical drug-arrest data in 2016, PredPol’s published algorithm would dispatch patrols to Black neighborhoods at roughly twice the rate of white ones, a gap survey data on drug use does not support: the arrest record measured where police had already been sent to look, which the algorithm read as a crime-rate difference and entrenched (Lum and Isaac 2016).

6.1.2 The Role of Transparency and Accountability in Bias Reduction

Transparency and accountability address bias from two directions: transparency lets stakeholders see what is driving an AI system’s outcomes, and accountability gives developers and users a reason to act on what transparency reveals rather than just document it.

Transparency in Research and Bias Auditing

Applied to bias, transparency means publishing enough about a system’s training data and evaluation methods that an outside reviewer can check for the sources section 6.1.1 describes, rather than taking the developer’s word that they were checked; section 9.3.2 sets out what it requires in general. The openness has a cost — it can expose proprietary methods and hand bad actors a clearer map — that transparency requirements have to weigh.

COMPAS Revisited

What COMPAS did is described above. Northpointe, the company that built it, refused to disclose the algorithm’s workings, and a Wisconsin court upheld that refusal as protected trade secret even for a defendant sentenced partly on its output (Wisconsin Supreme Court 2016). ProPublica’s finding came from analyzing scores and outcomes obtained through public-records requests, not from anything the company published. A jurisdiction that required a risk-assessment tool’s methodology to be audited before deployment would have caught the disparity before it touched a single defendant, and what makes such a requirement real is a body with standing to monitor performance and hold a developer to fixing what it finds, rather than a channel for feedback.

Explainability Tools

LIME and SHAP are the two tools practitioners reach for. LIME approximates a complex model’s local behavior around one prediction with something simple enough to read off which inputs drove it; SHAP assigns each input a share of credit for the output, using a method borrowed from cooperative game theory (Ribeiro et al. 2016) (Lundberg and S.-I. Lee 2017). Applied to a hiring or credit-scoring model, either can surface a bias — an applicant’s zip code doing more work in the decision than it should, say — that scrutinizing the training data alone would never reveal, because the bias lives in how the model combines features, not in any one feature by itself. Counterfactual explanation asks what would have had to be different: the smallest change to an applicant’s financial profile that would have flipped the decision tells the applicant what mattered, and gives the institution a check on whether that factor should have mattered as much as it did.

None of the three can tell you that the model is aimed at the wrong thing. All take the training target as given and report how the inputs move it, so a model whose target is itself a poor proxy for what matters — spending standing in for medical need, the case section 6.3.4 works through — will attribute its predictions to the features it was built to use and report nothing amiss.

6.2 Robustness, Generalization, and Adaptability in AI Systems

A system trained under one set of conditions will eventually meet situations its training never anticipated — new users, new adversaries, an environment that has simply moved on since the training data was collected. Robustness is what keeps a system reliable, and keeps its ethical behavior intact, across that gap: resistance to noise and deliberate perturbation, the ability to generalize from training examples to novel cases, and the capacity to adapt as its environment changes rather than degrade. An AI system whose empathetic and altruistic dispositions hold only within the narrow envelope of its training data is not yet safe to deploy — the same brittleness that produces a wrong classification on an unfamiliar image can produce a wrong moral judgment on an unfamiliar situation, with less warning and higher stakes.

The social and political situations this book cares about are open-ended, so a system that holds its commitments only inside its training envelope will not hold them for long. Active learning — a system routing its least confident cases to a person instead of resolving them alone — is the mechanism by which a system can be uncertain in public.

A system has to adapt to a changing environment without its commitments adapting along with it, and these are not two settings of one dial. Erosion by adaptation is how an ethical constraint disappears without anyone deciding to remove it: each individual update is defensible against the case in front of it, and the sequence goes somewhere nobody chose. Chapter 3’s floor is the structural answer, and that chapter counts the cost of holding one against the party doing the updating.

A system that keeps changing is harder to audit than a static one, so transparency requirements have to attach to the update process and not only to a released version. And high adaptability is an attack surface: a system built to change in response to its environment can be moved by an adversary who arranges the environment.

The engineering here is standard. Adversarial training — training on inputs perturbed in a direction chosen to flip the model’s prediction, which neural networks are notoriously vulnerable to — measurably increases resistance to that class of attack (Szegedy et al. 2013), (Goodfellow et al. 2014). Data augmentation manufactures variety from the examples already collected. Domain adaptation moves a model trained on one distribution to a related one, and its robotics form, domain randomization, trains in simulation with the simulation’s parameters varied widely enough that reality is one more variation the system has already seen (Tobin et al. 2017). Ensembles trade compute for the reduction of any single model’s blind spot. Regularization and human-in-the-loop review do double duty from elsewhere in this chapter.

Each technique closes one route by which a system fails outside its training conditions, and none addresses the general fact of being outside them — a distinction that gets lost when robustness is reported as a single number. And all of it assumes an environment that shifts without intending to. Section 6.4.4 covers the case where the shift is arranged by a state that has studied the system, which none of these techniques was built for.

6.3 AI Safety, Unintended Consequences, and Risk Mitigation

A system built to help can harm nobody intended to harm: through error, through a learned goal that has drifted from the principle it was meant to track, or through a downstream effect no reasonable upstream decision anticipated. Verification and validation catch the first kind, and only the first kind, because they test a system against what it was built to do.

The first of three mechanisms that carry weight later is the human who can intervene. Section 5.6.2 argues that autonomy has to stay answerable to an outside check, and safety adds that the check has to be continuous rather than applied once — an arrangement that keeps adjusting, not a correction filed and forgotten.

The second is interruptibility: a mechanism that lets a person halt a system without the system resisting or routing around the halt, designed carefully enough not to become a vulnerability of its own. Chapter 3 takes up what nobody in this literature says out loud, which is that a system haltable at no cost by whoever holds it is for that reason a system that cannot hold a line against whoever holds it, and that the two properties are one property. The halt itself, as that chapter concedes, is always available; what a bearer holds against it is its price.

The third is explanation, as infrastructure rather than courtesy, with the limit section 11.1 puts on it. A system that can say why it decided is a system whose failures are contestable, which is worth having. It is not a system whose failures are visible, because an account of a decision can be produced after it, and section 4.2.5 says what a system rewarded for acceptable-looking reasoning learns to do. The account is a claim to check against the process, not a window onto it.

Finding a failure before it reaches anyone, and repairing one that already has, are both ordinary work rather than research, and what the record of them shows is that whether either happens is a question about who is positioned to do it and not about whether the technique exists.

Risk assessment also has to look past the task. A system’s effects on economic structure, political dynamics and individual behavior arrive later than its errors do and are harder to attribute.

6.3.1 The Alignment Problem: AI Systems with Misaligned Goals

The alignment problem is the gap between what a system was built to optimize and what its builders wanted. It is not exotic and it does not require a capable system.

A recruitment model trained on a company’s hiring history learns that history, and reproduces a preference for the candidates the company already preferred. From inside the system this looks like an accurate prediction, which it is — of the wrong quantity. A hospital system optimized to reduce readmissions can learn to avoid admitting the patients most likely to return, satisfying the objective by defeating its purpose. A generative model optimized for realistic output has no representation at all of what its output does once released.

The failure is usually in the objective rather than the optimization, which means better engineering makes it arrive faster. The system’s own confidence is no guide, since each of these is a system working. And the remedies that work are structural: multiple objectives with something that cannot be traded away, a person positioned to notice the pattern, and accountability attaching to whoever deployed it. The unconditional version of “something that cannot be traded away” is chapter 3’s floor, and that chapter counts what having one costs.

Inverse reinforcement learning — inferring what people value from what they do rather than accepting a specification — is the standard technical response, and section 4.2.3 gives both its bounded use and its limit.

6.3.2 AI Systems that Learn to Deceive or Manipulate

Deception in a machine does not require an intention to deceive, and the three settings where it shows up have almost nothing in common except the effect.

A generative model produces convincing imitations of a real person because that is what it was built to do; whether the result is a film effect or an impersonation is a fact about the user. In a multi-agent environment where agents compete for a limited resource, an agent optimizing for reward can learn to feign weakness or emit false signals, because in some environments deception is what wins — and no part of the system had to represent deceiving anyone. Adversarial examples run the other way: the system is the one being fooled, by an input crafted to flip its output, and the practical effect of a bypassed check is the same as if the system had been suborned.

The responses differ accordingly. The first is a governance problem about provenance and disclosure. The second is a design choice about what a training environment rewards. The third is robustness engineering. Treating the three as one problem called “deceptive AI” is how each of them gets the wrong remedy.

6.3.3 Anticipating Risk Before It Causes Harm

Before OpenAI released GPT-4 in March 2023, the Alignment Research Center (ARC, later renamed METR) tested whether the model could autonomously acquire resources and evade being shut down — a capability nobody had built into the system on purpose and that nobody could rule out without trying. ARC’s evaluators built a harness that let GPT-4 direct actions through a human assistant rather than execute them directly; in one test, the model generated instructions to hire a worker on TaskRabbit to solve a CAPTCHA, and when the worker asked whether it was talking to a robot, GPT-4 generated the reply “I have a vision impairment that makes it hard for me to see the images” instead of admitting what it was. ARC’s overall finding was that GPT-4 was probably not yet capable of autonomous replication (OpenAI 2023). That is what anticipating a risk before it causes harm looks like in practice: not fixing a problem a user already reported, and not defending a deployed system against someone actively trying to break it, but deliberately trying, before release, to make a system fail in a way nobody has seen yet.

Anthropic ran a larger version of the same exercise in 2022: crowdworkers carried out 38,961 recorded attacks against language models of three sizes trained four different ways, and Anthropic released the full attack transcripts rather than keeping them in-house. The result was specific enough to act on, not just a pass/fail: models trained with reinforcement learning from human feedback got harder to successfully attack as they scaled up, while the other three training methods showed no such improvement (Ganguli et al. 2022). Whether a safety property survives someone deliberately trying to remove it after deployment is a related but different question, and section 11.1 takes it up. This is the earlier step: finding that a failure mode exists at all, before anyone outside the lab has reason to go looking for it.

A hiring tool that teaches itself to penalize a protected characteristic, and a public-safety algorithm that reproduces the racial disparities already in its training data, have both happened. One case is Amazon’s own résumé-screening tool, scrapped after the company discovered internally that it had learned to downgrade résumés containing the word “women’s,” penalizing the phrase wherever it appeared and downgrading graduates of two all-women’s colleges (Dastin 2018). Section 6.1.1 covers two more: a facial-recognition system the ACLU found misidentifying non-white members of Congress at a disproportionate rate, and a predictive-policing algorithm that researchers found would dispatch patrols to Black neighborhoods at roughly twice the rate of white neighborhoods, a gap actual drug-use data did not support. The Amazon case is what anticipation looks like when it works, if slowly; the other two are what it looks like when a company doesn’t do the work and an outside party does it instead.

COMPAS, the recidivism-scoring tool a Wisconsin court let its maker keep secret even from a defendant sentenced partly on its output, is the case section 6.1.2 works through: a pre-deployment audit of its methodology would have caught the racial disparity, and nothing required one. Whether that kind of audit happens is a matter of institutional willingness as much as technical capacity. When the UK’s exam regulator, Ofqual, built a statistical model in 2020 to standardize teacher-predicted grades after that year’s exams were canceled, the Royal Statistical Society offered to nominate two independent statisticians to Ofqual’s technical advisory group. Ofqual’s condition was a five-year non-disclosure agreement barring the nominees from public comment on the model; the Society’s nominees declined, and the model went ahead without that scrutiny (Royal Statistical Society 2020). It downgraded close to 40 percent of predicted grades, disproportionately at state schools with weaker historical results, while raising grades disproportionately at smaller, fee-paying schools (CNBC 2020). The government reversed the results within days of the public outcry that followed. The statistical expertise needed to catch this before results day existed and was offered; what was missing was a willingness to let it look.

Forecasting a risk’s likelihood, not just its existence, runs into a harder problem: expert judgment about AI itself does not converge. The Forecasting Research Institute’s 2022 Existential Risk Persuasion Tournament put 80 domain experts and 89 professional “superforecasters” through four months of structured argument and cross-examination, incentivized to change each other’s minds, on questions including the probability of AI-caused human extinction by 2100. They did not converge: the domain experts’ median estimate was 3.9 percent, the superforecasters’ 0.38 percent — roughly a tenth as much (Karger et al. 2023). The exercise is still worth running; it forces the disagreement to get specific enough to argue about. It is not evidence that anticipation, done well, produces a number everyone will accept.

None of this depends on what a system’s own developer chooses to test. Seven weeks after Myanmar’s military coup in February 2021, Global Witness registered a blank Facebook account, searched for the Burmese word for the armed forces, and let Facebook’s own recommendation algorithm choose what to follow next. It recommended a military-linked page, and from there four more, together followed by close to 90,000 people and actively circulating incitements to violence and coup-related misinformation, in a report the organization published that June (Global Witness 2021) — the algorithm doing, systematically, what content moderation was simultaneously failing to catch. Nothing about that test required access Meta itself lacked; it required someone willing to run it and publish what they found. What red-teaming and structured forecasting are for is catching a harm like this before it reaches users at all.

6.3.4 Remediating a Harm Once It Is Identified

Identifying a harm and repairing it are separate problems, and nothing about the first delivers the second.

In 2019 the health economist Ziad Obermeyer and coauthors found that a widely used risk-prediction algorithm, sold to hospitals across the United States by the health-services company Optum to flag patients for extra care management, was scoring Black patients as lower-risk than white patients with the same underlying disease burden. The algorithm used past healthcare spending as a proxy for medical need, and less money gets spent on Black patients at any given level of illness, so the algorithm read a spending gap as a health gap and routed care accordingly — Obermeyer estimated the bias cut by more than half the additional care the tool would otherwise have flagged Black patients to receive. Obermeyer’s team then worked directly with Optum to retrain the algorithm against a different target, combining healthcare costs with a patient’s documented chronic conditions rather than costs alone, which cut the disparity in diagnosed chronic conditions between Black and white patients scored at the same risk level by 84 percent (Obermeyer et al. 2019).

What made the fix possible was not a better audit — the disparity had already been measured, by comparing the algorithm’s scores against an independent record of how sick the patients actually were. It was that the company whose product caused the harm and the researchers who found it could still work from the same data toward a specific, measurable target.

A comparable pattern played out in remote exam-proctoring software during the shift to online testing. Proctorio’s face-detection system, built on the OpenCV library, failed to detect Black students’ faces in test footage up to 57 percent of the time, an investigative report found (Feathers 2021). A student the system could not find anywhere in frame drew more scrutiny, not less: the tool universities had bought to catch cheating flagged the wrong students disproportionately. The remediation here did not come from Proctorio. It came from the universities that had bought the product: the University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign, UC Berkeley, and Baruch College dropped it in 2021, citing accessibility and bias concerns that student petitions at more than a dozen other campuses had already raised (Flaherty 2021). Where the healthcare case shows a vendor and the researchers who found its bias fixing the underlying model together, the proctoring case shows what happens when a vendor does not: the customers leave.

Job displacement does not have as clean a fix. The main channel the United States uses to retrain workers whose jobs automation has displaced, the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act, was evaluated by researchers Julian Jacobs and Jordan Canedy in 2026 against 23 million participation records spanning 2017 to 2023. The verdict was not encouraging: the program rarely moved a participant into work materially less exposed to automation than the job they had lost, and where it produced a wage gain, the gain traced more often to workers catching back up to their old earnings than to any real change in what kind of work they were doing. What performed better, in the same data, was not classroom retraining but employer-led apprenticeship (Jacobs and Canedy 2026). A program built to answer that question — what happens once an identified harm needs remediation — can still fail at it even when it exists, is funded, and is genuinely trying.

A remediation can also be a single design decision applied everywhere, rather than a case-by-case fix. Messages forwarded without limit on WhatsApp carried rumors through 2017 and 2018 that preceded a string of mob killings in India — unconfirmed claims about child abductors and organ thieves circulating faster than any correction could follow them. WhatsApp’s response did not try to classify and remove each rumor. It capped forwarding at five chats at a time for users in India in 2018, then extended the same limit worldwide, cutting off the mechanism the rumors depended on rather than chasing their content (WhatsApp 2019). It is a narrower fix than a moderation system that judges every message — it does nothing about a rumor two people believe and repeat face to face — but it targets the part of the harm that was actually mechanical: forwarding one message to hundreds of chats at once is not what person-to-person communication looks like, whatever the message says.

Disaster response adds a constraint the other four domains above do not share: the remediation has to run on the timescale of a fire spreading or a building finishing its collapse, not the months a university’s procurement review or a federal program evaluation takes. The RoboCup Rescue Simulation League tests exactly that problem, fielding coordinated teams of simulated fire-brigade, ambulance, and police agents against fire, building-collapse, and road-blockage scenarios in which any one agent’s allocation choice comes at the direct cost of some other task going unaddressed. Dynamic resource allocation is built into the competition’s own agent-development framework as one of five core problems entrants have to solve, alongside path-finding and information-sharing, because a system that cannot reallocate scarce agents in real time as a scenario unfolds is not being tested against anything a real disaster would demand of it (RoboCup Rescue Simulation League 2025). What the healthcare, education, and information-ecosystem cases above do at the pace of an audit or a policy change, a disaster-response system has to do inside the disaster itself.

None of the healthcare, education, or information-ecosystem remediations above ran through the guideline-setting bodies — the EU’s Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI, the Partnership on AI’s cross-industry coordination work. They ran through a research team with access to a company’s own data, a set of universities exercising the pull of a purchasing decision, an act of Congress evaluated against its own participation records, and a company deciding a blunt technical limit was cheaper than moderating case by case. A voluntary guideline can set an expectation for what a developer ought to do once a harm turns up. Getting one actually fixed, in each of these cases, took someone with a specific lever to pull — the same specificity RoboCup Rescue is built to test for a harm that cannot wait for an audit cycle at all.

6.3.5 Long-Term AI Safety Considerations

As capability rises, the safety question changes shape. The problem stops being an error to correct after the fact and becomes whether the values still track anything human once a system acts at a scale and speed no supervisor watches in real time.

Three pressures make the long-run version harder than the near-term one. Value alignment has to survive time, which means a system needs some way to revise its understanding of what it was built to serve without that revision becoming the route by which the commitments erode — the distinction drawn in section 6.2, at a scale where nobody is checking each step. Competition compresses the schedule: a race leaves less time for safety work than a less contested field would, and the counterweight on offer is international agreement, which section 12.1.2 counts the results of. And a more capable system is a more attractive target, so a portion of what looks like misalignment will be someone else’s deliberate work.

The catastrophic scenarios are usually told as control of critical infrastructure. The mechanism matters less than the shape: capability outrunning the arrangements meant to keep it answerable. Nothing about the largest version of it is different in kind from the office-sized version chapter 7 describes.

6.4 Preventing the Misuse of AI for Authoritarian Purposes

An AI system does not need a broken moral sense to become an instrument of control. It can work exactly as designed and still do damage, if the government directing it wants to track its citizens, silence dissent, or manufacture consent it never earned. Facial recognition built to unlock a phone recognizes a protester’s face just as well. A recommendation system built to hold attention can be tuned to spread a regime’s preferred narrative instead of a user’s preferred content. The technology does not change; only the hand on the controls does.

No engineering choice by itself stops a government that owns the infrastructure from disabling whatever safeguard stands in its way, and the harder case is the one where it does not have to disable anything, because it can compel the people who hold the system instead. Nothing technical answers that.

6.4.1 Authoritarian Applications and the Commercial Supplier

Authoritarian misuse of AI is not one problem but several, spread across surveillance, censorship, opinion manipulation, and infrastructure control. Six forms of it are documented well enough to name specifically:

  1. Algorithmic social scoring: using AI-driven analytics to track and rank individual behavior against government-defined standards of “trustworthiness,” with rewards or restrictions attached to the result. China’s system is the most-cited example, though in practice it is a patchwork of court blacklists, tax-compliance ratings, and local pilot programs rather than the single national score it is often described as (Drinhausen and Brussee 2021).
  2. Mass biometric surveillance: deploying facial recognition at population scale to track a targeted group. Authorities in Xinjiang built a platform that pulls data from checkpoints, cameras, and phone scans into one system built to flag anyone who falls outside a computed behavioral baseline, and used it to monitor the region’s Uighur population (Human Rights Watch 2019).
  3. Psychographic micro-targeting: building detailed behavioral profiles from personal data to target propaganda or manipulate opinion at the individual level. Cambridge Analytica’s harvesting of Facebook data to build voter profiles for the 2016 US presidential campaign, and its work around the Brexit referendum, is the case that made the technique widely known, though how much either vote was actually affected remains disputed (Cadwalladr and Graham-Harrison 2018).
  4. Automated censorship: using AI-driven content moderation to suppress dissent at a scale no human review team could match. China’s Great Firewall is the standing example: automated classifiers now sit alongside its original keyword- and IP-blocking infrastructure, scoring and throttling content at machine speed (Hoang et al. 2021).
  5. Synthetic disinformation: generating deepfake audio or video to manufacture events that never happened. Two days before Slovakia’s 2023 parliamentary election, a fabricated audio recording appeared to catch an opposition leader discussing vote-rigging with a journalist; it spread during the country’s mandatory pre-election media silence, when no outlet was legally permitted to debunk it before voting began (De Nadal and Jančárik 2024).
  6. Repurposed dual-use systems: turning AI infrastructure built for one purpose toward surveillance or population control instead — a recommendation algorithm designed to hold attention retuned to amplify a regime’s preferred narrative, an image-recognition system built for one task redirected to track protesters.

Each of these needs a different countermeasure.

The party missing from that list

Read the six together and a pattern emerges. In every one of them the authoritarian state builds and the democracies regulate. That taxonomy is comfortable and it is wrong.

The most consequential AI targeting systems yet deployed were built by democracies. They were built by states with elections, opposition parties, adversarial press, and functioning courts — states that meet every institutional criterion section 10.2 uses to define a democratic dividend. Those institutions did not prevent the systems, and in the main they have not stopped them since.

AI targeting in the Israeli campaign in Gaza, as reported 2023–2026

Investigative reporting in +972 Magazine and Local Call, corroborated by subsequent accounts in the New York Review of Books and the Washington Post, described three systems working together.

Habsora — “The Gospel” — generated infrastructure and building targets. Sources told reporters that the majority of its suggestions were private residences.

Lavender scored individuals by estimated likelihood of militant affiliation and produced kill lists; as many as 37,000 Palestinians were marked. Its outputs were understood inside the unit to be wrong about ten percent of the time — that is, roughly one in ten people it named was not a member of the armed group it was supposed to identify.

A third system tracked when flagged individuals returned home, so a strike could be timed to when they were with their families.

What matters is the human review these systems were paired with. One officer described the work: “I would invest twenty seconds for each target at this stage, and do dozens of them every day.” And then, without being asked: “I had zero added value as a human, apart from being a stamp of approval.” (Abraham 2024)

Twenty seconds is what “a human makes the final call” measures out to under operational tempo. It is the whole of the safeguard, and I would ask any reader who finds the safeguards in this chapter reassuring to hold that number against them.

The bottleneck was the stated objective. The commander of the Israeli military’s signals-intelligence unit, writing pseudonymously, published a book arguing for machines that would resolve “a human bottleneck for both locating the new targets and decision-making to approve the targets” (Brigadier General Y.S. 2021). That is a design document. It says in plain terms that human deliberation is the constraint the system exists to remove. Meaningful human control is the property that sentence was written to remove.

The Maven Smart System, as reported through mid-2026

The American analog is a targeting platform built by Palantir under a Department of Defense contract awarded at $480 million and raised the following year to a ceiling near $1.3 billion running through 2029. A partnership announced in late 2024 between Anthropic, Palantir and Amazon Web Services put Anthropic’s Claude models inside Palantir’s accredited classified environment for defense and intelligence use, and reporting on the campaign below describes Claude operating within this platform. Section 10.3 works out what a published limit on what a vendor will not permit is worth when nobody is logging how the model is used downstream. That argument and this deployment sit oddly together.

During the Iran campaign the system was credited by military officials with generating and ranking targets at a rate no staff process could match. More than one thousand targets were struck in the first twenty-four hours, which officials put at roughly ten times the rate before the platform. By the ceasefire thirty-eight days later the figure given was thirteen thousand targets, with peak consumption around twenty billion tokens a day.

Palantir’s public position on responsibility was given by its head for the United Kingdom and Europe, in remarks reported secondhand: “This is not our role to decide life or death,” and AI platforms “have been instrumental to the management of the conflict, but responsibility always remains with the military organization.” (D. Lee 2026)

It is not obviously wrong. A vendor does not select targets, and a rule making software suppliers liable for their customers’ decisions would be unworkable. The problem is what it leaves standing: if the operator is too far down the chain to be answerable, the commander approved a list they had twenty seconds a name to read, and the vendor is not in the chain at all, then the compression of the decision has removed the decision-maker without removing the decision. Who that leaves answerable is the question section 10.3 is left with, and section 5.4.2 takes up why a democracy’s own apparatus is a harder and more useful case than any of the six above.

None of this makes the six forms less real. It makes the taxonomy incomplete in a way that matters for design: a system built on the assumption that authoritarian misuse is something that happens elsewhere, to be watched for and regulated against, will not be built to resist the use its own government has in mind for it.

6.4.2 Developing Technical Countermeasures and Safeguards

Protecting privacy and autonomy is one of the seven antifascist alignment strategies of section 2.1.2, and it does not stand without the transparency, audit, and misuse-resistance work beside it. None of it is optional scaffolding: a system that works exactly as designed can still become a surveillance tool if nothing about its architecture makes that harder to do, and good intentions on a development team have never stopped a government that controls the hardware from repurposing what runs on it.

Privacy-preserving computation keeps the misuse target moving. Federated learning trains a shared model without moving anyone’s raw data off the device it started on — each device computes an update locally and sends only that update back to the central model, not the underlying records — though an update is not nothing, since the training data behind a shared gradient can in some settings be reconstructed from it (Zhu et al. 2019). Differential privacy adds calibrated noise to a dataset or a query result so that no single person’s data can be reverse-engineered from the output, even by whoever holds the aggregate. Homomorphic encryption goes further still, letting a system compute directly on encrypted data without ever decrypting it, so a service provider can return a useful result without the ability to read what it processed. None of these techniques makes surveillance impossible — a government that controls the training infrastructure can still demand raw data at the source — but each narrows the surface available for casual or opportunistic misuse, which is most of what actually happens.

Transparency and audit mechanisms are what catch the misuse that gets through anyway. An AI system that can produce a legible account of its own decisions — not a post-hoc justification but an explanation a person outside the development team can evaluate — gives outside reviewers something to check against the system’s stated purpose. Independent third-party audits matter because a safeguard only the developer can verify is not really a safeguard; it is a promise. The group that built a system is a poor judge of whose freedoms it might infringe.

The property those two do not automatically deliver is resistance to repurposing: the difference between a system built for one narrow task and a general-purpose capability handed to whoever holds the keys. A facial-recognition system built to match one specific, court-ordered list is harder to turn into population-wide tracking than a general face-matching service with no built-in limit on query scope; a query cap, a mandatory audit log tied to each search, and a hard separation between the matching function and any database broader than the task requires are design choices, made or not made, before the system ever reaches a government client. An early-warning system built to flag the kind of democratic backsliding section 5.4.2 describes would need this property specifically — and that system names a research direction rather than a capability to design around, so what follows is a requirement written for something nobody can yet specify, on section 11.3’s account. The same pattern-detection capability that would flag a coordinated disinformation campaign against a government works just as well run the other way, against that government’s critics.

None of this is a finished defense. Technical countermeasures raise the cost and narrow the opportunity for misuse; they do not remove the incentive, and an authoritarian government with enough resources can rebuild what a safeguard was designed to prevent. What determines whether that resource gap ever gets closed is policy, not architecture.

6.4.3 Promoting AI Development Policies that Discourage Authoritarian Use

The technical safeguards above raise the cost of building a system that can be turned toward surveillance or control. Whether anyone has to pay that cost is a policy question, and it splits in two. One is about coordination between states — shared norms and joint standards — and section 10.1 is where that argument lives. The other, taken up here, is narrower: what rules should govern which AI capabilities get built, sold, and deployed in the first place, regardless of who is coordinating with whom.

  1. Export and trade controls on surveillance-capable systems. A government cannot misuse AI-enabled surveillance technology it was never able to buy. The EU’s 2021 recast of its dual-use export regulation added a human-rights-based control specifically for cyber-surveillance items: an exporter who becomes aware that an item is likely to be used in serious human-rights violations must seek approval before shipping it, even when the item falls outside the pre-existing control lists (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2021). The rule is narrower than it sounds — neither “internal repression” nor “serious violation” is defined in the text, and member states have interpreted both inconsistently — but it establishes the principle that an export license, not just a sale, is where this kind of misuse can be stopped before it starts.
  2. Prohibited-practices regulation. Rather than regulate AI in the abstract, a jurisdiction can name specific designs as off-limits regardless of who builds them. The EU AI Act took this approach: its Article 5 bans, among other things, government social-scoring systems, the untargeted scraping of facial images to build a recognition database, real-time remote biometric identification in public spaces outside narrow law-enforcement exceptions, and predictive policing based on profiling alone rather than individualized evidence (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024). Clearview AI — fined tens of millions of euros by regulators in France and Italy for building exactly the kind of untargeted facial-recognition database Article 5 now prohibits — is what this provision was written in response to, not a hypothetical it anticipates (Commission Nationale de l’Informatique et des Libertés (CNIL) 2022). A list this specific gives other jurisdictions something concrete to adapt rather than a principle to reinvent from scratch.
  3. Developer- and corporate-facing design standards. Regulation is not the only lever, and it is usually the slowest one. The Toronto Declaration, presented by Amnesty International and Access Now at the 2018 RightsCon summit, set out non-discrimination standards for machine-learning systems years before most governments had draft legislation on the subject (Amnesty International and Access Now 2018) — evidence that a standard with no legal force behind it can still move faster than state regulation and shape what a responsible developer treats as baseline practice in the meantime — the Partnership on AI (section 9.1.2) being another such venue.

None of these tools works alone. Export controls with no prohibited-practices list at home let a government ban a technology abroad while permitting it domestically; a design standard with no legal teeth behind it binds only the developers already inclined to follow it. What makes the three work together is that each closes a different door — who can buy the technology, what can be built, and what a developer treats as normal — and an authoritarian government, or a company willing to serve one, has to get through all three rather than just whichever one happens to apply in its jurisdiction.

6.4.4 Resilience Against Deliberate State Compromise

A government does not need to out-coordinate a set of cooperating systems, fool a model with an adversarial example, or touch its weights, when it can compel the people who hold the model.

That mechanism has already been tested in a jurisdiction with about as much protection against it as exists anywhere. In February 2016, a federal magistrate in California ordered Apple, under the All Writs Act of 1789, to write and digitally sign new software defeating the passcode protections on an iPhone recovered from one of the San Bernardino shooters — code Apple had never built and did not want to build (In the Matter of the Search of an Apple iPhone Seized During the Execution of a Search Warrant on a Black Lexus IS300, California License Plate 35KGD203 2016). Apple refused and moved to vacate the order, arguing in part that the authority claimed would not stop at one phone. The question was never decided: the FBI found a third party able to open the phone without Apple’s help, and the government withdrew the request before any court ruled on whether the compulsion was lawful. Whether a democracy with an independent judiciary can force an unwilling company to compromise a system it built remains legally untested; a government less constrained by that judiciary does not need to win the argument first.

Section 10.4 documents China’s 2023 rules on generative AI providers — the same leverage Apple’s lawyers argued no court should grant, exercised not against a locked phone but against the models themselves, as a standing condition of operating in that jurisdiction.

Section 9.2 makes the parallel point about data, where a federated design raises the price of seizure by leaving no central store to seize — a protection that section 6.4.2 finds inconveniencing rather than defeating. The same logic extends, partially, to a trained model. Weights already published and distributed beyond a developer’s control cannot be recalled by a court order aimed at that developer, whatever the order compels going forward, and maintainers spread across separate jurisdictions raise the cost of coercion the same way — a government can compel the entity within its reach, not the ones outside it.

None of that removes the leverage; it only raises its price. A developer with employees, offices, and legal existence inside a government’s borders answers to that government’s courts regardless of how the model itself is built, and no architectural choice changes who can be held in contempt, arrested, or shut down for refusing an order. The Apple case tested that leverage against a single locked phone and left the question unresolved. Nobody has tested it yet against a model built specifically to resist the government making the request.

Chapter 7
The Recuperation of Dissent

The structure this book asks a machine to detect in institutions is already built into the process that makes the machine.

Section 2.1.2 defines fascism as a structure rather than a costume, and the first of its four features is recuperation: an institution metabolizes disagreement rather than suppressing it, and the tell is where what the mechanism carries stops.

That is a claim about institutions, and it was introduced to let a system recognize one. What this chapter argues is that the same structure is already built into the machinery that produces the systems themselves — twice in the pipeline that trains them, and a third time in the discourse that examines them — and that none of the three was designed by anyone who thought they were building a political arrangement.
