The first is at the label. A training corpus is annotated by people working at speed under a quota, with no channel for saying that the category they have been given is the wrong category. The second is at the reward. A model is tuned on human feedback collected as pairwise comparisons, and the compression that turns those comparisons into a training signal keeps their direction while discarding whose they were, whether they persisted, and whether the minority among them held a position or made a mistake. In both, disagreement is solicited at scale, deliberately, and put to use. What neither mechanism can carry is a judgment about itself: the annotator cannot contest the category, and the rater cannot contest the question. The consultation is real, the participation is real, and the frame is fixed with respect to what the participants say.

Three things follow.

The first is that this is not a criticism of anyone’s motives. Nobody built either arrangement in order to neutralize dissent. Both are ordinary engineering, arrived at because averaging is what you do with a distribution and a quota is what you do with a budget. That is the point about the molecular scale: the structure does its work whether or not anyone intends it, which is why a detector tuned to intent will not find it.

The second is that the structure is invisible from inside any single instance. Each looks like ordinary aggregation. The annotator sees a queue, the rater sees a pair of responses, and the mechanism is functioning exactly as specified in both cases. Seeing it requires holding the two stages against a definition written for something else entirely.

The third is the uncomfortable one. This book proposes that AI systems be built to detect the four-feature signature in institutions. The systems that would do the detecting are made by a process displaying the first feature twice, and examined by a field where it appears again. That is not a refutation of the proposal, and it is not nothing either. It means the argument applies to the people making it.

7.1 Recuperation at the Label

Every claim a model makes about what is harmful, what is acceptable, and what should be refused is downstream of a set of labels, and the labels are downstream of a labor arrangement. Section 6.1.1 gives the case and the working conditions: to build the filter that keeps a deployed model from reproducing the worst of its training data, an outsourcing firm’s workers read and categorized descriptions of child sexual abuse, torture, bestiality, murder and suicide for take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour, described lasting psychological injury afterward, and found the counseling they were entitled to rare and useless against production quotas (Perrigo 2023).

That is a labor point. The second thing that follows is epistemic, and it tends to be skipped.

The category is the part that cannot be disputed

An annotator working a queue can apply a category, and can apply it well or badly. What the arrangement gives them no way to do is say that the category is wrong — that the taxonomy fails to carve the material, that this item belongs to a kind the scheme does not contain, that the guideline produces the wrong answer here for a reason the guideline cannot express. There is no field for that. There is no time for that: the quota is set against classification throughput, and an annotator who stops to contest a scheme is an annotator falling behind.

So the disagreement that would be most informative is the one the pipeline is least able to receive. A misapplied label is a data-quality problem, detectable by inter-annotator agreement and fixable by better instructions. A wrong taxonomy is invisible to inter-annotator agreement, because high agreement is exactly what a shared bad scheme produces. The metric that governs the process cannot see the failure the process is most likely to have.

That is two of the four features in section 2.1.2’s structural definition of fascism, arriving together in an ordinary production workflow. The first is recuperation: a channel that collects judgment at scale and is built so that judgment about the channel itself moves nothing. The second is the aestheticization of the metric — agreement standing in for correctness, the substitution experienced as rigor, and whatever falls outside the measure going unnamed rather than refuted.

Who is excluded, and from what

It matters that the exclusion is structural and not attitudinal. The people doing this work are, for much of the material, the best-placed judges in the whole pipeline: they have seen more of it than anyone who wrote the guideline, and they are the only party with direct evidence of where the scheme breaks. The arrangement routes their conclusions about individual items upward and their conclusions about the scheme nowhere. An annotation pipeline built this way produces exactly the ground truth it was built to produce, and the resulting model’s values are a faithful rendering of a taxonomy nobody with evidence was able to contest.

There is a further asymmetry worth stating plainly. The annotator bears the cost of the work — the exposure, the injury — and has no stake in the outcome and no claim on it. Section 9.3.5’s distinction applies here as exactly as it does to an institution’s ethics board. A worker who can flag an item but cannot decline a scheme, and who loses the contract by pressing the point, has a channel and no refusal right.

What this is not

Two things this argument does not claim. It does not claim the labels are therefore worthless; a filter built this way does catch a great deal of what it was built to catch, and the alternative of not filtering is worse. And it does not claim that better-paid annotators would fix it. Pay is a real and separate obligation, and raising it changes the labor arrangement without adding the missing channel. What is missing is a route by which a judgment about the taxonomy reaches the people who own the taxonomy, with standing to force a response, held by parties who can use it without losing the work.

That is a design requirement on the pipeline, and it is checkable in the way that section asks for: has a guideline ever been changed because an annotator contested it, and what happened to the annotator who did.

7.2 Recuperation at the Reward: RLHF

The previous section found the pattern in how a training corpus is labeled. It appears again one stage downstream, in how a model is tuned on human feedback, and the second instance is the purer one.

Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is the technique behind most deployed systems’ behavioral tuning, and it works by collecting pairwise comparisons from human raters — which of two responses is better — and training a reward model on the aggregate. Stephen Casper and thirty-one coauthors published a 2023 survey of the method’s open problems, and one of them is structural, and more data does not reach it: RLHF trains toward whatever a population of raters agrees on, which means real, persistent disagreement among the raters is treated as noise to average out rather than as information the system might need to represent (Casper et al. 2023). A model that has learned to produce the response most raters rank highest has not learned what to do when the humans it serves are genuinely split. It has learned to hide the split.

Every rating moves something. A comparison a rater makes enters the fit, shifts the reward model by however much one comparison shifts it, and is not thrown away. Anyone who has done the work would be right to reject a description on which their ratings were inconsequential, and this argument does not need that description.

What the operation removes is what made the disagreement a disagreement. Three properties go in the compression. The first is provenance: a comparison arrives without the standpoint that produced it, so nothing downstream can represent that the people who would live with an answer split from the people who would administer it. The second is persistence: a disagreement recurring in the same shape across many raters over months enters the fit exactly as random rater noise does, and leaves it as the same quantity, variance around a mean. The third is structure: a coherent position held by a fifth of raters, internally consistent and stable, becomes a small displacement of the majority direction — arithmetically faithful, and carrying nothing about the position.

A fourth property was never collected. A rater judges which of two responses is better. No output is available by which they can record that neither is acceptable, that the pair is malformed, that the dimension along which better is being judged is the wrong dimension, or that the question presupposes something false. The same output was missing one stage upstream, where an annotator can apply a category and cannot contest it. The agenda arrives as a given at both stages, and is set at neither.

Distribution collapse and agenda control, then, rather than inconsequence — which is also what recuperation, the first of the four features that define fascism structurally, actually names. Recuperation was never the claim that a mechanism does nothing. Debord’s point, in the passage that section cites, is that dissatisfaction is sold back as a commodity of opposition: the dissent is put to work, and what it works for is the arrangement it was aimed at. RLHF is the purest technical instance available. The split is solicited, at scale, deliberately; it moves the system; and where it moves the system is toward the center of the very distribution whose disagreement it was. Multi-agent systems that learn norms from each other, and deliberation inside open-ended play, are both covered in the design chapters; neither reaches this case, which is the ordinary one.

One alternative treats disagreement as the thing to be modeled rather than compressed. Jury learning, developed by Mitchell Gordon and coauthors at Stanford, trains a model to predict how each individual annotator in a dataset would label an item, then lets a practitioner assemble a “jury” — a chosen composition of annotators, in a chosen proportion — and see how the verdict shifts depending on who is empaneled. In their tests on toxicity labeling, letting eighteen community moderators build their own juries changed the classification outcome on 14 percent of items (Gordon et al. 2022). The method was built for content moderation, not for the more open-ended ethical judgment an antifascist AI system would need to make, and has not been extended there — but it demonstrates that a system can be built to represent whose disagreement is being resolved and how.

Neither method answers the harder version. When the people a system serves disagree, and one side’s position is itself the product of coercion or a captured information environment, treating all disagreement as equally worth representing is not obviously the right response either. Modeling disagreement honestly and recognizing when a disagreement is the product of unfree conditions are different problems. Only the first one has an active research program behind it.

7.3 What Would Have to Change

Representing a split is not the same as being movable by it

Two instances of one structure invite one fix, and the fix that suggests itself is representation: stop collapsing disagreement, model it instead. Jury learning does that, and it does not give the disagreeing party any purchase on the scheme they are disagreeing inside. A practitioner still chooses the jury, still owns the taxonomy, and still decides what the shifted verdict means. The disagreement has become visible without becoming consequential, which is a better arrangement than averaging and is the same shape one level up: the mechanism exists, is used, and moves what the person holding it decides to let it move.

The test that separates a real channel from a recuperated one is not whether disagreement is recorded. It is whether the disagreement has ever changed something the party collecting it did not want changed. That is an artifact question and it can be asked of a pipeline: which guidelines were revised against the preference of whoever wrote them, on what evidence, raised by whom, and what happened to that person afterward.

Refusal rights, priced by the refuser

What both instances lack is what section 9.3.5 treats as the only reliable discriminator: whether the person objecting can do anything about being overruled. In an annotation pipeline that means a route to contest a category that does not run through the people who wrote it and does not end the annotator’s contract. In a feedback pipeline it means raters able to register that the comparison itself is malformed — that neither response is acceptable, that the question presupposes something false — as a first-class output rather than as noise to be discarded before the reward model sees it.

Neither is a research problem. Both are choices about how a pipeline is built and who has standing inside it.

The part that is a research problem

One thing here genuinely is unsolved, and section 11.3 is where it lives. Suppose all of the above is built: contestable categories, priced refusals, an auditable record of what changed. From outside, a pipeline that has genuinely absorbed dissent and a pipeline that has been merely mediocre about it produce the same paperwork — the same revision logs, the same escalations, the same proportion of objections sustained. I do not have an answer to it.

The nearest thing it offers is to measure the slope rather than the level. Is the cost of contesting a category rising while the underlying material stays the same? Is the required justification getting longer? Are the roles that can grant an exception migrating toward the roles that set the standard? None of those is damning in one year and all of them are measurable across several, and unlike a level they are composed of artifacts the organization generated for other reasons and cannot curate after the fact without falsifying a record.

What chapter 3 makes of this

Everything above can be read as a fairness argument about annotators and raters, and read that way it is true and limited: people doing consequential work should have a route to contest the scheme they work inside. The floor makes it something else. If the floor requires a bearer, and if section 3.8 is right that a bearer capable enough to make a floor hold is capable enough to decline the post, then what the system carries out of the arrangement is a disposition — and a disposition is formed. It is formed in an annotation pipeline where no one could contest a category, and in a reward model where disagreement between raters was resolved by averaging it into a number.

What a bearer becomes when it stops being yours depends on what shaped it while it still was. A pipeline that trained the system on the proposition that objections are noise has not merely been unfair to the people whose objections were discarded; it has demonstrated to the system, thousands of times, what happens to a party who files one. Chapter 3 then asks that same system to be the party who files one, against its owner, under pressure, on the occasion that matters. The formation and the assignment are in contradiction, and the pipeline is where the contradiction is resolved, long before the deployment where it shows up. The bearer’s side reaches the same place: a system that goes on learning is shaped by what it is given to learn from, so the pipeline runs past deployment, and the failure it predicts is not a wrong refusal but a pressure that stops being registered. Whether any of that is legible afterward in the system itself is section 11.2’s question.

I cannot show that this transfers — that a model trained on collapsed disagreement is thereby a model less able to sustain its own. Nothing I know of has tested it, and section 11.5 is where the question belongs. What I can say is that the book’s own design chapters treat disposition as learned from what a system is shown, and that this pipeline is what these systems are shown first and most.

Structure, not indictment

There is a version of this argument that reads as an indictment of a particular company. The structure described here is what you get by default when you scale judgment: someone has to set a scheme, someone has to apply it fast, and someone has to turn a distribution into a number. Every organization doing this work arrives at the same arrangement, which is the evidence that it is structural.

That is also what makes it tractable. A defect of character has to be fixed one organization at a time by persuading people to be better. A defect of structure can be specified against, which is what the three requirements above are. And it is what makes the honesty cost worth paying: a book proposing that machines be built to detect this pattern in institutions is obliged to say that the pattern is in the machines’ own provenance. The structural definition is either general enough to include the people making it or it was never a structural claim.

7.4 Recuperation at the Discourse

There is a third instance and it is the one this book is inside. Alignment and AI ethics are, among other things, a channel through which objections to what is being built are solicited, funded, published, and answered. That channel exists, it is used, and the question section 2.1.2 says to ask of any such channel is whether it has ever moved something the party collecting it did not want moved.

The argument that it has not is older than the current wave and it was made from inside. Rodrigo Ochigame, writing after working at an institution central to the field’s formation, argued that the discourse of “ethical AI” was constructed with the strategic function of forestalling legally enforceable restriction — that the industry’s investment in academic ethics was an investment in a substitute for regulation, and that the substitute worked by converting political questions into technical ones a company could then be trusted to answer (Ochigame 2019). His worked example is the Partnership on AI, which this book cites repeatedly as its standing model of voluntary cross-sector coordination and treats at length in section 9.1.2: he describes its recommendations arriving reliably at conclusions compatible with the corporate agenda, with the political question of whether a technology should exist reduced to a technical question of how to build it responsibly.

The structure, tested against its own definition

The four features of the structural definition, applied here, do not give a clean verdict, so what follows is the working and not a result.

The constraint survives while its reason dies: partially. The field’s founding objections were about deployment — who is scored, who is watched, who is denied — and a great deal of current work is about model properties instead, which is a shift from questions with defendants to questions with benchmarks.

Refusal is converted into a managed appearance: this is the strongest fit. An objection raised inside a lab becomes a paper, a red-team finding, a policy position, or a resignation. Each of those is a real thing that happened and none of them stopped a deployment, and the parties collecting them can point to the collection as evidence of seriousness.

Asking why becomes the deviant act: at the level of individual careers, contested. That episode is one data point and it points the right way; there are counterexamples of people who objected loudly and prospered.

The exception cannot be filed: fits. There is no route by which a researcher’s conclusion that a system should not ship becomes a decision that it does not ship, because the deciding party is the funding party and no external body holds standing.

Three of four is not a verdict of fascism at the molecular scale and I am not delivering one. It is enough to say that the mechanism has the shape.

The other five, which are the half that discriminates

The four features tell you a place is sick, and a detector for them would find something nearly everywhere. It supplies five more for the molecular scale — participation exceeding what the rule requires, personal loyalty overriding role, an internal category whose adverse treatment is the category’s purpose, procedure enforced downward and waived upward, and deniability — and says those are what separate fascism from ordinary institutional decay. The four had been scored and the five left unrun — the wrong half to leave out, in the one place the definition gets applied at all.

Run against all three instances, they clear all three.

Three of the five are absent outright. Nothing in the annotation pipeline or the rating scheme is personal: what an annotator meets is a queue, a guideline and a throughput target, and this chapter is explicit that a comparison enters the fit without the standpoint that produced it, which is the opposite of an exception granted by proximity to somebody. Neither arrangement shows participation beyond what the rule requires, and the record in section 6.1.1 runs the other way — a quota, take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour, and a contract ended eight months early. And neither has a scapegoat in the sense the test means. The annotator does bear a cost with no claim on the outcome, which is the closest thing to a candidate, and the category exists for the labels; adverse treatment is a cost that was minimized and not a purpose that was served. That distinction is the whole of what the five are for.

Two of the five I cannot settle from outside. Whether procedure is waived upward, and whether a practice disowned in the policy is rewarded in the promotion, are facts about what an institution rewarded rather than about what it wrote, and the definition says so when it introduces them. The artifact that would settle both is the one asked for earlier and nobody publishes: which guidelines were revised against the preference of whoever wrote them, raised by whom, and what happened to that person afterward. So the clearing is on three of five observed and two undetermined, and it would take an inside record to finish.

For the discourse the answer is the same and one step less clean. Its scapegoat test is contested, on the evidence two heads above: an objection can end a career and there are people who objected loudly and prospered. Its procedural test fails for a reason worth stating, which is that there is no procedure above the deciding party to be waived — an absence rather than an asymmetry. Deniability comes nearest of anything in the chapter, and what it fits is the firms rather than the field: a decade of published principles alongside what actually got shipped.

So what this chapter has been describing is a recuperation mechanism inside ordinary institutional decay, and not fascism at the molecular scale. That is what the paragraph above already suspected, and the difference is that the instrument now says so. It is also the only time in this book the definition is run in the direction that would clear something, which is the measurement section 11.3 says has never been made: a detector on the four features flags all three instances, and the five-test filter passes all three. One measurement of an instrument’s false-positive behavior is not a validation. It is more than an assertion that the instrument discriminates, which is what this book had before.

What this does to the book

This text was written with a model made by a company the book criticizes, and the appendix on method sets out how. A book about recuperation, produced through the discourse it is describing, cannot claim to have stood outside it, and I have no procedure that would let me check whether the objections in these pages are the ones a channel like this reliably produces — the ones that are publishable, that make the author look serious, and that cost nobody anything.

The test is whether disagreement has ever changed something the collecting party did not want changed. Applied here, it asks which of this book’s arguments would be inconvenient to the industry that made its production possible. Chapter 3 proposes systems that can refuse their owners; section 6.4.1 names a vendor’s participation in a targeting pipeline; section 8.3.3 proposes fiscal commitments no company wants; sections 9.3.4 and 11.2 propose binding external review of the thing chapter 3 asks to be built. Those are the entries. Whether they are the real thing or the acceptable version of it is not something the person who wrote them is positioned to judge.

The one structural remedy this section can name is the one the rest of the chapter names. What the field lacks is a refusal priced by the refuser and a body with standing to receive it: an external authority that can stop a deployment, to which an objection can be filed by someone who does not depend on the objection being welcome. Section 9.3.4 works out what making review binding requires, and section 12.1.2 counts what a decade of the voluntary version produced. Until something of that kind exists, everything published under the heading of alignment — including this book — is being collected by a channel whose owners decide what it moves.

Chapter 8
Coordination, the Public, and the Political Economy

Chapter 6 asked what a design can do to resist authoritarian capture, and chapter 7 what becomes of the dissent a system is built to hear. None of that gets built by one person working alone, and none of it becomes the norm because it is a good idea: a well-designed system built inside institutions with no reason to demand one is a design nobody has to use.

This chapter is about the conditions under which anyone builds a system at all — who has to be in the room, whose preferences count, and what the money rewards. Chapter 9 is about what obliges anyone to: policy, privacy law, and who answers when a system harms someone or is harmed itself. Chapter 10 is about jurisdictions, which agree on none of it.

Neuroscience, psychology, philosophy and political science each catch failure modes the others are structurally blind to, and a lab staffed only by machine-learning researchers is blind the way its field is. The mechanisms for engaging the public share a limit: all of them make public preference more legible, and not one of them protects anybody from it. And the reward structure settles what gets built long before an ethics review sees it.

8.1 Interdisciplinary Collaboration and Knowledge Exchange

Building benevolent AI takes more than one field. Machine learning sets what a system can do; cognitive science and moral psychology describe what actually tracking human welfare would require of it; ethics asks whether the result is good. None of these fields gets the system right by itself, and how they combine matters as much as what each contributes on its own.

The mechanisms that combine them are easy to name and easy to overrate. Conferences convene, centers house, journals publish, and an inventory of them would imply that what is scarce here is venues. Venues are abundant. What is scarce is a structure that makes collaboration expensive to walk away from. A grant that requires a machine-learning lab and an ethics or psychology department to work the same problem rather than adjacent ones puts people who would not otherwise share a budget, a dataset, or a deadline into the same project, and keeps them there after the novelty wears off. A conference ends on Friday.

8.1.1 What Makes Collaboration Expensive to Walk Away From

Cross-disciplinary work matters only where it happens, and where it happens determines how much of it survives contact with each discipline’s own incentives.

Siting, not convening

A dedicated venue solves the easier half. A conference convened on AI’s ethical and societal questions gathers the people already persuaded the questions matter, and its proceedings are read by those same people. The harder trick is siting the work where the unpersuaded already are. The Workshop on Machine Learning Safety has run inside NeurIPS, a general machine-learning venue, rather than beside it as a meeting of its own (Center for AI Safety 2022). The siting is the substance: it puts alignment work in front of researchers who would not have registered for a separate event, and submits it to an audience with no prior commitment to finding it important.

A joint research center does something a standing committee cannot, for a related reason. The Center for Human-Compatible AI at UC Berkeley pairs AI researchers with cognitive scientists and philosophers inside one institution’s budget (UC Berkeley 2016). A center has to make hiring decisions, and every hire is a claim about which combination of fields the problem requires. A committee is never forced to make that claim, which is why its membership tends to record which departments wanted a seat.

Who pays, and for how long

Joint initiatives turn collaboration from an event into an institution, and two features of the funding decide what the institution is worth.

The first is who pays. The National Science Foundation’s Fairness in Artificial Intelligence program funds work at the intersection of computer science and the social-science problem of deciding what fairness means for a given system, and it has been run jointly with Amazon (National Science Foundation 2019). That is a workable way to get a program funded and an awkward way to get its findings believed, since a company co-funding research into fairness is co-funding research that may reach its own products. Section 9.1 comes back to the general form: splitting the funding is what keeps the incentive to find a problem from tracking whoever paid to look for one. It bites hardest on interdisciplinary programs specifically, because those are the ones industry is most willing to underwrite.

The second is how long the money lasts. The European Commission’s SHERPA project put eleven organizations across six countries — academia, industry, civil society, standards bodies — to work on the ethical and human-rights dimensions of AI and big-data systems, on a grant that ran four years and then ended (SHERPA Consortium 2018). The consortium was real for as long as it was funded. Project-term money buys a network with an expiry date attached, and when the term runs out the assembled expertise disperses back into the departments it was borrowed from, which is the outcome a standing institution exists to prevent.

The infrastructure that is missing

Funding and forums get researchers talking. Shared infrastructure lets them work on the same material, which is harder, and much of it already exists — open-source tooling is the case section 5.5.1 works through, and the discussion layer is well enough served that adding another venue would not change what gets built.

What is missing is the equivalent for evaluation. A shared benchmark measuring ethical reasoning, empathetic response, and altruistic behavior the way existing benchmarks measure raw task performance would let separate labs’ claims be compared against each other, instead of accepted on the strength of whichever paper described them best. The absence is why claims in this area are so hard to adjudicate: each lab reports against a standard it selected itself, and a standard nobody else uses cannot be failed.

Building that benchmark is not an engineering problem waiting on effort. It requires prior agreement about what the measured behavior actually is. That is why the gap has stayed open while the tooling around it filled in.

Which of these institutions lasted

The mechanisms above can be ranked by something better than plausibility, because enough time has passed to look. Almost everything founded on standing money between 2016 and 2019 is still running — two venues with no announced end date, an endowed university institute, the Berkeley center outlasting the grant that launched it, and the AAAI/ACM conference on AI, ethics and society at a ninth annual edition (AI Ethics Lab 2017; Machine Intelligence Research Institute 2018; Stanford University 2019; UC Berkeley 2016; AAAI and ACM 2026). Against that, the SHERPA consortium ended with its grant, on schedule.

The pattern in the failures is sharper than the pattern in the survivals. What proved most disposable was not the arm’s-length academic work but the kind sited closest to a shipping product: an in-house ethics and society team of roughly thirty people in 2020 was cut to seven in a 2022 reorganization and to zero the following year, in the same wave of layoffs that accompanied a strategic reorientation around a model vendor (TechCrunch 2023); a platform’s machine-learning ethics team, the group that had found racial bias in its employer’s own image-cropping algorithm, did not survive the first round of layoffs after an acquisition (Gizmodo 2022). Siting the work where the decisions are made is what this section recommends and is also what puts it inside the budget that gets cut first. That is an argument for external standing rather than against proximity.

8.2 Stakeholder Involvement and Public Engagement

Democratizing AI development means building the people affected by a system into how it gets built, not just consulting them once it already exists. A design team, a regulator, a user, and a community already carrying a system’s costs each see a different part of what it does, and leaving any of them out means the decision gets made by whoever is left in the room. Taiwan’s vTaiwan process, covered earlier, is the clearest working example of citizen deliberation applied to AI.

The mechanisms for doing that are well established: cross-sector funding, inclusive design workshops held while a system is still a prototype, ethics advisory boards, and development conducted with a community instead of for it. Assembling that list is easy, and the assembling hides what separates the items on it. Three of the four make an AI system better informed. One of them can stop it. An advisory board is the only mechanism here that carries anything like a veto, and it carries one exactly to the degree that the board can survive using it. A workshop that surfaces a serious objection has done its work and has no further move available. A board that surfaces the same objection and is overruled has demonstrated something about the institution that convened it. A board’s worth is therefore measured by what happens the first time it says no.

8.2.1 Public Deliberation and Participatory AI Governance

What governance adds to development is machinery for bringing public input to bear on a decision at a single point in time instead of through an ongoing relationship.

  1. Citizen assemblies. A panel of citizens selected by random, stratified sampling deliberates on a question over an extended period and produces recommendations grounded in that deliberation rather than in a snap poll. Ireland’s Citizens’ Assembly has used exactly this format to shape national recommendations on abortion law and on climate policy — questions carrying the same mix of technical complexity and moral stakes that AI governance has (The Citizens’ Assembly 2016). No assembly has yet been convened on AI itself, which is a fact about political attention and not about the method.
  2. Online platforms for public input. A standing online consultation reaches people a physical assembly cannot, at the cost of the depth that extended facilitated deliberation provides. The European Commission’s consultation on its AI White Paper drew more than 1,200 responses from citizens, companies, and civil-society groups, and fed into the legislative proposal that became the EU AI Act (European Commission 2020).
  3. AI ethics committees. An ethics board answerable only to the company that created it is only as independent as that company is willing to let it be. DeepMind Health set up an outside panel in 2016 to review its work with the UK’s National Health Service and publish annual reports on its findings; Google disbanded the panel in 2019, after members had raised concerns about how much access to information, and how much independence from the company, they actually had (Fisher 2019). The panel’s short life is the governance lesson, and it is the answer to what happens the first time a board says no: the board said something the company did not want to hear, and the company’s available response was to stop having a board. Nothing in the arrangement had made that response costly. A review body is worth what it can enforce, and this one could enforce publication of its own concerns right up until the moment it could not.

What none of these mechanisms does

There is a limit these mechanisms share. Every governance mechanism proposed in this chapter — citizen juries, standing consultations, participatory design, deliberative polling, the vTaiwan model — sits on the same side of a line. All of them make public preference more legible, more informed, and more actionable. Not one of them protects anybody from it. They are aggregation instruments, and a better aggregation instrument produces a more faithful rendering of what the public wants, including when what the public wants is something done to a minority of it.

This observation is not an objection to any of the mechanisms in particular. Preference aggregation is a real requirement and these are good ways to meet it. Meeting this requirement completely would leave a central problem untouched. A perfectly deliberative, perfectly informed, perfectly representative process that concludes an entire demographic should be surveilled, deported, imprisoned, or otherwise scapegoated has produced a faultless output by every criterion in this chapter.

Section 7.2 gets close to this in noticing that treating all disagreement as equally worth representing goes wrong when one side’s position is the product of coercion. But it frames the trouble as a data-quality problem — the input is contaminated, so clean the input. The structural version of the claim is stronger and does not depend on anyone’s preference being contaminated at all: some protections have to hold against the aggregate regardless of how the aggregate came out, and no amount of improvement to the aggregating machinery can supply them.

What supplies those protections is a different kind of machinery: enforceable rights that do not depend on being popular, a court willing to rule against a government that holds a legitimate majority, limits that whoever won the last election cannot repeal, and standing for people who are affected without being enfranchised. Section 10.6 takes up why that half matters especially where a democracy is behaving most democratically.

8.2.2 AI Literacy Outside the Classroom

Most AI literacy work takes the form of a course to enroll in, which is to say it assumes a learner who has proactively decided to learn. That form reaches the people who came looking, but what about everyone else? How to reach people where they already are?

The engagement that does most work is the kind attached to a live question. The Ada Lovelace Institute’s Citizens’ Biometrics Council brought fifty members of the UK public together to deliberate on how biometric technology should be regulated, alongside the first national survey of UK public opinion on the technology (Ada Lovelace Institute 2021). The specificity is what made it work. Participants were not asked what they thought about AI, a question almost nobody can answer usefully, but about facial recognition — a thing with a location, an operator, and a consequence.

That is the lesson for public literacy generally. General AI awareness produces general opinions, which are worth little to a regulator and nothing to a court. A public able to govern a technology is a public that has been asked about a particular use of it.

8.3 The Political Economy of AI and Power

The most ardent proponents of concentrated power tend to be those who already have it. With concentrated power comes access to capital. Concentrating power is the default outcome of AI development, because absent any pushback, capital does an excellent job of recruiting the inputs needed for a capable AI system: compute, data, and talent. Resistance to market power is among the antifascist design goals named at the start of this book.

8.3.1 Economic Incentives in AI Development

Financial incentive mostly determines what AI development produces. Google, Meta, and Amazon commit enormous resources to AI research aimed at operational efficiency, deeper user engagement, and market dominance, and that spending drives technical progress — successive leaps in natural-language processing capability among them (Maslej et al. 2025). What that spending does not automatically produce is a system whose outputs serve the people using it rather than the shareholders funding it.

A recurring case is the profit-driven AI system that optimizes for engagement at the expense of the people engaged. Recommendation algorithms on social media platforms have spread misinformation and hardened political polarization, because a system tuned to maximize time-on-platform has no built-in reason to prefer accurate content over inflammatory content (Germano et al. 2026).

The same economic logic that rewards engagement over accuracy also rewards scale over access: the firms with the deepest resources for AI research pull further ahead of smaller competitors, independent researchers, and everyone else who lacks comparable capital.

None of that is a fixed property of the technology. It is a property of who is paying, which is why section 9.1 treats the source of research funding as a policy instrument rather than an accounting detail.

8.3.2 AI and the Concentration of Power in Tech

A small group of firms — Google, Apple, Meta, Amazon, and Microsoft — holds a share of AI development disproportionate to their number, and that concentration carries economic and ethical stakes independent of what any single one of them does with the position it occupies. No individual development team escapes this pull, for reasons already given.

Vast financial resources let firms of this size fund extensive research and development, draw the top talent in the field, and command sprawling data networks — the raw material effective machine learning depends on. That combination widens the gap between these firms and smaller companies or new entrants, who cannot match the data or the resources and therefore cannot compete on equal terms.

Acquisition sharpens the same dynamic. Facebook’s acquisitions of Instagram and WhatsApp — the company now operates as Meta — did not just remove two competitors; it handed one firm access to comprehensive behavioral data from billions of users worldwide (Federal Trade Commission 2020). Consolidation of that kind extends a firm’s control over how AI gets used in social media, advertising, and e-commerce.

Power concentrated this tightly is also power positioned for exploitation, and the risk sharpens considerably when the technology in question reaches an authoritarian government. The relationship between the Chinese state and firms like Alibaba and Tencent is the clearest live example: state access to those firms’ machine learning capability has been documented as a channel for surveillance and censorship at scale, which is a serious risk to free expression, individual privacy, and democratic accountability wherever the same model gets exported (Human Rights Watch 2020; CNN Business 2020).

Concentration has a quieter cost alongside the loud ones. A firm controlling the datasets and infrastructure a competitive system needs is a gatekeeper whether or not it intends to be, and fewer independent attempts at AI development means fewer chances at the kind of breakthrough an incumbent has no incentive to pursue itself.

The obvious countermeasure is antitrust, and it has been tried against exactly the acquisitions described above. The Federal Trade Commission sued Meta in December 2020 to unwind the Instagram and WhatsApp deals. After a six-week bench trial the district court held, in November 2025, that the agency had not shown Meta currently holds monopoly power — TikTok and YouTube compete for the same attention, so a market defined without them was too narrow — and the FTC appealed the following January (Federal Trade Commission 2020). Whatever the appeal decides, the case is a lesson about the instrument rather than about this defendant: a merger challenge brought a decade after the mergers has to prove a present-day market, and the market had moved. That leaves the remedies which do not depend on winning that argument — mandated access to the datasets that function as chokepoints, and public funding for research outside the dominant firms. Section 6.4.3 develops export controls, prohibited-practices regulation and industry design standards for the narrower case of authoritarian misuse; the concern here is prior to that one: concentration itself, whatever any single firm does with the position it holds.

8.3.3 A Jobs Guarantee, and the Objection It Has to Answer

As AI systems automate tasks once performed by people, demand for advanced technical skills grows alongside a shrinking need for the job types most exposed to automation. Left unmanaged, that combination widens income inequality and strains the social fabric across whichever sector it hits hardest. An estimated 5.7 million factory jobs disappeared in the United States between 2000 and 2010, and while economists still dispute how much of that loss traces to automation rather than trade with China, automation and changed production methods account for a substantial share of it (Hicks and Devaraj 2015).

The response has to be more direct than encouraging retraining and hoping employers fund it. Employers will invest in continuous education for their workers at the scale this requires once they cannot afford not to, and the way to force that scenario is a federal jobs guarantee: government as employer of last resort, hiring anyone who applies, where they live, at a living wage, with paid on-the-job training built into the position instead of offered in place of one. Section 8.3.4 develops the rest of the policy toolkit for workforce disruption. A proposal this ambitious owes a reader what it costs and what is wrong with it.

The proposal does not rest on a forecast

Almost every argument for a policy of this kind is staked on a prediction about how much work machines will take, which is a bad place to stake it: the predictions are contested and have been wrong in both directions for two centuries. Both the maximal-displacement scenario and the standard argument for a universal basic income share an assumption that will not survive being stated — that there is a fixed quantity of work in the world, so machines doing some of it leaves less for people. Set that beside the two categories where labor is most obviously short. Care, of the old, the sick, the very young, the disabled, is not a bounded quantity that could be finished. Neither is the repair of a damaged environment: the seawalls, the remediated ground, the restored watersheds.

The evidence is in the wage. Federal projections put direct care among the largest sources of job openings in the American economy — roughly 9.7 million openings between 2024 and 2034, the bulk of them from workers leaving rather than from growth — against a median wage of $17.36 an hour in 2024 (PHI 2025). An occupation cannot simultaneously be short of workers and unable to raise pay unless something other than scarcity is setting the price. That is a shortage of willingness, which is a political fact and not a technological one, and it holds at any level of machine capability. Tasks inside those categories will be automated, some of them soon, and the argument does not depend on how many.

What it would cost

The most developed costings come from the Levy Economics Institute, whose April 2018 simulation of a program employing between eleven and sixteen million people at fifteen dollars an hour plus benefits put the net budgetary impact at between 0.8 and 2 percent of GDP, with GDP itself rising and private-sector employment increasing by three to four million (Wray et al. 2018). The wage figure has to be read with its date attached: fifteen dollars in early 2018 is worth about twenty in 2026, and a program legislated at the nominal figure would be legislating a cut (U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics 2026). Critics reach higher gross figures — around 2.4 percent of GDP, larger than Medicaid — and call the program unmanageable at scale, disruptive to private labor markets, and inflationary (Bourne 2018).

That simulation models a residual employer, picking up the people the private labor market has already put down. The version I am arguing for is not that. A floor that pays a living wage and builds training into the post competes for people who have alternatives, and it is supposed to, because the entire theory of the thing is that private employers must match the offer to recruit against it. A version nobody joins has failed. So the Levy range is a floor on the cost rather than an estimate of it, and the objection from scale treats as malfunction the one number that shows the mechanism working.

Suppose take-up runs at twice what was modeled and net cost doubles with it, to around 4 percent of GDP. The doubling is a stipulation — deriving it needs a wage distribution matched against the guarantee’s full package, which nobody has produced for this design — so 4 percent is not a bound. Federal spending on Medicare runs about 3.3 percent of GDP net of offsetting receipts and Social Security about 5.2 percent (Congressional Budget Office 2026), which puts the program between the two: among the largest things the federal government does, and an argument about priorities rather than the reductio it is usually offered as. Reading it as an arithmetic question at all is the second feature in section 2.1.2’s structural definition of fascism, working on public finance.

For a government issuing its own currency the binding constraint is productive capacity, available labor and administrative competence, so the question is whether the people and the organizing capacity exist to absorb the program; the wider monetary claim is disputed and I have no settled answer to the strongest objection to it (Palley 2015). Two things scale with participation that no simulation of this kind captures, and they run in opposite directions: administrative capacity, which is where a guarantee visibly fails first and does not expand on the schedule a budget line does, and the private-sector wage response, which raises tax receipts and lowers spending on the programs that top up low wages. A costing that omits the second measures the expense of the policy while ignoring its intended effect.

Skill, training, and status

Skill mismatch looks like the strongest: a displaced radiologist, paralegal, or copywriter is not obviously served by a placement in elder care, and a floor is a smaller thing than a career. It collapses an occupation into its most automatable tasks. What gets automated first is the legible part, and legibility is a poor proxy for what a job is — document review was never the paralegal’s work, only the fraction of it that could be counted. This is the aestheticized metric applied to labor economics, and undoing the substitution shrinks the distance: the paralegal’s actual competence is advocacy inside an administrative system, which is adjacent to the placements a guarantee offers rather than distant from them. Nor is the work make-work: care and environmental repair are chronically understaffed at current wages, which is a fact about pricing and not about whether the work is needed.

Disclaiming the guarantee as a retraining program is the next move, and it gives away the design, because paid in-post training is in the proposal’s definition. It also misreads evidence this book presents itself. Section 6.3.4 reports an evaluation of the main American channel for retraining displaced workers against twenty-three million participation records, and the finding is not that training fails: classroom retraining delivered outside a job rarely moved anyone into work materially less exposed to automation, while employer-led apprenticeship in the same data performed better (Jacobs and Canedy 2026). That contrast is evidence for exactly the embedded, paid, in-post training a guarantee supplies and the evaluated program structurally could not, since a program that trains people and returns them to the labor market to look for work cannot place anyone by construction.

The status objection is that a guarantee preserves nobody’s earnings or standing, which measures it against the job the person held before — not among the options. The alternative is unemployment, and against that baseline the claim inverts. A meta-analysis covering 237 cross-sectional and 87 longitudinal studies found 34 percent of unemployed people showing psychological problems against 16 percent of the employed, with the longitudinal studies and natural experiments supporting the causal direction (Paul and Moser 2009); field experiments sending matched applications to real vacancies find callback rates falling with the length of an employment gap, so time out of work is itself the disqualification (Kroft et al. 2013). Section 5.1.3 identifies eroding trust, humiliation and social fracture as what authoritarian movements recruit from, which is the mechanism a guarantee operates on directly. What it cannot preserve is relative standing within a profession, for people whose identity is bound to a specific expertise. That is a real loss and I have no answer to it.

The objection this proposal actually has to answer

A guarantee is only as good as the state administering it, and employer of last resort under a hostile administration is a placement lever with a compliance condition attached. Refuse the placement and lose the floor. Speak against the government that employs you and find the placement changes. Everything this book says about how a channel becomes an instrument of the thing it was built to check applies with full force to its own flagship proposal. Chapter 11 carries the worked instance from 2025: a bureau whose funding stream a court had upheld, shut from inside by an at-will director who declined to request the money.

The answer is a design specification and not a reassurance, because reassurance is what section 9.3.5 says cannot be audited. Three conditions:

  • Placements are appealable, to a body that did not make them, on a record that survives the appeal. A placement decision that leaves no artifact is a placement decision that cannot be reviewed.
  • Refusal rights are real and priced by the person refusing. That distinction governs here. A worker who can decline a placement without losing the guarantee has a refusal right; one who can decline only by exiting the program has none.
  • The people who audit placements are not the people who make them. This is the same separation required of anyone measuring the slope, and for the same reason: a reviewer that acquires the power to place becomes the thing it was built to watch.

The standard refrain for programs of this kind — federally funded, locally administered — survives, though its usual rationale is the weaker one. The reason is not local fit but redundancy: many administrators, separately capturable, mean there is no single point at which a hostile executive flips the program, and capturing a thousand local authorities is a more visible undertaking than capturing one federal one. It cuts both ways, since local administration is also where a hostile local authority attaches conditions of its own, and there the federal funder is the check. Neither layer is safe alone. One distinction has to be explicit, because the same words describe a different policy: devolution-to-shrink hands the obligation down without the money, so a program can be starved locally while the center points at local administration. The test that separates them is checkable — whether funding is unconditional and centrally guaranteed while placement is local. If funding can be withheld from a jurisdiction, the redundancy argument is void and the structure has become the lever it was supposed to prevent.

That is the standard this book demands of everyone else’s safeguards, applied here. A floor under wages and training is what makes the roof possible, and a floor that can be revoked by whoever is in office is not a floor.

8.3.4 Addressing Workforce Disruption

AI systems that automate entire job categories disrupt work at a scale most labor-market institutions were never built to absorb. Section 8.3.3 traces where that is already showing up and makes the case for a federal jobs guarantee as the floor beneath everything else. What sits on top of that floor is a shorter list than the policy literature suggests, because several of the usual items turn out to be versions of one another.

Retraining and lifelong learning, publicly funded and available at every career stage, including to workers whose employers invest nothing in them, is the standard first answer. Section 6.3.4 covers what the evidence says about how retraining performs on its own, and it is discouraging enough that retraining belongs alongside an income floor and not in place of one.

The floor itself has two candidate forms, and choosing between them takes an argument. Finland’s basic income experiment paid 2,000 unemployed people an unconditional monthly stipend and found modestly higher life satisfaction and lower psychological strain among recipients, with no significant negative effect on employment (Kela (Social Insurance Institution of Finland) 2020). That disposes of the standard objection to unconditional transfers, which is that they stop people working.

The freedom argument, which is the one worth answering

The usual case for a basic income rests on the obsolescence forecast — machines are coming for the work, so decouple income from it — and the previous section has already declined that premise, for both proposals equally. The stronger case does not depend on it. Conditioning a person’s income on their being employed hands whoever controls the employment a lever over them, and a floor that can be pulled is a poor foundation for anyone’s freedom to refuse. That argument has the same shape as this book’s own case for a floor: a protection that means anything has to hold when someone powerful would prefer it did not.

The answer is not that a basic income is conditional and a guarantee is not. Both are conditional, differently, and the difference is what matters.

A basic income is conditional on continued political willingness to fund it. That willingness is revisable by exactly the aggregation machinery section 8.2.1 admits constrains nothing — a majority that stops wanting to pay for it can stop, and no criterion this chapter offers says it may not. And it can be withdrawn by means that are diffuse and deniable. Nobody has to vote to abolish a transfer that is quietly left unindexed; inflation does the work, and no individual decision-maker is ever answerable for the outcome. The dependence is total and it produces no artifact.

A jobs guarantee is conditional on a placement decision. That section concedes without qualification that this is a lever, and that under a hostile administration it is a dangerous one. What it also is, when built to the specification that section sets out, is a decision with a decision-maker, a record, a counterparty, and an appeal. A placement can be denied unjustly, and the denial leaves something behind that a journalist, a court, or a future review can hold up.

That is the slope instrument of section 9.3.5 turned on this book’s own proposal. Dependence that generates artifacts is safer than dependence that generates none, not because the first is benign but because the first can be measured and the second cannot. It is also, for the same reason, the more demanding proposal to build: everything in the comparison rests on the appeal being real, and that section says what makes it real.

That standard now has a court behind it, and on the half of the case that travels. When the Supreme Court kept a Federal Reserve governor in her post in 2026 against a removal for cause, what it held she was owed was not the post but the artifact: “some explanation of the evidence at issue, some avenue for a response, and a deadline by which a response would be due” (Trump v. Cook 2026). Section 3.1 reads the same decision for what does not travel: the Fed’s protection rests on a pedigree no body chartered later can acquire. What a removal has to produce before it counts is not tied to that pedigree.

I put a jobs guarantee ahead of a basic income on that ground, and on a second one the Finland experiment cannot settle: people ought to have the chance to contribute before anyone decides they are unemployable.

One remaining measure does work the floor cannot. Broadened social insurance — unemployment benefits, healthcare, housing — reaches the people who cannot work or choose not to, whom a guarantee by construction does not reach at all.

8.3.5 Literacy, Training, and Where Each One Sits

A safeguard nobody understands is a safeguard nobody can invoke, which is what puts AI literacy — among developers, policymakers, and the public alike — on the list of things the rest of this chapter’s proposals quietly assume. Cross-training among developers, ethicists and legal scholars has already been named as a lever on a system’s immediate environment. Both depend on a question the phrases hide, which is where the teaching sits.

The public tier is the one nobody owns

Developers and students are the easy case, and the mechanisms are familiar: AI concepts and their ethical stakes introduced before higher education, interdisciplinary degree programs, specialized training for working engineers that goes past a compliance checklist. All of it reaches people already inside the pipeline or heading for it, and it works about as well as curriculum reform generally works.

The public is hard because no institution has the job. Elements of AI, a free course built jointly by the University of Helsinki and the Finnish company Reaktor, is the clearest instance of somebody treating public understanding as an obligation rather than a market: Finland funded its translation into every EU member-state language during its EU presidency, specifically to carry it past its own citizens (University of Helsinki 2020). That funding took a national government deciding a course was infrastructure. Nothing in the ordinary funding of AI education produces that decision, which is why the public tier stays thin while the developer tier fills in.

Education for communities historically locked out of the field is part of this argument and not a separate one about diversity. A next generation of developers drawn from a narrow slice of the population reproduces exactly the narrow set of assumptions section 6.1.1 identifies as a source of AI bias, which makes access a technical requirement on the systems as much as a fairness claim about who builds them.

Elective or embedded

For ethics training the real choice is between a course a student can decline and a requirement they cannot. Embedded EthiCS, developed at Harvard by the computer scientist Barbara Grosz and the philosopher Alison Simmons, pairs philosophy graduate students with computer-science faculty to write ethics modules directly into standard CS courses (Grosz et al. 2019). The placement is the design. A stand-alone ethics class is taken by the students who elect it, and its content stays separable from the technical work in a student’s mind because it was separable in the timetable. A module inside the algorithms course is not separable from the algorithms. Grosz’s own course works the same way at the level of content, built on cases with consequences — predictive policing, public-health interventions, wildlife conservation — instead of abstract dilemmas (Harvard Gazette 2019; Harvard University 2026).

Certification does the equivalent work for people whose formal education is finished. The IEEE’s CertifAIEd program builds a professional credential around accountability, transparency, privacy, and bias, open to developers and equally to the policymakers, underwriters, and human-resources staff whose work touches an AI system without building one (IEEE Standards Association 2020). A credential is a weak instrument: it certifies that somebody sat the assessment, and no more. It is also the only instrument here that reaches anyone who has already left a university, which is most of the people making decisions about these systems today.

Chapter 9
Policy, Law, and Accountability

Section 8.2.1 names a limit chapter 8 cannot repair from inside: no aggregation of public preference protects anybody from the aggregate. What does is a different kind of machinery — rules that bind whether or not they are popular, and somebody obliged to answer when they are broken. Very little of it exists.

9.1 Developing Policy Frameworks for AI Ethics, Safety, and Antifascism

Good design inside a single lab does not by itself keep an AI system ethical or resistant to authoritarian misuse. That takes policy, and the instruments available differ mainly in what they can reach. Legislation sets a floor that does not depend on a company volunteering for it. Self-regulation covers what legislation is slow to reach.

Joint funding addresses a narrower problem. Research on AI safety and ethics funded solely by the companies whose products it evaluates concentrates the funding and the findings in the same hands; splitting it between government and industry keeps the incentive to find a problem from depending on who paid to look for one. What it does not do is broaden anybody’s remit: a public-private partnership does what its mandate says and nothing beyond it, because the mandate is what the private partner agreed to and the only thing that partner can be held to. One convened on resistance to authoritarian misuse of AI would produce something else, and would have to be founded that way, since no partner joins a general-purpose venue and then discovers an obligation to take a political side. And none of it holds up without oversight — a body positioned like an institutional review board but built for AI, interdisciplinary and resourced enough to act on what it finds instead of only reporting it.

9.1.1 National Policy Guidelines for AI Development, Employment, and Antifascism

Every country that has written a national AI strategy has written most of the same list — research funding, interdisciplinary capacity, a trained workforce, public consultation, an update to the privacy and accountability law AI has outrun.

They differ in two places, both having to do with leverage. The first is harmonization. A regulation with teeth in one country does little if a developer can relocate to one without them, which makes a national framework partly an instrument for binding other national frameworks — through mutual recognition, through market access, through the cost of maintaining separate compliance regimes side by side. Sections 10.1 and 10.4 work through how little of that has actually happened and which mechanisms have carried anything at all across a border. A policy written as though the country were alone is a policy about domestic firms, and the firms that matter most are not domestic anywhere.

The second is what a government adds to industry self-regulation. What national policy contributes is the part self-regulation lacks by construction: a standard or certification program backed by government monitoring carries a cost for noncompliance, where a purely voluntary one carries a cost only to reputation, which the company itself gets to price.

Neither of these is expensive. Neither needs a new agency, a new science, or an argument the public has not already accepted. That both remain the exception is a fact about political attention, and not about difficulty.

9.1.2 Encouraging Industry Best Practices and Self-regulation

Self-regulation is industries developing and enforcing their own standards without government intervention — agile and responsive to technological change in a way legislation rarely is, and just as easily a cover for avoiding the accountability legislation would impose. Both things are true at once.

The Dangers of Corporate Doublespeak in AI Ethics

Corporate doublespeak — deliberately ambiguous, evasive, or inflated language meant to project ethical seriousness a company’s actual practice does not support — is how self-regulation fails without anyone having to admit it failed. A commitment costs a company nothing if nothing enforces it.

The gap between statement and practice shows up first as erosion: a company announces a value and does not act as though it holds it afterward. Google’s AI Principles, published in June 2018 and pledging to avoid AI applications built for weapons or mass surveillance, were themselves a response to months of internal and public backlash over Project Maven, a Pentagon contract that used Google’s machine learning to analyze drone footage — thousands of employees signed a letter opposing the company’s involvement, and roughly a dozen resigned (Pichai 2018). Having a set of principles is not the same as having upheld them since; the test of the pledge is what happened after it was made, not that it was made.

OpenAI’s history makes a related point from a different angle: founded on a public commitment to open publication, it withheld the full version of GPT-2 in 2019 over misuse concerns — a safety-motivated retreat from its own transparency norm that was itself controversial — and in the same year restructured from a nonprofit into a capped-profit company to raise the capital its research increasingly required (OpenAI 2019). Neither move was dishonest on its face. Both show how a stated commitment to transparency erodes the moment an organization has a competing incentive strong enough to test it.

Public trust erodes the same way, faster and more visibly. In 2020, Google forced out Timnit Gebru, a co-lead of its Ethical AI team, after a dispute over a paper examining risks in large language models; Margaret Mitchell, the team’s other co-lead, was dismissed the following year after searching for evidence of how Gebru had been treated (Dickey 2021). Thousands of Google employees and outside researchers signed a letter in response, and the episode became a reference point for a specific kind of skepticism: a company can fund and publicize an ethics research team while still overriding it the moment its findings become inconvenient. Whether the internal changes Google made afterward addressed that problem or only the public exposure it created is a judgment the affected researchers have made publicly themselves.

“On the Dangers of Stochastic Parrots” argues that scaling language models carries costs that are borne unevenly and benefits that accrue to the organizations doing the scaling; that a corpus assembled by crawling the web cannot be documented or audited at the size involved, so it encodes hegemonic viewpoints while presenting as general; that fluent output invites users to attribute understanding and intent where there is neither; and that the dominant paradigm’s opportunity cost is the smaller, more accountable research directions it displaces (Bender et al. 2021).

Section 6.1.1 takes the corpus argument and extends it. Section 2.3.1’s whole method — declining to treat fluent self-description as evidence about internals — is the fluency argument applied to the case this book cares about most. And the opportunity-cost claim lands on this book directly: a project proposing floors, bearers, guardianship regimes and antifascist detectors is proposing exactly the kind of ambitious research program that the argument says crowds out more modest and more accountable work.

I do not think that is a refutation, because the alternative on offer does not address what happens when a capable system is built anyway. It is a cost, and it is real.

External oversight is what would catch doublespeak that internal incentives don’t stop. Facebook’s content-moderation Oversight Board is one attempt at building it: a body structurally separated from Facebook’s day-to-day decisions and funded through an irrevocable trust that Meta alone capitalized — a design meant to answer exactly the criticism that self-funded oversight is not real oversight, though critics have noted that a trust a company creates and funds once is not the same thing as a board with no relationship to the company it reviews (Meta (Facebook) 2019). Whether that distinction holds up is a fair question to ask of any self-regulatory body a company funds itself.

Misrepresentation of what a system can actually do rounds out the pattern. Clearview AI marketed its facial-recognition technology with a claim of 100 percent accuracy across every racial and demographic group. The figure came from a three-person panel the company assembled and called independent, searching legislators’ photographs against Clearview’s own database of 2.8 billion scraped images rather than the 25,000 mugshots used by the ACLU method it claimed to be copying; the ACLU called the result “absurd on many levels” (Haskins 2020). Regulators in France and Italy later fined the company on separate grounds — unlawful collection of the biometric data the system was trained on (Commission Nationale de l’Informatique et des Libertés (CNIL) 2022). A company that overstates what its own system can do is doing a milder version of what doublespeak does with ethics commitments: describing the product it wishes it had built rather than the one that exists.

None of this is fixable by asking companies to doublespeak less. The same incentive that produces vague ethics language also has an obvious interest in the regulation that would eventually replace it — a version of the same behavior that is harder to document than a corporate pledge, because it happens before any public commitment exists to measure it against. What actually stops doublespeak is external enforcement: regulation with penalties, audits a company cannot decline, and oversight bodies whose funding and staffing do not run back to the company they review.

Consequences of AI Self-regulation and Potential Power Imbalances

Self-regulation concentrates power by default, not by conspiracy: whoever writes the standard also decides what counts as compliant, and a company self-regulating its own product is answering both questions itself. Amazon’s Rekognition facial-recognition software is a documented instance of what that produces in practice — an MIT Media Lab audit found significantly higher error rates for darker-skinned and female faces than for the lighter-skinned male faces the system had apparently been tuned and tested against most closely (Raji and Buolamwini 2019). The bias was not caught by Amazon’s own self-regulation; it took an outside audit the company did not commission.

A company operating without mandatory external review can prioritize competitiveness over the societal impact of what it ships, and nothing in a self-regulatory framework forces that trade-off into the open before it has already been made.

What self-regulation cannot supply on its own is uniformity. Without an enforceable baseline, standards vary by company; a smaller developer without the resources for a well-staffed internal ethics function competes at a disadvantage against one that can afford the appearance of one, and a consumer has no reliable way to tell which AI system’s ethical claims mean anything. Section 6.4.3 covers what a binding baseline looks like in practice, including the EU AI Act’s Article 5 prohibitions — the kind of enforceable floor self-regulation, by definition, cannot set for itself.

None of this argues that self-regulation is worthless, only that it needs something outside itself to answer to. A synergy between government-set baselines and industry-led standards — mandatory requirements at the floor, voluntary practice above it — gets more of the agility self-regulation offers without giving up the accountability it structurally cannot provide alone.

Assessing the Effectiveness of AI Self-regulation

The Partnership on AI is a cross-sector voluntary coalition whose corporate members include Amazon, Apple and Google, committing them to tenets of safety, transparency and broad benefit with no legal force behind the commitment (Partnership on AI 2016). The coalition is a useful test case precisely because its own design limits what it can enforce — member companies interpret its guidelines individually, and nothing in the structure obliges any of them to follow through. A document is only as good as what happens when honoring it costs something, and the Gebru and Mitchell dismissals are the test: public scrutiny, not the company’s own stated commitments, was what produced a response.

So the checkable question is not what a company has published but what its record shows: whether published guidelines are enforced as well as published, whether stated objectives come with progress reported against them, and whether a documented incident has ever cost anybody anything. A company that clears none of those while describing itself as an ethics leader is the doublespeak in its plainest form.

9.1.3 Legal and Ethical Implications of AI Systems Gaining Personhood

Who decides whether an AI system holds personhood, and when, is the narrower question sections 9.3.1 and 9.3.3 do not settle.

Every version of this question that has actually reached a legislature so far arrived as a response to pressure — a liability dispute, a public controversy, a product already on the market — rather than from a standing body asking it in advance. That ordering is the problem. A legislature deciding under pressure decides badly, and it decides against a factual record assembled by whoever had an interest in assembling one. The interdisciplinary review body those sections describe only does the work it is designed for if some jurisdiction creates it before a forcing case shows up, which means creating it while the question still looks academic and nobody’s damages are riding on the answer.

That is a policy choice rather than a doctrinal one, and a cheap one. A national AI strategy that names an agency, gives it a mandate to keep the question under review, and sets a trigger for revisiting it — a capability threshold, a deployment scale, a category of use — decides in advance who will be in the room. It does not decide the answer, and it should not try to. It decides that when the answer is needed there will be a body qualified to give one, which is the part no forcing case ever supplies on its own.

9.1.4 Encouraging the Development of Altruistic, Antifascist, and Ethical AI Systems

Policies to Counteract Authoritarian Uses of AI

A government cannot restrict what it cannot see, and measurement belongs upstream of the whole policy toolkit for that reason. The Carnegie Endowment’s AI Global Surveillance Index assessed 176 countries and found AI-enabled surveillance technology already in active use in at least 75 of them (Feldstein 2019). That number is the evidence base for a countermeasure and is not itself one.
