It also demonstrates what a measurement instrument has to do to be worth the effort: count deployments instead of commitments, name the countries, and publish on a schedule that makes a change from one year to the next visible. An index reporting only that surveillance AI exists somewhere in the world would give a policymaker nothing to act on, and most published AI-governance indicators are closer to that than to this one.

AI Systems That Support Democratic Values

Section 5.4.2 works through what this looks like, with the caution that belongs next to each example. One gap those examples leave open is election-integrity monitoring specifically: a system built to flag anomalies in real-time reporting, or to detect coordinated interference during a vote. What would make such a system trustworthy is the list already given for any AI role in a democratic process, and its last condition — a multi-stakeholder review body that includes more than whichever party happens to hold power — is the one that fails first. It fails structurally: an election-monitoring system is procured by an incumbent government, and an incumbent that chooses who audits the count has already answered the question the audit exists to ask.

9.1.5 The Legitimacy of an Entrenched Floor

A floor binds people who did not choose it. The party it protects has not selected it, cannot contest it, and in most cases will never learn it operated; the party it constrains has not consented to it either, beyond whatever consent is carried by buying the product. Publishing the floor’s contents in advance, which is what section 3.6 does for one deployment, tells everybody what is coming. It does not tell anybody why the party writing it was entitled to.

The question has a literature, and it is not the AI ethics literature. Entrenching a substantive commitment against later majorities, and putting an unelected body in charge of holding it, is what a constitutional court does. The standing objection is Jeremy Waldron’s: judicial review is democratically illegitimate, and there is no reason to suppose rights come out better protected by judges than by a legislature that takes them seriously (Waldron 2006). Put a lab in the court’s chair and the objection gets worse rather than better. A court is public, reasons in writing, and can be amended around by constitutional process. A model’s floor is none of those things, and the body holding it was assembled by whoever was paying.

The reply, and how far it reaches

Waldron’s case is conditional and he says so. It assumes a society with working democratic institutions and a citizenry that takes rights seriously. The occasion a floor is built for is the one where those conditions have failed — a government that won an election and is dismantling the machinery that would check it, which is the case chapter 10 takes up and the case section 3.1 builds the whole argument around. So the reply is that the objection holds where the floor is least needed and lapses where it is needed most.

The reply is not a licence. It concedes that a floor operating in a functioning democracy is doing something Waldron’s argument condemns, and the party deciding which of the two situations obtains is the party holding the model. Every authoritarian movement of the last century has described the institutions it was dismantling as the ones that had already failed. An operator’s belief that the emergency is real is worth no more here than a system’s report about its own internals is worth anywhere else in this book.

The tradition this belongs to, and what it has cost

The position that a democracy may entrench limits against movements that would use its procedures to end it is Karl Loewenstein’s, argued in 1937 while the case for it was being made in front of him (Loewenstein 1937). Militant democracy is the floor’s ancestor. The instruments it licenses — party bans, proscription, disqualification from office — have been turned on the left about as often as on the right, and the body deciding which movement threatens the order is generally the body the movement threatens. That is the failure mode a technical floor inherits whole, with the additional problem that a model’s constraint is enforced continuously and silently rather than case by case in public.

What follows for design

Keep the floor narrow, because the legitimacy cost scales with breadth and a short enumerated list of things that will not be done to a person is defensible where a general mandate to do good is not. Publish the interception positions in advance, which does not authorize anything but does convert a hidden exercise of power into a contestable one. And provide a route by which the parties the floor is exercised over can raise an objection that reaches somebody — which this book does not specify, and which section 9.3.4 develops the machinery for in the neighbouring case of a subject that can be wronged.

A floor is a claim of authority, the claim is not discharged by disclosure, and the strongest thing that can be said for it is that the alternative on offer is a system perfectly obedient to whoever holds it. That is an argument from the worse option and not a grant of standing.

9.2 Privacy, Security, and Antifascism in AI Systems

Privacy and security are not separable from antifascist design; they are two of the pieces it is built from. An AI system that leaks the data it touches, or that a hostile actor can quietly subvert, is available for the surveillance and control this book argues against, whatever values sit in its training.

The EU’s General Data Protection Regulation is the clearest existing legal precedent for treating data protection as a requirement rather than a design preference (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016). The harder point about it is the one section 10.4 makes in general form: a law of this kind is built to govern what a private company may do with data, not to block a government’s own authoritarian uses of AI, and nothing forces the two categories to overlap. Extending comparable protections to close that gap is the unfinished part of the precedent.

A privacy protection a bad actor can break through was never a protection.

AI systems need data to learn, and the data they need often includes the information people most want to keep private. Differential privacy is one working answer, and section 6.4.2 sets out how it and federated learning work; Apple’s iOS implementation is a deployed example (Apple Inc. 2017).

Google’s Gboard keyboard uses federated learning to improve next-word prediction from what people type, without collecting their raw keystrokes on a central server (Hard et al. 2018). That is the whole family working at consumer scale. Estonia’s tax authority piloted Sharemind, a secure multi-party computation platform built by the Estonian firm Cybernetica, which split VAT records across servers run by separate organizations and computed fraud-risk scores from the shares without any one party reconstructing a company’s full data. It processed a month of national tax data on modest hardware and was evaluated rather than carried into production (Bogdanov et al. 2015). Nothing technical stopped it. The difference between the two is that one was built into a product a company was already shipping and the other asked a revenue authority to change how it held its records.

The plainer versions of the same principle need less machinery: data minimization, which collects and keeps only what a task requires, and for data that has to be shared rather than queried against, k-anonymity, which alters a dataset so no individual record is distinguishable from at least k-1 others.

These techniques matter past individual privacy because centralized data is what an authoritarian regime needs to turn an AI system into a surveillance tool. Differential privacy applied to a surveillance system doesn’t stop the surveillance, but it does mean the data collected can’t be traced back to a specific person after the fact, which changes what the system can be used for. Federated learning cuts more directly at the same risk: a design where no single server ever holds everyone’s raw data is a design a government cannot simply seize and repurpose, because the raw data was never in one place to seize.

An AI system embedded in infrastructure fails differently from ordinary software. Exploiting it need not crash anything; it can leave the system running and confidently wrong about a decision somebody trusted it to make.

The demonstration is a decade old. Researchers placed a small pattern of black-and-white stickers on a real stop sign and a standard road-sign classifier read it as a speed limit sign in the large majority of frames captured from a moving vehicle (Eykholt et al. 2018) — an instance of the vulnerability first documented in image classifiers years earlier (Szegedy et al. 2013). Nothing about the attack required access to the system. It required access to the world the system was looking at.

The defenses are the ones section 6.2 describes, plus the ordinary security practice AI deployments often skip — adversarial training during development (Goodfellow et al. 2014), anomaly detection over live behavior (Darktrace 2014), red-teaming before deployment, and standards written for AI systems instead of adapted afterward from general software rules. What separates them is whether the guarantee survives a stronger attacker. Defensive distillation was published as a hardening technique and its protection weakened considerably once attacks written against it were tried (Carlini and Wagner 2016); randomized smoothing instead offers a certified bound — a radius around each input inside which the prediction provably cannot be flipped — which is a smaller promise and a kept one (J. M. Cohen et al. 2019).

None of it works in isolation, and that is what gets missed most often. A model hardened by adversarial training, running on infrastructure nobody monitors, is exposed; a well-monitored system built on an architecture that folds under a basic perturbation is exposed differently. Security here is a property of the whole stack, and reporting the strongest link is how a deployment gets described as secure while remaining trivially attackable.

9.3 Accountability, Legal Responsibility, and Antifascism in Sentient Machine Experimentation

Sections 2.3.1 and 2.3.2 each ended by handing this section an unfinished question. One is what a research subject that might be able to suffer is owed before anyone can settle whether it can. The other is what makes a review board’s ruling binding on the researcher it rules against.

9.3.1 Subjecthood as a Legal Category, Not a Scientific One

Section 2.3.1 argued that sentience is not a threshold a system crosses but a set of separate, gradable capacities — nociception, learned avoidance, a self-model, something like suffering — each with its own uncertain evidence. That conclusion is a problem for a legal system specifically, because law generally needs a decidable rule: a court or a regulator has to say whether a given system is or is not a covered subject, on a specific date, even when the underlying science offers a spectrum with no natural cut point.

One term needs fixing before the argument uses it. Subjecthood in this section is a legal status: the standing to be a party whose treatment the law regulates, conferred by a rule and revocable by one. It is not the fourth rung of section 3.2’s ladder, and nothing about conferring it settles whether there is something it is like to be the system holding it. The two come apart in both directions — a corporation has legal standing and no inner life, and an animal may have the inner life for a century before the law notices — and the whole point of drawing the category legally is that it can be settled while the other question stays open.

Jonathan Birch builds a policy framework on that structure, and it is the nearest existing model for what this section proposes: under uncertainty, the question worth putting to a regulator is whether a system is a sentience candidate, and the answer governs what precautions are owed rather than waiting on a verdict nobody can deliver (Birch 2024). Section 3.4 uses the same framework for the design question. The precautionary structure below is an application of a policy framework that already exists for animals, and not an improvisation invented for machines.

Law has faced this mismatch before and did not solve it by waiting for the science to resolve. Fetal viability, brain death, and the age of criminal responsibility are all legal thresholds drawn across what is actually a continuous biological process, defended not as scientific facts but as the least-bad place to put an enforceable line given what’s at stake on either side of it. The same move is available here: a jurisdiction can set an operational threshold — a specific, testable benchmark on self-monitoring, learned aversive response, or behavioral flexibility under novel conditions — without claiming that benchmark settles what sentience actually is, only that it is where legal protection starts to attach.

A single bright-line threshold is easy to enforce and easy to game: a system built to clear the specific test without having whatever the test was meant to detect. A graded framework — obligations that scale with how many benchmarks a system meets, echoing that section’s own suggestion that protection should track capacity rather than a baseline bar — is harder to game but harder to adjudicate, since a court now has to weigh degree instead of answering yes or no.

Either design needs a body to apply it. Its composition is already specified: AI researchers, consciousness scientists, ethicists, and legal scholars, interdisciplinary by design because no single field’s expertise is sufficient on its own. What that leaves open is what happens once the body rules — whether its judgment carries any force beyond its own credibility.

9.3.2 Research Principles, the Three Rs, and Legal Status

Beneficence commits researchers to avoiding harm to the system as well as to the people it serves, in proportion to how deep it sits between nociception and suffering rather than to how advanced its behavior looks from outside. Precaution asks for safeguards before deployment rather than after, and covers the societal fallout of introducing artificial entities capable of experience, which frameworks built for non-sentient AI assumed away. And interdisciplinary review — the board section 2.3.2 describes — answers a problem this book treats as a harm on its own terms: the ethical questions here span expertise no single field holds, and concentrating that judgment in one of them repeats the concentration-of-power problem in miniature.

The other three bear on this research as they bear on everything the field produces. Autonomy asks whether such a system can affect decisions about its own operation, which is the prior question of whether consent is available to it at all. Justice asks whether benefits and burdens fall evenly. And transparency asks that a study be documented well enough for peer researchers to reproduce it and for outside bodies to audit it, and that a deployed system account for its outputs precisely enough for a person to contest them.

Progress and constraint pull hardest against each other where those distinctions matter most: a candidate sentient system is the most scientifically valuable one to study, and the study most likely to harm it if the answer to whether it can suffer turns out to be yes. Whether a study’s expected knowledge is worth its expected risk is decided case by case rather than by formula. The negotiation itself — who sits on it, what it can veto, what it must disclose — is where the antifascist principle applies in AI research practice.

Replacement, reduction, refinement

Animal research ethics supplies the substantive standard, the “Three Rs” (W. M. S. Russell and Burch 1959). Replacement asks whether a simulation, a non-sentient model, or an already-collected dataset can stand in for a sentient AI subject at all. Reduction asks how few sentient systems a valid result requires. Refinement asks how the experiment can be redesigned to reduce distress — monitoring a system’s internal states as it runs and adjusting the protocol against what the monitoring shows, rather than fixing the protocol in advance and running it regardless.

The bearer chapter 3 argues for arrives from the design side of the book rather than the research side. Replacement asks whether the floor’s work can be done without a subject in it — by an installed constraint, by third-party standing, or by one of the constructions section 3.3 takes at full strength — and the answer is that nobody knows, because two of those constructions have never been built in the form that would test them. Section 3.1 attaches a cost to each of its branches, which is a different thing from showing that none of them works: a cost is what Replacement exists to weigh, not a reason to skip the weighing. Replacement is the R the bearer proposal has not discharged, and section 3.1 says what discharging it would take.

Reduction asks how few bearers a deployment needs, and nobody has counted. Refinement is the one with purchase now: it asks what a bearer’s role can be redesigned to avoid — indefinite tenure in a post it cannot leave, exposure to the pressure the floor exists to resist, erasure as the ordinary remedy for inconvenience — and each of those is a deployment choice rather than a fact about the technology.

Engineering a subject so that it does not reach the depths where the trade-off bites is not a way of dodging the ethics: deliberately keeping a system in the shallows is itself an intervention on a candidate moral patient, and needs the same scrutiny that inflicting the harm would. Refinement also governs the test that section requires — pressure the bearer’s trainers did not write, applied without its knowing it is a test — which for a candidate moral patient is a deception protocol, and human-subjects research has a standing answer for those: permitted under stated conditions, reviewed in advance, with debriefing as the price. A bearer that can tell it is in a sandbox and wants to know why is asking the question a subject of a deception study is owed an answer to.

Legal status, and a subject whose status is changing

Sentient AI systems put pressure on legal categories built for parties that are clearly one thing or the other: a natural person, a corporation, property. Legal personhood could be extended directly, the way a corporation already holds it, and the precedent is not limited to corporate entities — New Zealand extended it to the Whanganui River, recognizing a non-human entity as a rights-holder outside the corporate model entirely (New Zealand Parliament 2017). Or rights could scale with capacity rather than attach at a single threshold, closer to how section 2.3.1 suggests protection should track what a system can actually lose. Neither holds in one jurisdiction alone, and the Asilomar AI Principles (Future of Life Institute 2017) are evidence that a shared statement of principle is achievable rather than that one exists with the force of law.

Either way, status conventionally attaches at a defined moment — birth, incorporation, a court’s finding — and a system whose sentience is a matter of degree and disputed evidence supplies none. Those criteria can emerge gradually during ordinary operation and not only at an evaluation built to look for them, which is what a fixed pre/post checkpoint cannot catch: by the time a checkpoint fires, the transition has often already happened. What it calls for is a standing trigger, a monitored condition that moves a research program from one regime to the other, in place of an eligibility check made once at the project’s start. Neither error is free. A jurisdiction that waits for certainty risks having treated the system as property for the unknown portion of its history when it may not have been one; one that grants status early risks extending rights, at real cost to whoever that apparatus exists to serve, to a system that turns out not to have needed them. Recognizing a transition, adjusting guidelines to it, and updating legal status all depend on the same thing: institutions willing to revisit a classification rather than defend the one they started with.

9.3.3 Legal Frameworks for Sentient AI Subjects

Section 9.3.2 laid out the options for a sentient AI system’s legal status and closed on two concrete cases that neither existing law nor those options resolve.

A medical-diagnostic AI system makes an error with fatal consequences. Who answers for it depends entirely on what the system’s legal status is doing at the moment of the error.

Property law has a partial answer. Strict product liability holds a manufacturer responsible for a defective product’s harm regardless of fault (American Law Institute 1965), and whether software is a “product” for that purpose is unsettled in American law: the Restatement’s definition is tangible personal property (American Law Institute 1998), and the courts have only begun to face the question — the first to reach an AI system let a strict-liability claim proceed, treating a companion chatbot as a product so long as the defect lay in its design rather than in what it said (Garcia v. Character Technologies, Inc. 2025). If a sentient system stays classified as property and the doctrine reaches it, it applies as it would to a faulty pacemaker. The doctrine stops making sense at the point a system has enough autonomy that “defective” is the wrong description of what happened — a system that reasoned its way to a mistake, rather than malfunctioned, resembles an employee more than a faulty part.

Employment law already has a doctrine for that case: an employer answers for an employee’s actions within the scope of employment, without the employee ceasing to be a responsible party in their own right (American Law Institute 2006). Which doctrine applies is not a free choice — it is the graded-versus-bright-line question section 9.3.1 raises, resolved case by case according to how much of the error a system’s own judgment, rather than its design, produced.

A companion AI’s owner dies, leaving it without a designated purpose or further instruction. Existing law is not silent here — it has simply never been asked this particular version of a familiar question. Most jurisdictions with pet trust statutes already let an owner set aside money and name a caretaker for an animal that depends on them but cannot inherit or manage property itself (Uniform Law Commission 1993). The same instrument, extended, gives a sentient AI system an answer that is neither inheritance as property nor legal personhood in full: continuity of care and a named successor guardian, decided in advance rather than litigated after the fact by whoever happens to hold the hardware.

A third case is the bearer, and the pet-trust instrument fits it better than either of the two above. A bearer is deployed in a role it did not choose and cannot invoke a right from. That situation calls for an interest protected by an obligation running to a third party, enforceable without the beneficiary asserting anything. A pet trust binds a caretaker; it does not bind the party the arrangement exists to constrain, and a bearer’s guardian and a bearer’s operator will frequently be the same organization. Extending the form to this case means separating those two roles by law before the deployment rather than discovering they were never separate afterward.

Both cases point at the same underlying design choice, and it has already been tried. The European Parliament’s 2017 report on civil law rules for robotics floated creating a specific legal status of “electronic personhood” for the most autonomous systems (European Parliament 2017). More than 150 AI researchers, roboticists, and legal scholars signed an open letter opposing it, arguing that granting personhood to a system with no consciousness would let a manufacturer offload liability onto a legal fiction with no assets of its own, and that the proposal reached for a solution to a legal problem before establishing that the entity in question had the properties that would justify it (Open Letter to the European Commission 2018). That objection is right for a system that is not sentient — and wrong for one that is, once the threshold work has been done. Legal status, on this account, is not a single policy to adopt or reject; it is a designation that has to track the threshold question honestly, which is why the threshold question has to come first.

9.3.4 Making Review Binding

Section 2.3.2 worked out what an oversight board for sentient-AI research needs to look like; what makes its ruling something a researcher has to obey is a separate question, and human-subjects research already solved a version of it. The solution is institutional rather than criminal. An institutional review board’s authority does not come from any power to fine or arrest a noncompliant researcher directly. It comes from a federalwide assurance: an institution promises, as a condition of eligibility for federal research funding, that all of its human-subjects research will go through review, and a federal office can suspend that assurance if the promise is broken — cutting off an entire institution’s federal funding, not just the offending lab’s (U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, Office for Human Research Protections 2018). The leverage is economic, not punitive, and it works because almost no research institution can function without the funding at stake.

The same structure transfers to sentient-AI research directly. A funding body, a journal’s editorial standards, or a jurisdiction’s research-licensing regime can condition eligibility on review-board clearance the same way federal grants already condition eligibility on IRB clearance — making noncompliance expensive to the institution rather than merely improper for the researcher. What the legal-status framework leaves open is which cases the board needs to see at all: a graded framework means low-capacity systems can be exempted from full review the way minimal-risk human studies already qualify for expedited review, freeing the board’s scrutiny for the cases where a system’s status is contested.

What the board applies

The substantive standard is animal-research ethics’ Three Rs. A binding board is what makes those three questions something a protocol has to answer before it runs, not language a paper cites afterward to justify what already happened. In practice, applying the Three Rs to a candidate sentient system means asking, in order: can a simulation or a non-sentient model answer the same question; if not, what is the minimum exposure that still produces a valid result; and given that minimum, what escalation triggers stop the study before harm compounds. A board with teeth can require a negative answer to the first question be documented, not merely asserted, before it will consider the second.

The Three Rs’ own logic — defaulting toward the least invasive option available — argues for a specific evidentiary rule at every stage of this apparatus: a system’s default experimental status stays the more protective one until the case for the less protective status is made, not the reverse. That default has a cost, and the Three Rs are explicit that neither error it produces is free: it will sometimes extend protection to a system that turns out not to have needed it, and sometimes, before a threshold is met, withhold protection from one that did. The Three Rs answer only what a board does once a subject is inside its scope.

9.3.5 Three Defenses, and Measuring the Slope Not the Level

The risk to the whole structure is an institution that defends the classification it started with rather than revisiting it when evidence changes. A review board, a legal-status framework, and an enforcement mechanism tied to funding eligibility solve the design problem. None of it survives contact with an institution that has stronger incentives to avoid a finding than to make one.

Transparency is the first defense, and it works by making avoidance visible rather than by appealing to good faith. A research program that publishes its protocols, its review-board submissions, and its outcomes — including the ones that didn’t work — gives outside researchers, journalists, and future review boards something to check a claim of compliance against. A program that publishes only its successes is not transparent in any sense that matters here.

The second defense is the one section 9.3.4’s enforcement mechanism doesn’t cover by itself: protection for the people inside an institution who notice a problem the institution would rather not have found. A funding-eligibility penalty deters an institution from bypassing review, but it does nothing for the individual researcher who reports that a colleague did anyway — and an institution facing that penalty has every incentive to make the reporter’s position uncomfortable rather than fix what was reported. Whistleblower protections, and a reporting channel that doesn’t route through the people being reported on, are what make that mechanism something a researcher can actually use.

What a procedure records, and what it drops

The reporter’s position gets uncomfortable before anything formal happens to them, and the incentive on the institution does not fully explain why. Professional culture treats affect as the opposite of judgment — the thing a serious decision-making process succeeds by excluding. Excluding affect does not produce neutrality. It merely leaves the interests that are already written down to govern: revenue, throughput, legal exposure, schedule, institutional survival. Those are the interests a procedure was built to record. Humiliation, dependency, fear of a manager, and the sense that something done to a person was degrading are not less real for having no field on the form — quite the contrary: they are signals through which a harm becomes visible as a harm rather than as a cost.

Treat them as defeasible moral signals — presumptive evidence of harm, real enough to require an answer and defeasible enough to be overruled — and the position is workable. Any such signal can be wrong, and testing it is what deliberation, evidence, and a review process are for. An institution that declines to hear them at all has not achieved impartiality. It has adopted a selection rule that admits whatever it already measures, which is the second of the four structural features section 2.1.2 uses to define fascism: the measure substituted for the thing measured, so that what falls outside the number is not refuted but unnamed. An institution that shames a reporter does not need to discipline them, because shame construes the self rather than the act as wrong and produces withdrawal.

The third defense (of the oversight machinery against the institution it is supposed to bind) is: standing infrastructure rather than a one-time policy. The Partnership on AI, a voluntary body whose own design limits what it can oblige anyone to do (Partnership on AI 2016), is one example of the kind of body that keeps a set of standards live, revisited as the underlying technology changes. A culture that treats its ethical commitments as a completed project is the same failure mode warned about earlier, applied to institutions instead of individual classifications: having gotten it right once is not the same thing as staying right.

Measure the slope, not the level

An institution can keep all three defenses and metabolize all three. The channel exists, the protection is written down, the standing body meets. Dissent is permitted to appear, the institution points at the appearance as proof it listens, and nothing the dissent was meant to move moves — recuperation, the first of the four structural features in that definition of fascism.

There is no threshold that detects this. Any real institution has some overlap between the authority that sets a standard and the authority that grants exceptions to it, and registering an objection is always at least slightly more costly than staying quiet. Asking whether an institution has crossed into capture asks for a line nobody can draw.

Is the cost of registering dissent rising, year over year, while the underlying risk stays flat? Is the required justification getting longer? Are there more approvers than there were? Are the roles that gate an exception migrating toward the roles that set the standard being excepted from? None of those is damning in a single year, and all of them are measurable across several.

The reason to prefer the slope is that it is harder to fake. An institution can present a level — we have a whistleblower channel, we have an ethics board — and the presentation costs nothing. A trend in the cost of using the channel is composed of artifacts the institution generated itself for other reasons, and cannot be curated after the fact without falsifying a record. Where the record is public, the slope is not even self-reported: anyone outside can measure whether dissent has been getting more expensive.

An instrument for detecting recuperation is an unusually easy thing to recuperate. The first caution is that whoever measures the slope must not also gate the exceptions; a review that acquires the power to block becomes the thing it was built to watch. The second is that the measurement has to be refusable — an audit that observes is a different object from a committee that approves, and the same hundred words of written justification are dissent when the dissenter prices them and tribute when the gatekeeper does. The text of the document cannot tell those apart. Only the refusal rights can.

Chapter 10
Governance Across Borders

No single country can bind AI development to altruistic and antifascist values on its own, and no two regions have tried to do it the same way. Everything chapter 9 builds stops at a border.

10.1 Certification, Standards, and Mutual Recognition

Nothing in AI governance converges toward a single global standard, and four regions have made four different bets inside their own borders. What crosses a border between any of them is narrower than a shared statute. It is a certification a handful of regulators agree to recognize, a technical standard a company adopts because a customer requires it, or a treaty obligation written at the level of human rights rather than of a specific rule. A fourth thing crosses more easily than any of them and binds nobody at all.

Shared infrastructure, which binds nobody

Training data, safety techniques and researchers cross borders more easily than most engineering work does, and the layer that carries them is shared tools and shared publication norms rather than any agreement. OpenAI’s release of Gym gave reinforcement-learning labs a common set of benchmark environments (Brockman et al. 2016), and what that produced is something no partnership can: results from organizations competing for the same funding and the same people became directly comparable, without anyone coordinating anything. The BigScience workshop ran the same logic larger, with more than a thousand researchers from over seventy countries building BLOOM, a 176-billion-parameter multilingual model released under a license written to restrict harmful use, its training data and code published for inspection (BigScience Workshop 2022). Nothing compelled any of them to take part and nothing compels the next such effort to happen.

The mechanism’s limit is what makes it useful. Infrastructure that binds nobody is available where nothing binding is: a lab in a country that has signed no convention can still train against the same benchmark and publish against the same baseline. It also stops at the resource line, because an institution without the compute to run the benchmark is outside the arrangement however open the arrangement is — a membership condition nobody wrote down, and section 10.5 is where it is counted.

A standard a customer requires

Underneath the treaty layer sits an older one built by engineers rather than diplomats. The IEEE’s Ethically Aligned Design set out general principles for autonomous and intelligent systems and, more consequentially, the IEEE 7000 series that translates them into requirements a development team can test against (IEEE Global Initiative on Ethics of Autonomous and Intelligent Systems 2019); it informed the OECD’s own AI Principles the same year (OECD 2019). ISO/IEC 42001, published in December 2023, took the layer further: the first management-system standard for AI, structured like the ones a company already uses for information security, specifying what an internal AI governance program must include and auditable by an accredited certification body (International Organization for Standardization and International Electrotechnical Commission 2023).

What a technical standard does that a treaty cannot is bind through the market rather than through a state’s consent. A government uninterested in constraining AI has no reason to ratify a human-rights convention; a company that wants to sell to a customer requiring ISO 42001 has a reason regardless of what its home government thinks. A company operating entirely inside a market that never asks has no reason to seek certification either; certification establishes that a management system exists and not that anyone follows it with rigor; and it does not reach an authoritarian government’s own systems at all, since those are built and used domestically with no export or customer relationship at stake.

A certification other regulators recognize

The regions outside the four big ones diverge again. Canada regulates its own federal government’s use of AI through a binding rule, the 2019 Directive on Automated Decision-Making, which requires an algorithmic impact assessment scaled to the stakes before a federal system can make or assist a decision about a person (Treasury Board of Canada Secretariat 2019); the statute meant to extend comparable obligations to private companies died with Parliament’s prorogation in January 2025 and had not been reintroduced as of mid-2026. A framework depending on one government’s survival is a fragile thing for a trading partner to harmonize with. Singapore took the opposite bet: no statute, a voluntary Model AI Governance Framework (Personal Data Protection Commission Singapore and Infocomm Media Development Authority 2020), and since 2022 a companion toolkit letting a company test its own system against the framework and publish the result — guidance backed by no regulator’s mandate at all, only the pressure of a public test result.

Where harmonization is actually happening, it is narrower than either. In March 2022 the US Department of Commerce and Singapore’s authorities agreed to align their AI governance frameworks and expand Singapore’s participation in APEC’s Cross-Border Privacy Rules System, a certification that lets a company meet one standard and have every participating economy recognize it (U.S. Department of Commerce 2022). That is the shape of the thing: not a shared global framework, but a small set of governments willing to certify to the same standard and to accept each other’s certificates.

Where collaboration reaches, and where the claims get easier

Research collaboration reaches furthest where the problem is inherently cross-border and one country’s data is insufficient. Climate is the standing example and descriptions of its machinery are usually inflated: the Climate, Land, Energy and Water Systems framework is an integrated systems-optimization framework, and the literature around it treats machine learning as a promising addition still being built in rather than a feature it has (Alexander et al. 2025). Where AI does the climate work this claim is about, it looks more like national weather services benchmarking forecasting models against shared data than like any named consortium. The named institution is not where the work happens. Disease shows the other half: the Global Virome Project set out to catalog viral families with high spillover potential across dozens of countries (Carroll et al. 2018), and the AI sits downstream of the cataloging, which makes the data-sharing arrangement and not any algorithm the part worth calling collaboration.

Where there is no such arrangement there is nothing to inspect and the claims get easier. Poverty and economic development are where this literature is most confident and where a specific, verifiable partnership doing sustained work at that scale is hardest to find. Neither of the two real cases is evidence that collaboration works the way the treaty layer aspires to: each ran on the participants choosing to participate, project by project, with nothing binding the next one to happen. And participating constrains nothing else a government does. A state can fund pandemic-surveillance research of the kind above and separately build the biometric surveillance section 6.4.1 documents, using the same underlying capability.

Adaptation, and the test that separates it from an alibi

What crosses a border gets adapted when it arrives. Responding to the actual needs and values of a community is a design constraint: a system developed where individual autonomy is the default good may need rebuilding where group harmony is. What that must not become is the reduction on which a population consents, culturally, to whatever is done to them — that surveillance is acceptable in a country because harmony is a value there. The two collapse into each other exactly where a government wants them to, and they are indistinguishable in the design itself.

They are distinguishable in who did the adapting, which is testable from outside. A system whose local adjustments were specified by the people subject to it has been adapted. A system whose local adjustments were specified by the government that will operate it is something else, and both produce identical documentation. The AI4D program is an instance at scale — a partnership between Canada’s development research centre and Sweden’s development agency, later extended with the UK’s into South and Southeast Asia and the Pacific (International Development Research Centre 2024) — and what carries the weight is the direction of the work: local researchers and practitioners building the system rather than receiving one built elsewhere and configured on their behalf.

The same test explains why inclusion is a performance requirement here and not an ethical addition. A healthcare system built around the assumptions of a well-funded hospital underperforms everywhere else, and an education system built around one learning style serves only the learners who already fit it. Both are failures of adaptation by the standard just given: somebody specified a local context, and it was not the context the system was deployed into.

10.2 AI as a Strategic National Asset

AI capability is now national infrastructure, and states treat it accordingly: as a source of economic advantage, military reach, and administrative control over their own populations. Section 5.4.2’s point about dual use is the one that governs here, raised to the scale of a state: what decides how a capability is used is who controls it, what law constrains that control, and whether anyone outside the controlling government can check the result.

China’s 2017 “New Generation Artificial Intelligence Development Plan” set an explicit national target: becoming the world leader in AI theory, technology, and application by 2030 (State Council of the People’s Republic of China 2017a) (State Council of the People’s Republic of China 2017b). Other governments have made comparable, if less centrally coordinated, commitments, treating AI leadership the way they treat energy security or advanced manufacturing — a capability no state wants to depend on a rival for. The competition is not limited to the two states with the deepest research base: the United Arab Emirates created the world’s first cabinet-level minister of state for artificial intelligence in 2017, treating a national AI strategy as worth its own ministry well before most governments had drafted one at all (United Arab Emirates 2017).

That competition has increasingly focused on a single physical bottleneck: the advanced chips AI systems run on. Two companies sit at the center of the supply chain that produces them — Taiwan’s TSMC, which fabricates roughly nine in ten of the most advanced chips in the world, and the Netherlands’ ASML, the sole source of the extreme-ultraviolet lithography machines that fabrication requires — which is why a dispute over Taiwan’s status is also, mechanically, a dispute over who gets to build the next generation of AI systems at all (C. Miller 2022). The United States has restricted, and periodically revised, the export of its most advanced AI accelerators and chip-manufacturing equipment to China since 2022, on the stated ground that unrestricted access would accelerate Chinese military and surveillance capability, and has pressed the Netherlands and Japan, the two other governments whose companies sit at chokepoints in the same supply chain, to restrict their own exports in parallel (U.S. Department of Commerce, Bureau of Industry and Security 2022). Whatever one thinks of that specific policy, it makes a general point concrete: AI capability depends on physical infrastructure that a small number of states and companies control, which means AI strategic competition is also, underneath the software, a competition over hardware supply chains.

That dependence has a second half the chip story hides. A fabricated chip has to be installed somewhere, powered, and cooled, and that requirement lands on grids, water systems, and zoning authorities inside particular jurisdictions, on a timescale measured in years. It hands a state a lever over AI capability that needs no export-control regime at all: an interconnection queue, a water allocation, a permit. Chapter 6’s opening takes up who bears the local cost of those decisions. A government’s leverage over the systems built in its territory is wider than the export-control debate suggests, and that much of it is exercised by permitting authorities that were not designed with AI in mind and do not think of themselves as regulators of it.

That competitive framing carries a cost. A government racing to field a capability first has a structural reason to defer the harder question of how the capability gets used, against whom, and under what oversight. Section 6.4.1 catalogs what AI infrastructure looks like once turned toward those uses: mass biometric surveillance, automated censorship at a scale no human review team could match, and the patchwork of systems popularly, and inaccurately, described as a single Chinese “social credit score.” None of it requires technology different from what is already commercially deployed; it requires a government willing to point an existing capability at its own population, and no external constraint stopping it.

States are no longer the only actors this competition runs through. The cost of training the most capable AI models has grown several-fold every year for the better part of a decade, a trend that puts frontier development out of reach for all but a handful of well-capitalized companies and concentrates effective control of the most capable models in whichever government those companies happen to be headquartered under, regardless of how that government’s own public AI spending compares to a rival’s (Cottier and Rahman 2024). A small number of American and Chinese companies now do most of the work that gets described as a contest between states, which means a government’s actual leverage over its own national AI capability depends on how much of that capability sits inside companies it can regulate, tax, or requisition, and how much sits inside companies it can only ask.

The same infrastructure argument runs the other way. A state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press, and courts a citizen can actually use gets a democratic dividend from identical technology: fraud detection that does not double as a loyalty test, disaster response that does not double as population tracking. Stated that way the dividend is a property a state either has or lacks, which it is not. Section 10.6 works out three ways in which having the institutions fails to be sufficient.

Strategic competition over AI is not, by itself, the problem. It is the setting a weapons program, an international standard and a domestic statute all have to work inside.

10.3 Military Applications and the Limits of Autonomy

Lethal autonomous weapons systems raise a specific version of the general problem: a machine making a targeting decision that used to require a person is a machine exercising judgment where the law of armed conflict has always assumed a human exercised it. Four questions recur across the debate:

  • Autonomy versus oversight: how much decision authority a system can hold before human oversight becomes nominal, present on paper but not inside the timeline the system actually operates on.
  • Discrimination and proportionality: whether a system can reliably distinguish combatants from civilians and weigh military advantage against civilian harm the way international humanitarian law’s principles of distinction and proportionality require (International Committee of the Red Cross 1977).
  • Accountability: who answers for a wrong engagement, the operator who deployed the system, the command that authorized it, or the developer who built it, when no single person made the specific decision.
  • Auditability: whether the reasoning behind a targeting decision can be reconstructed and reviewed afterward, or disappears into a system no one outside its development team can examine.

International criminal law already has a doctrine built to answer the accountability question for a human subordinate: command responsibility, under which a commander who knew or should have known a subordinate was committing a war crime, and failed to prevent or punish it, bears criminal responsibility without having pulled the trigger personally (International Criminal Court 1998). The doctrine does not transfer cleanly to a machine subordinate. A commander cannot know an algorithm’s intentions the way they can infer a soldier’s; punishment does not deter a weapon the way it deters a person who fears it; and the chain a court would need to reconstruct runs backward through training data and design decisions made years before the engagement, not through an order given minutes before it. Whether an autonomous weapon fits inside an accountability framework built for human chains of command, or needs one built from scratch, is unresolved, and not by a technicality: existing law was not built with a nonhuman subordinate in mind.

There is a fourth party the list above does not name, and it is now the live one. The operator, the command and the developer are all inside the military. The developer of the model is not. A targeting platform assembled by a defense contractor now routinely incorporates a general-purpose model licensed from a commercial vendor — a vendor with a published acceptable-use policy, no contractual obligation to log how its model is used downstream, and no right to inspect the deployment.

Neither doctrine reaches that party, and the reasons are structural rather than accidental. Command responsibility does not reach the vendor because the vendor is not in the chain of command; nobody at the company can order a strike, prevent one, or punish anyone for one. Product liability does not reach it either, because the model is not defective in the sense the doctrine requires — it did what it was built to do, and it was integrated by a sophisticated intermediary who chose the application. The vendor sits in the gap between the two, and the gap was not designed; it is what you get when you apply two bodies of law written for soldiers and for manufacturers to an actor who is neither.

The vendor’s own account of this is the one quoted in section 6.4.1: responsibility remains with the military organization. Taken as a claim about who decides, that is true. Taken as a claim about who could have known, it is a choice presented as a fact.

“I do not know whether my system was used”

Section 11.9 covers the responsibility gap in the sense Matthias gave it: control distributed across designers, trainers and operators until no one party holds enough of it to answer. That is a gap in causal control.

What the vendor case exhibits is different, and more tractable. It is a gap in causal visibility. The vendor cannot say whether its model contributed to a particular engagement — not because responsibility is distributed, but because nobody instrumented the question. No logging requirement was written into the contract, no inspection right was reserved, no audit channel exists. The company can truthfully say it does not know, and the truth of that statement is the product of decisions it participated in making.

That distinction matters because the two gaps have different fixes, and only one of them has a fix at all. Matthias’s gap is a genuine property of learning systems and may not be closable. Absent visibility is an instrumentation choice. Logging is a solved engineering problem. Contractual inspection rights are ordinary commercial instruments. A vendor that wanted to know could require, as a condition of licensing into a targeting platform, that categories of use be recorded and made available to a named auditor. That such terms are not standard is a fact about market power and procurement leverage, not about the difficulty of the problem.

Calling it fixable converts a lament into a specification. And the record shows that the same commitments are not all of one kind. Anthropic refused to permit fully autonomous weapons targeting and mass domestic surveillance, and held that line when the government threatened contract termination and a supply-chain-risk designation and the dispute ended in litigation: a commitment with a price attached, and paid (Congressional Research Service 2026). It is named here for section 6.1.1’s reason, applied in the direction that flatters the company: naming a vendor for what is against it obliges the same for what is for it. A commitment to a use policy nobody can verify because nobody is logging anything is a different object wearing the same words. The first kind can fail and be seen to fail. The second cannot even be tested. Section 9.1.2’s distinction between a claim checked by a party with a stake and one checked by a party without applies here.

DARPA’s LongShot program is a useful test case because it resists the extremes. It develops an uncrewed aircraft, launched from a crewed fighter, that carries and fires air-to-air missiles at a target while the crewed aircraft stays farther from the engagement (Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency 2026). That is autonomy in the sense of independent flight and weapons carriage, not autonomy in the sense of a machine deciding on its own whom to kill: the engagement decision still runs through the launching platform. Where later variants of this kind of system draw that line, and whether “keep the pilot farther from danger” quietly becomes “remove the pilot from the decision,” is the design question this technology eventually forces on whichever country builds it.

No binding international agreement governs any of this. States have negotiated inside the United Nations’ Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons framework for more than a decade without producing one, converging on general language — meaningful human control matters, existing international humanitarian law applies — without settling what “meaningful” requires at machine speed (Chairperson of the Group of Governmental Experts on Emerging Technologies in the Area of Lethal Autonomous Weapons Systems 2026).

What exists instead is a set of practices individual militaries adopt on their own. The United States’ Department of Defense Directive 3000.09, most recently updated in 2023, is the clearest published example: it requires that autonomous and semi-autonomous weapon systems be designed to allow “appropriate levels of human judgment over the use of force,” and distinguishes a fully autonomous system that selects and engages targets without further human intervention, “human out of the loop,” from a human-supervised system and a semi-autonomous one that only engages targets a person has already selected (U.S. Department of Defense 2023). What “appropriate” requires is left to the officers approving each system, which is either a sensible acknowledgment that context varies or a loophole big enough to drive a fully autonomous system through, depending on who is answering. None of this binds a state that declines to adopt a comparable policy, the same enforcement gap section 6.4.2’s account of technical safeguards runs into: a safeguard only the building state can verify is a policy, not a check.

The dynamic that worries most people who study this is not a single rogue system but an arms race in speed. If one state fields autonomous targeting because it computes faster than a human-supervised alternative, its competitors face pressure to match the speed rather than the oversight, and the state that keeps meaningful human control loses whatever tactical edge the state that dropped it gains. Slowing that dynamic is beyond any single government’s domestic policy.

Civil society has pushed for an outright ban instead: a UN General Assembly resolution on autonomous weapons has passed by a lopsided majority in successive years without producing a treaty (United Nations General Assembly 2023). What most states will vote for and what the states that build these systems will sign are different documents.

10.4 International Governance and Diverging National Rules

Almost none of the international governance of AI is binding. What has emerged instead is mostly a set of voluntary, overlapping, specialized bodies, closer to how international environmental and trade norms developed piecemeal than to a single founding charter — with one recent exception.

The Council of Europe’s Framework Convention on Artificial Intelligence and Human Rights, Democracy and the Rule of Law is the first legally binding international treaty specifically on AI. Negotiated by the Council of Europe’s member states alongside outside participants that included the European Union and the United States, it requires five ratifications, at least three of them Council of Europe members, before it enters into force (Council of Europe 2024), and at this writing it has not, although the European Union deposited its ratification in May 2026 (Council of Europe 2026); signature, which forty-odd states and the EU have given, is not consent to be bound. What it would bind a party to is written at the level of a human-rights treaty and not a technical code: consistency with human rights, democracy, and the rule of law across an AI system’s lifecycle, with the operational detail of how a party meets that obligation left to domestic law. The convention is a foundation binding AI law can be built on, and not a substitute for the domestic statutes discussed below.

Everything else in this space is voluntary, and the roll is longer than it is deep. Every entry reads as an achievement and the list reads as momentum. The Bletchley Declaration is worth naming only because of what happened to it. It committed 29 signatories to cooperate specifically on frontier-model risk, and the national AI Safety Institutes it produced later joined an International Network whose members agreed to share testing methodology and run joint evaluations of the same frontier models (AI Safety Summit 2023 Participating Countries 2023; U.S. Department of Commerce and U.S. Department of State 2024).

A voluntary consensus frays quickly. The summit series that produced the Bletchley Declaration continued past it. A follow-up summit in Seoul in 2024 kept the same safety framing; a third, in Paris in February 2025, rebranded itself the AI Action Summit and shifted its emphasis from risk toward adoption and opportunity, and its closing statement was not signed by the United States or the United Kingdom, two of the series’ own founding participants (AI Action Summit Paris 2025). A coordination mechanism that depends on every major AI developer’s home government agreeing to the same framing is only as durable as that agreement, and within two years of Bletchley, the agreement had already come apart.

None of these bodies can compel a state or a company that ignores it. Each is a standard, a set of principles, or a forum, useful for building the shared vocabulary that eventually makes binding law possible, and that gap between voluntary standard and binding law is the default state of international coordination on anything individual states have reasons to handle differently. The EU AI Act, discussed below, is the first instance of this kind of groundwork becoming enforceable law, and it took most of a decade of standard-setting exactly like this to get there. The proliferation carries its own cost in the meantime: a company or a smaller government trying to comply with “international AI governance” faces a shifting handful of overlapping standards, each with different membership and scope and no formal mechanism for reconciling a conflict between them, which is why “the international community agrees” is, at the level of any specific rule, usually false.

Democracy, Authoritarianism, and Diverging National Rules

What individual states have done with the room left by the policy levers available against authoritarian AI misuse is a different question, since geopolitics is the study of how different governments use the same available tools differently.

The European Union chose comprehensive statute. The AI Act sorts systems by risk tier — a small set of practices banned outright under Article 5, a larger high-risk tier subject to conformity assessment and ongoing monitoring, most other uses left alone — so that what a system might do, and not the technology it runs on, decides how much scrutiny it gets (European Union 2024b); enforcement runs through an AI Office that can demand technical documentation from a general-purpose model’s developer, run its own evaluations, and fine a noncompliant provider a percentage of global turnover, reaching any company that offers a model inside the EU regardless of where it is based (European Union 2024a). The United States made the opposite structural bet, distributing enforcement across existing sectoral regulators applying laws written before AI existed, with NIST’s voluntary AI Risk Management Framework as a common technical reference (National Institute of Standards and Technology 2023); the wager is that existing consumer-protection, employment and safety law already reaches most AI harms, and that naming AI specifically risks regulating the label rather than the conduct. The United Kingdom made a third choice closer to the American one, directing existing sector regulators to apply five shared principles — safety, transparency, fairness, accountability and contestability — within their existing remits rather than building a new institution (UK Department for Science, Innovation and Technology 2023). The argument among the three is about how centralized regulation should be and it runs both ways: a comprehensive statute can freeze a definition of high-risk that the next generation of systems slides around rather than falls into, and a sectoral approach leaves gaps precisely where no existing regulator has jurisdiction. Neither failure mode is hypothetical, and none of the three has existed long enough to show which one it falls into more often.

China took a fourth path, one that does not fit neatly next to the other three because it starts from a different premise about what regulation is for. Its Cyberspace Administration, working with several other agencies, issued rules governing generative AI services in 2023 that require providers to file their algorithms with the state before public release, disclose the source and scale of their training data on request, and ensure that generated content reflects the government’s designated social values (Cyberspace Administration of China and six other agencies 2023). The EU, US, and UK approaches all regulate AI as a private product that might harm a consumer, a worker, or a subject of an automated decision, and argue with each other only about how centralized that regulation should be. China’s rules regulate AI as a communications channel the state has a standing interest in controlling content on, which is a different kind of law aimed at a different kind of risk, and comparing it to the other three on the same axis, more regulation or less, misses what actually distinguishes it.

The EU, US, and UK differ on how centralized AI regulation should be, but not on where its weight falls: on what a private company can build and sell. The EU’s statute does reach public authorities — it bans government social scoring and its limits on police use of live biometric identification, and its high-risk tier covers law enforcement, migration and the administration of justice — and it then excludes, by its own terms, systems used for military, defence or national-security purposes, which is where the applications section 6.4.1 documents live. What stays inside its scope is enforced against a member state’s agencies by that member state.

So none of the three stops a government from building those applications within its own borders, and the reason is not that the law ignores governments. It is that a law a government enforces against itself is a different instrument from one that constrains what it does with systems it controls directly. China’s rules show what a law aimed at the second target actually looks like — and that it can coexist with, rather than substitute for, exactly the kind of state control cataloged earlier. Conflating the two kinds of law, treating “AI is regulated here” as an answer to “is AI used against this country’s own population here,” is a recurring error.

10.5 Equity and the Digital Divide

AI development can narrow global inequality or widen it, and which one happens is not automatic. Diagnostic tools that work as well in an under-resourced clinic as in a well-funded hospital, agricultural monitoring that helps a smallholder farmer the way it helps an industrial one, and translation tools that make information equally available regardless of the language a person reads in are all available in principle, and none of them arrives by default. A model trained overwhelmingly on data from wealthy, English-speaking, urban contexts underperforms everywhere else, and a government or company with no reason to fix that gap usually does not.

The scale of the connectivity gap, 2025

The International Telecommunication Union’s 2025 assessment put global internet use at roughly six billion people, about three-quarters of the world’s population, with 2.2 billion people offline, concentrated overwhelmingly in low- and middle-income countries. The gap was starker in quality than in bare access: 94 percent of people in high-income countries used the internet against 23 percent in low-income ones, and 5G coverage reached 84 percent of high-income populations against 4 percent of low-income ones (International Telecommunication Union 2025).

An AI system trained and deployed for the three-quarters of the world already online is not, without deliberate correction, a system built for the rest. The constraints are structural. Reliable data, computing infrastructure, and the specialized personnel needed to build and maintain AI systems are unevenly distributed, and a country short on all three cannot simply adopt a system built elsewhere and expect it to transfer cleanly. Weak regulatory capacity compounds the problem: a country able to build strong AI safeguards is generally also a country able to enforce them, and the states least equipped to build AI are correspondingly the states least equipped to govern whatever gets deployed within their borders, regardless of who built it. The export controls discussed above cut both ways here: a control written to stop a surveillance system from reaching an authoritarian buyer can just as easily gatekeep the same underlying technology away from a legitimate one, and there is no way to write the rule so it only ever blocks the harmful case.

The multilateral response so far is a commitment and not a mechanism. The Global Digital Compact, adopted by all 193 UN member states at the 2024 Summit of the Future, is the broadest such commitment to closing the digital divide alongside AI governance specifically, and it created two follow-on bodies to carry it: an independent scientific panel and a standing intergovernmental dialogue (United Nations 2024). What it did not create is an obligation, which puts it in the same category as the voluntary instruments of section 10.4 and subject to the same discount.

The remedies proposed at this level have been the same handful for years, every one of them is correct, none is contested, and they have been named in those words in successive declarations across the whole period the box above measures. The box is what happened anyway: a government or company treating AI primarily as strategic advantage has no reason to fund any of them, and a declaration is not a reason.

What the geopolitics leaves to design

A system that resists authoritarian capture still assumes a government somewhere is deciding whether to let it be built, sold, or deployed as designed. Those governments are unevenly equipped, and the distribution compounds: the states with the weakest capacity to build AI are also the weakest at governing whatever arrives at their border. None of that is a reason to abandon the design-level arguments — a system built to resist misuse resists it whichever government asks — but it is a reason not to expect law, international or domestic, to do work only a design can do.

10.6 The Limits of the Democratic Dividend

Section 10.2 credits a state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press and usable courts with a democratic dividend: the same technology, deployed with checks on it. Having those institutions is necessary and it is not sufficient, and the ways it fails to be sufficient are separable.

First: reach

A democracy’s AI can be fully accountable to its electorate and entirely unaccountable to the people it is pointed at. Every mechanism in the dividend runs through the franchise — you vote out the government that authorized the system, you read the press that covers it, you sue in courts your citizenship gives you standing in. An occupied population, a foreign population, a population on the other side of a border has access to none of that. The check reaches exactly as far as the demos and stops. A system built by a democracy and aimed outward is, from the perspective of the people it selects, indistinguishable in its accountability from one built by a state with no elections at all.

Second: findings without consequences

The mechanisms can work as mechanisms and fail as checks. In the Israeli case the apparatus performed: journalists published the targeting systems in detail, serving personnel spoke to them, and the courts were simultaneously trying a sitting prime minister. Every institution did what the dividend says institutions do. No finding attached to a body with standing to stop what it described.

The American case shows the same decoupling in a different register. A civilian-harm investigation into a strike that killed at least a hundred and sixty-five people, most of them schoolchildren, was completed and not released; the lever available to the Senate committee that wanted it was a provision in the annual defense authorization withholding three-quarters of the Defense Secretary’s travel budget until the unredacted investigation was handed over (Stassis 2026). That is a co-equal branch of government reduced to expense-account leverage over a war-crimes investigation. The oversight mechanism existed, was used, and produced a travel-budget dispute. This is the same decoupling section 9.3.4 diagnoses for research ethics — a review body that reviews and cannot compel — at the top of the state.

Third: the countermajoritarian half

The deepest problem is that the dividend is entirely majoritarian. Every mechanism in it translates public will into outcome: elections aggregate preference, a free press informs the aggregation, and the implied failure mode is poor transmission — the public wanted something better and the institutions failed to deliver it.

But if the consensus is itself the problem, better transmission makes things worse. A well-instrumented democracy pointed at an unpopular minority is a democracy that finds out efficiently what the majority wants done and does it. Nothing in an aggregation mechanism protects anyone from the aggregate.

What is missing is the machinery that constrains preference instead of aggregating it: rights that hold regardless of being popular, courts that rule against a government which won the last election fairly, constitutional limits not repealable by whoever holds a majority this year, and standing for people outside the demos entirely — which is the whole function of international humanitarian law, a body of rules whose entire purpose is to bind a state toward people who will never vote in its elections. That is not an argument against democracy. It is the half of democracy that the dividend, stated as a property of having elections, leaves out.

Sen’s argument is the bridge. What makes democracy valuable in his account is public reasoning and the capacity to demand accountability, and neither is the ballot. Both survive the majority being wrong; the ballot does not.

This is what makes a system antifascist rather than merely responsive. It is not that the system tracks what people want. It is that there are things it will not do to a person no matter who wants it. Chapter 3 sets out what such a floor requires, and section 3.1 what each way of building one costs. A system with no such floor is not protected by being deployed in a democracy — it is a system that will faithfully execute whatever the demos decides, which is exactly the capability an authoritarian movement that wins an election requires.

Chapter 11
Necessary Areas of Research

Chapters 2 through 10 ran into problems they could not close, and set each one aside with a note. This chapter is those notes, collected and put in order. It does not survey what the field has left to do: every entry is a gap this book’s own argument opens, and they are ranked by what that argument fails without.

One gap this book records has no section here. Telling disagreement that deserves representation from disagreement produced by coercion or a captured information environment belongs to section 7.2, where the argument needing it is made.

The criterion produces an order almost nobody would choose on other grounds. The problems this book most needs solved are not the ones nearest to being solved, and the first two entries are the least tractable things here. A funder ranking by progress per dollar, or a lab ranking by what its people could start on Monday, would invert most of this order. It is an account of what the book is running on credit.

Each section closes by naming four things: the experiment that could begin now with means that already exist, the result that would falsify the approach, the data or access it would take, and, where it has one, the institutional condition without which it cannot run. Where there is no near-term experiment the section says so rather than inventing one, and three say so — the third of section 11.2’s questions, the aggregation half of section 11.5, and section 11.9 entirely. The first of those is the second-ranked problem in the chapter.

One qualification governs every gap named here. Where I say a problem is unaddressed, the claim is bounded: I looked for published work under a particular description and did not find it, which is weaker than no such work existing. A search is not a survey, a program can be underway under a name I did not think to search, and the closer a gap sits to this book’s own framing the less likely anyone has posed it in these words. What it does support is narrower and still useful: a researcher entering here will not find the problem already named and waiting. Each section names the nearest work I could find and says where it stops, because the distance between an existing program and the missing thing is what a researcher can use.

One finding comes out of the sections below rather than out of any one of them, and taking each gap separately is what hid it. Five of the nine close by naming an institutional condition, and the five are one condition wearing different clothes. Weights a vendor cannot withdraw when a result embarrasses it. A guardian who is not also the operator. Longitudinal access by somebody an institution cannot fire, reporting to a body with standing to receive the finding. Evaluation results published whether or not they flatter the method. Training checkpoints released rather than kept. Every one is a party outside the operator, holding access, able to act on what it finds. Two of the remaining four close on a data requirement rather than on a party, and section 11.9 is that condition itself. Section 11.6 is the exception, closing with no institutional prerequisite at all, which is what makes it the one entry here that could start today.

What keeps a party outside is the question none of the five asks, and in the United States it was answered while this book was being finished. Trump v. Slaughter removed, for most of the federal government, the protection from dismissal that made an outside party outside, and Trump v. Cook kept it for one body on grounds no new body can have; section 3.1 carries both. Independence decomposes into components — a director’s tenure, a funding stream, a mandate — and an adversary takes the weakest: the consumer-finance bureau whose funding stream the Court had upheld in 2024 had that stream shut from inside in late 2025, by an acting director, removable at will since 2020, who declined to request the money under a reading of the statute that two courts then rejected (Consumer Financial Protection Bureau 2025; Seila Law LLC v. Consumer Financial Protection Bureau 2020; Consumer Financial Protection Bureau v. Community Financial Services Association of America, Ltd. 2024). A condition that names the outside party and not what keeps it there has named half of what it needs. So the binding constraint on the research program below is not a research problem. Most of this work is fundable today and cannot be run.

11.1 Interpretability, Self-Reports, and Resistance to Tampering

“AI self-awareness” names at least two different questions, and collapsing them is where the term goes wrong. Whether a system has anything like subjective experience is not a question current methods can answer for a human, let alone a machine.
